one? What is she like?"
Melanie arose and faced him; and when he looked at
her, scowling, annoyed, she moved swiftly to press him
from her knees to her breasts and to clasp his face and
kiss him. She clung with the kiss, her tongue seeking his
tongue, while he tried to thrust her away. Then, suddenly,
he remembered the texture of her skin and he turned
weak and surrendered, putting his arms round her and
his lips to her neck. Her skin was so soft and fair and so38
delightfully scented with its own odor that he liked to
play over it with his tongue, feeling its smooth softness.
"Your Jewess," she murmured against him, “doesn't
have a skin like mine!"
"Only Melanie has such a skin. Only Melanie has
body odors that need no perfume."
"Darling, kiss me!"
He kissed her, and then she drew him to the couch and
he knelt with his arms clasping her, his nose moving back
and forth to part her garment. With one hand she was
caressing his hair, which was of the color of dark olive
leaves. Waiting until he was wholly absorbed by his
seeking she asked him murmuringly:
"Is this what Sappho meant by vagrant love? What
man anywhere has ever known any other kind? Men
don't respect wives or concubines-they respect only
the heterae. Is it true?"
"If you think so," he said, speaking against her bosom.
"Do you think you'll ever really love?"
"Maybe."
"A Jewess?"
"Perhaps."
4
IT WAS TRUE, as Reuben said, that pious Jews like Hosah
lived their lives by the Torah. The morning Philemon
awoke in the arms of Melanie, Hosah came abruptly
out of sleep, thinking he had heard the bugles of an alien
army. Then he remembered that he had dreamed of
running water and at once he uttered these words from
the holy writings, "I will extend peace to her like a river,"
lest the words, "Distress shall come in like a flood,"
should overwhelm him. He believed that words were
endowed with tremendous power for good or evil, that39
wicked words could be neutralized with holy words, if
these were spoken in time. Three times he murmured the
verse from scripture and then relaxed and examined his
depths.
Becoming conscious, after a moment, that he had
opened his eyes, he whispered: "Blessed are you, our
Holy One, King of the world, who have made the blind
to see." When he thought he heard the crowing of a cock,
he murmured: "Blessed are you, our Holy One, King of
the world, who have given understanding to the cock to
distinguish between night and day." For practically every-
thing that Hosah saw, did, or thought there were words
in the holy books for thanksgiving or protection. When,
a little later, he put on his first garment, he whispered:
"Blessed are you, our Holy One, King of the world,
who have given clothes to the naked." When from a
bowed position he straightened, he said: "Blessed are
you, our Holy One, King of the world, who raise up
them that are bowed down." When he stepped forth,
he blessed God for directing his steps; when he fastened
his sandals, for providing for his needs; when he bound
his girdle, for girding Israel with holiness; when he tied
the kerchief round his head, for crowning Israel with
beauty. So it was with Hosah from the moment he waken-
ed until he slept again.
For an enlightened Jew like Reuben, the piety of such
men as Hosah was incredible imbecility. But men like
Reuben failed to understand his patience, his devotion,
his reservoirs of spiritual strength, his joy-joy so deep
that it lay almost beneath his senses; his delight in subtle
distinctions; his practiced intuitions; and, above all else
his peace of mind that came from faithful observance
of all the commandments and laws. He was proud of
his knowledge. Not even in Alexandria was there an
abler exegete.
Reuben, Hosah might have said, was a fine one to be
contemptuous of his betters-this cynical son of wealth
with his fleshpots. In Israel there were only two classes
-the rich with their cynicism and fine raiment and love40
of Greek ways, the poor crushed by taxes-by poll and
crown and temple taxes, export, import, ectroi, bridge,
harbor, market and salt taxes, not to mention the assess-
ments laid upon them by the Syrian tyrants. Hosah barely
managed to clothe himself and family in the coarsest
garments, to feed them with the coarsest food. But Amiel
ben Micah said that a powerful king, a son of David,
would soon come.
Having put on his garments and blessed the Name,
Hosah went to the door to look at the early morning
light; and there he touched the mezuza and kissed his
finger. This was a parchment fixed to the door, containing
holy verses. Then he turned to prepare for his morning
devotions. He would put on his prayer scarf, lay the tefil-
lin, say the shema. Some said the morning shema was to
be uttered as soon as a person could tell the difference
between blue and white; others said between blue and
leek-green. Some said the evening shema was to be spok-
en when the priests returned to eat their heave, others
said at midnight, still others, at dawn. So many matters
were disputed! So many remained to be settled by learned
men like Hosah. If a person was reading the Torah and
happened to read the shema verses need he say them
again that evening? Some said no, some said yes. When
reciting the shema was he allowed to return a greeting?
Was it all right to recite it inaudibly? Hosah knew only
that reciting the shema cooled the fires of the underworld,
that no harm could befall one while uttering it.
Having washed his hands he put on the prayer-scarf
and prayer straps, commanded for morning prayer ex-
cept on the Sabbath and holy days; for so it was written.
Wealthy men had a scarf of silk ribbon or a strip of gold
or silver passementerie. Hosah's was of cheap wool. He
laid the tefillin, also of coarse wool, one for the arm and
one for the head. These housed a piece of parchment con-
taining holy words. He attached the one to his left arm
just above the elbow, and while doing so he murmured:
"Praised be you, our Holy One, King of the world, who
sanctified us with your commandments and commanded41
us to lay the tefillin." He wound the strap seven times
around his arm. He put the charm on his forehead and
recited the verse.
...
The small two-room hut in which he lived had a flat
roof, to which a rickety ladder led. He now climbed this
ladder and on the roof, facing toward the sun, prepared
to recite the shema. But first he offered a benediction
which began: "With abounding love you have blessed us,
our Holy One; with great and exceeding pity you have
pitied us! O our Father, have mercy upon us, and put
it in our hearts to discern, to understand, to learn, to
teach, to keep, to do.. Then, with hands uplifted,
he recited the shema, one of the greatest of all the cries
from the heart of Israel: "Hear, O Israel, the Lord our
God, the Lord is One! And you shall love the Lord your
God with all your heart, and with all your soul. . . ." And
the triumphant conclusion: "I am the Lord your God,
who brought you out of the land of Egypt, to be your
God: I am the Lord your God!"
99
Feeling purified and enriched he went down from the
roof and took off the scarf and the charms and reverently
laid them away in their chest. He kissed his finger. Like
most people he took no breakfast or only a very light
one; this morning he chewed a handful of roasted barley
and thought of his devoted wife and parents; and then,
with a pang, of Hepzibeth, the wild harum-scarum who
called herself Angela; and with an even sharper pang of
his beautiful sister Judith. Jesus ben Sirach had written,
"From a woman did sin originate, and because of her
we all must die." Whenever Hosah thought of Judith
he felt that he came close to sin.
He refused to admit that she was surpassingly lovely
-or Hepzibeth either, for that matter. When he looked
at either one, which was seldom, and then only to read
their eyes or to note whether they were using abominable
lures on their lips, he murmured: "If your eye cause you
to sin, pluck it out; if your hand, cut it off." It was better
that a member of the body perish than that the soul
should go down into perdition. For Hosah and the Pious,42
the thought of sinning was itself a sin; whoso looked
on a woman with desire debauched her. The mind or eye
could commit fornication. Indeed, there could be adultery
with the feet; for it was written, "He that hastens with
his feet sins." "Gaze not upon lovely maids," Jesus had
said, "and gaze not on a woman dressed up to lure you."
To keep from sinning was for Hosah and his kind
a ceaseless task. In spite of his will, when he thought
of Judith he saw her ripe mouth, and gagged on the barley
and for a moment looked straight ahead. A pious Jew
would never walk behind a woman, not even his wife,
lest he be tempted; and never would he engage in pro-
longed talk with one. He drove Judith from his thoughts
and resumed his chewing. Hosah knew of a wife so vir-
tuous that it was said of her that the rafters of her
house had never seen her hair uncovered, nor the hem
of her garment. Well, that, he thought, could be said
of his own wife. . . .
His thoughts returned from Judith to holy matters.
Hosah was a scribe, a teacher, and very learned in the
religious lore of his people; and to the interpretation
of the holy writings he had dedicated his life. He ad-
mired, and in many ways emulated, Jesus ben Sirach,
who from fear of losing his holiness never laughed, or
played with his children. Hosah had never been known
to laugh and not even his sisters had ever seen him smile.
After eating the barley he again washed his hands.
He washed his hands many times daily to be sure that
he kept himself ritually clean. Sometimes he drank milk.
A pure Jew could drink only milk that had been taken
by a pure Jew, or by a heathen when a Jew was watching
him. Hosah loved milk but he was always suspicious of it.
Before beginning the day's labors he offered a prayer:
"Search me out, O Holy One, and know my heart! Try
me, and know my thoughts! See if there be any covetous-
ness in me, and lead me in the ancient road!" In the tiny
school adjacent to the synagogue, Hosah had taught:
"It is best to meditate on the Torah from the moment
you wake until you fall asleep, and then to dream of it;43
but, failing that, it is better to meditate on it nine-tenths
of your time; but, if this you do not do, then fail not to
devote half your time to it. All wisdom is in it; there is
no other."
He went to the doorway to look again at the light;
and while standing there, looking out and up-for he
lived on a hillside-he saw two priests hurrying toward
the palestra grounds. The sight of them made him feel
ill. He hated them because the Holy One hated them.
They were doers of evil, men of blood, speakers of lies,
braggarts and robbers, enemies of the Name. If Hosah
did not think of them in terms still more opprobrious it
was only because his capacity to hate was less than his
need to love. He could not bring himself to think of them
as dogs and pigs.
A few minutes later Amiel presented himself at the
door. He touched the mezuza and kissed his finger and
murmured holy words; and then, with a bow of reverence,
he entered. This man was, like Hosah, short, dark and
rather stocky. He had a nature so withdrawn that to the
casual observer he seemed to be drugged. All of Amiel's
physical yearnings were so deeply insulated with his scru-
ples that no heat came through to the surface. He had
taken no wife. He had never known a woman. He de-
voted himself completely to the Ancient of Days. A
visionary, he saw fearful and wonderful things—for
this was the man who would write the book that would
be known as the Book of Daniel.
He had seen faces in lightning and eyes burning like
lamps and feet glowing like the metal in braziers. The
visions in his head did indeed trouble him. He had such
intensity of thought and emotion that he sometimes gave
the appearance of being cataleptic; and this was so, in
part at least, because he had entirely repressed all his
sexual impulses. Though only in his late twenties, a beau-
tiful woman, such as Judith, presented to him the aspect
of a stone slab or a blank wall. It was his custom to keep
his gaze down, his brows drawn into a scowl of medita-
tion, his lips closed, his breath well-controlled.44
He had had a vision recently and had come to tell
Hosah about it. In a voice as toneless as his face was
empty of passion he said:
"I put on the spirit of the Holy One."
"Blessed be the Name!"
"It clothed me like a splendid garment."
"The Lord our God, the Lord is One!"
"The light of the wicked shall be quenched."
"Say on," said Hosah, staring at Amiel's eyes.
"There came a voice from above and I saw a figure
clothed in brilliant light."
Hosah was trembling. "Blessed be Israel! What did
the King of the worlds say?"
"The Holy Name said the saints of the Most High
will surely have power and dominion over all the peoples
of the earth. All languages, nations and peoples will bow
before him; for his dominion is from everlasting to ever-
lasting and will not pass away or be destroyed. All domin-
ions and kingdoms and principalities will obey and serve
him-blessed be he and his chosen will be the priests
to all nations."
Like a voice speaking out of a mummy that served no
purpose but to house it-like a figure of wood-like a
face from which all blood and emotion had been drawn,
Amiel uttered his words. The two men, the seed of fore-
fathers who had been borne into exile, enslaved, exterm-
inated-who had lost their kingdom, whose holy place
itself had been razed to the earth-looked at one another
and thought of the glorious age to come, when the rem-
nant of the chosen, who had in their holy books all
science, all philosophy, all knowledge, would be exalted
above all peoples.
In an awed voice Hosah asked: "Did the Holy One
-blessed be he tell you how soon this glorious day will
come ?"
"It will come soon."
"Glory to the High One whose Name is Holy!”
Amiel went outside, pausing to touch the mezuza; and
Hosah sank to a stool, with the vision warming his be-45
ing like a furnace. He wanted to utter a few words appro-
priate to the occasion but hesitated, knowing that words
accomplished what they promised; the magic in the Word
had to be used with the utmost caution. Once a word was
spoken it could not be recalled, but would go forth, for
good or evil, to fulfill its mission. When blind old Isaac,
deceived by Jacob and Rebecca, gave to Jacob the bles-
sing he intended for Esau, he could not annul or recall
it. He could only give a second and feebler blessing to
his firstborn. When Micah cursed the unknown thief and
learned that the thief was her own son, she could not
annul the curse. Hosah was searching his mind for the
right words when with a start he recalled the time that
the Creator rebuked Moses for prolix prayer. On the
brink of the Red Sea the God of Hosts had cried:
"Moses, my children are in straits; the sea shuts off the
way and the enemy pursues, and you stand there and make
a long prayer! Why cry unto me? There is a time to pro-
long prayer, and a time to make it short!"
This, Hosah felt, was a time to make it short. He fell
down and lay on his face, with his arms outstretched to
the sanctuary; crying: "Hear, O Israel, our God is One!"
The One he drew out on a long breath with nasal over-
tones, for it was thought of as filling the sky and earth
and all the regions roundabout. By filling his breath with
it the pious Jew felt that his spiritual being was expanded
coextensively with the Father's. Then he prayed: "Sound
the great horn for our freedom! Lift up the standard
for the assembling of our exiles! Blessed are you, O
Holy One, King of the world'
"Hey there!"
""
He was lying on his face, a small man with narrow
sloping shoulders and abnormally long arms, praying,
when those irreverent words smote his ear. Looking up,
he saw the wicked smiling face of his sister Angela, and
in that moment he observed that her eyelids and cheeks
were painted, her lips dyed.
"Praying again!" she said. "Always flat on your face
before your superstitions!"46
"Go away.
"Get up, you absurd fellow."
Hosah arose with dignity. "Don't touch the door,"
he said.
"Would your unclean sister touch anything that lives
with her holy brother?"
Hosah shrugged gently. Having extinguished the light
of the world women still lived intimately with the powers

"I came to tell you about Judith." He was waiting and

"Judith-"
"She says Enoch is very dull."
"Go away," said Hosah, knowing that the imp was

"She says to tell you—'

"Did you hear me?" cried Hosah furiously.
Hepzibeth, who called herself Angela, turned away,

Of course, Hosah reflected, Judith would be betrothed
to Enoch as soon as his parents could raise the marriage-
price. As for Angela! Hosah shuddered and went to the
door to look out. He touched the parchment, kissed his
finger and returned to his devotions.
In the other room of this small hut Hosah's wife was
bent over her work. She had to scour, card and spin the
goathair, steep, dry and beat the fibers of flax, make
barley flour with a stone mortar, prepare their simple
meals and observe, like all other pious women, the
rules that applied to her. And silently, unobtrusively
she assisted her husband in his labors. There were hun-
dreds of rules which pious men had to obey, and Hosah,
overlooked some of the commandments. When he arose
he must not walk more than four cubits before washing
his hands, nor touch his mouth, nostrils, eyes or ears;47
he must pour water alternately on each hand three times,
to the wrist, and then wash his face and mouth; and
while drying his hands he must utter a benediction. In
dressing and undressing he had to observe many divine
rules, removing the left shoe first, tying the lace first in
the left shoe, putting on his garments before walking
four cubits, covering his head before uttering thanks.
.. After eating he must neither walk nor ride nor excite
his body or in any way weary himself. He must sleep
neither on his face nor back, but the first part of the
night on his left side, the second part on his right
side. His wife had to watch him because by preference
he slept on his back. Such food as grapes, figs and melon
he must eat before the meal, not mixing them with heavier
dishes; but Hosah might absentmindedly reach for a
grape while chewing his bread. When washing his hands
following the thanksgiving after a meal he must cleanse
only the first two joints of the fingers, whereupon, said
the holy writings, he should hold his hands down while
drying them. Hosah sometimes forgot this commandment
and lifted his hands. Knives and other utensils used for
cheese and meat were marked, so that they would not
be confused and exchanged. If cheese were eaten it was
permissible to take meat at the next meal, provided the
hands had been carefully examined to cleanse them of
cheese morsels. . . . In all these matters of food, utensils,
prayers, ablutions, Hosah had to be watched. There was
nothing that Israel's God had overlooked when he gave
the commandments to his chosen people-not only for
food and dress, birth, marriage, mourning, burial and
death, labor and rest and sleep, holy days and prayer
and thanksgiving; he had also given rules on giving and
lending, debts, slaves, adornments, grief and bathing and
suicide, losing and finding—and even on love and wooing
and copulation. There were so many rules that a devoted
man like Hosah, watched by a faithful wife, erred now
and then. . . .
When noon came he was bent over the Torah, devoted
to a mitzvah. A mitzvah was a duty, an opportunity to48
serve God. The Pious delighted in the abundance of the
mitzvoth and looked on them as evidence of the Holy
One's regard for his people-simhah shel mitzvah—the
joy in the commandment! The stupid Samaritans said
that the Torah was written by the finger of God and
then given to Moses. Hosah knew on the contrary that
God had dictated the words and Moses had written
them down, making the errors that any human scribe
might make. To say that the Torah was written by God
himself left no way to explain the gross errors and con-
tradictions. ...
"My lord!" a voice whispered.
The words were whispered several times before at
last Hosah raised his head and looked over at his wife.
She said that the time was noon, and at once he arose
and prepared to utter his midday prayer. After praying
he went outside, pausing to touch the mezuza, and looked
up the hill; and while thinking of the wickedness in the
city he murmured:
"In that day the Holy One will take away the bravery
of their anklets, and the filets, and the crescents; the
pendants and the bracelets and the veils; the headtires
and the armlets; the rings and the nose-jewels; the
aprons and the mantelets and the cloaks and the girdles;
and the gauze robes and the fine linen and the turbans
and the mantles."
While looking up the hill there came toward him two
old men with gray beards and keen bright eyes. They
were Jose ben Joezer of Zeredah and Jose ben Johanan
of Jerusalem-two sages, two men deeply learned in the
Torah, who assisted Hosah in his interpretations and
revisions. Ben Joezer had recently pronounced glass un-
clean, because the sand in it came from heathen soil. Ben
Johanan had said: "Do not converse with women." Both
old men were very poor, having little but the thread-
bare garments on their backs.
They came down the hill and Hosah greeted them as
soon as they were within hearing. The old men came up49
and in turn clasped Hosah's face and kissed his fore-
head. He was their favorite of all the scribes.
Ben Joezer lifted his right hand toward the temple
and said: "The Holy One is enthroned upon the praises
of Israel."
"His praise," said the other Jose, "is in the mouth
of his saints."
"Ah, Israel today is filled with blind sheep."
"Let them be. Leave them to Elijah's decision."
Ben Joezer in a low voice asked Hosah: "Have you
seen the High Priest?"
"I don't want to," said Hosah.
"He's a hypocrite and an ignoramus," said Ben Jo-
hanan.
Ben Joezer, the gentler of the two old men, touched
his brother softly and said: "We must not judge our
neighbor until we have been in his place. Remember
that a fool crosses on a rainbow from earth to heaven,
but the wise man builds a fence around the Torah."
Ben Joezer was beaming on Hosah and admonishing

was not smiling.
"Son of the blessing," he said to Hosah, "have you
seen Amiel lately?"
"Only this morning," said Hosah. He looked round

spoken to him."
"Blessed be the Name! What did he say?"
"The wicked will be destroyed and Israel will be en-

The two Joses looked at one another. Then Ben

"Will this be soon?"
"The Holy One, blessed be he, says it will be soon."
Slowly and quietly the two old men looked round theni

"Let the Holy One be praised!"50
Ben Johanan burst forth, "The abominable wicked-

One blesses his children."

Hosah, bowing reverently before them, said: "O pious.
O gentle, O worthy sons of Ezra! May our Father who
is in heaven bless and keep you!"
"May his name be blessed!"
Then the pious old men, most saintly of all the saints
of Israel, drew their robes close to protect them from
thorn and stone and went down the hill. Hosah looked
after them, his face luminous with wonder and love.
Turning back to his sanctuary-which was the name a
pious Jew gave to his house-he touched the mezuza,
kissed his finger and entered. Before him was a long
afternoon of labor.
He washed his hands, took the Torah from its chest,
and sat by the door where light fell; and there, until
his faithful wife spoke again, he would bend over the
holy writings, his patient and subtle mind separating
the true from the false, the errors of Moses from the
living waters.
5
ONE WATCHING HOSAH as he read would have sensed
that he was deeply perturbed. He had need to be. The
sages of Israel had been perturbed ever since Alexander
spread Hellenic civilization over this part of the world.51
Jews had returned from the Hellenic cities, saying: "They
tell us there is deep and abiding wisdom in the books of
the Greeks, but in our writings only the follies of the
ancients"; saying, "The philosophers and scientists in
Alexandria ask us, What kind of science is it that says
the Jew-god made light before the sun was made?-
that says the camel divides not the hoof?—that makes
the Nile and Euphrates branches of the same river? Do
you tell us that your god showed his hind parts to Abra-
ham? What nonsense is this?"
Devout Jews returned to their holy city, saying: "The
Holy One is infallible, yet there are errors in our holy
books. How came these to be?" The Great Assembly,
the Bet Din Ha-Gadol, or Great Court, had considered
these matters-and were considering them still. The wise
men said, "How can we explain these things?" and no-
body more than Hosah bent over the scriptures to find
the answers.
He was making many changes. In the Torah it said.
that Jacob wrestled with God, and this he had changed
to with the man: how absurd and blasphemous to imag-
ine that the Holy One came to earth and wrestled like
a common clown! Or was the meaning symbolic? In all
places where they appeared he was changing the anthro-
pomorphisms: in his text God's hand became power;
his feet became stability-had Moses been half-asleep
when he gave to God hands and feet and hair and all
the other gross and common human traits and features?
Between the Holy One and man there stood whole hier-
archies of dominions and powers and thrones-and yet
in the holy books there were abominable words about
his bowels and nostrils!
He was also taking out the word Yahweh, the name of
Israel's God, wherever it appeared and writing in a pa-
tronym. The secret Name must not be pronounced or
written: it made Hosah shake with dread merely to look
at it. He was also busy trying to reconcile the innumer-
able contradictions. In the Book of Job it was written,
"Answer not a fool according to his folly" and, "Answer52
a fool according to his folly." In the one text Hosah
deleted the word not. Ezekiel again and again contra-
dicted what Moses had written. There were countless
discrepancies in historical chronologies. But Hosah was
shocked above all by the crudeness.
Moses had written, "And it came to pass by the way
in the inn, that God met him and sought to kill him.'
What wickedness, Hosah asked, seething with shame,
had been in Moses when the simpleton imagined that the
Almighty, the Master of Life, the King of the Universe,
had had to seek to kill an ordinary man? Had he been
out of his mind when he wrote that Abraham hastened
to Sarah and told her to bake cakes, for God was coming
to eat with them?
When a problem baffled him he would pray: "I have
wearied myself, O Holy One, I have wearied myself, O
Holy One, I am consumed! Who has gathered the wind
in his hands? Who has bound the waters with his gar-
ments? . . ." Or he would stretch forth his hands and
cry: "Wisdom cries aloud in the streets and is an oracle
in the broad places; but who is wise who is born of
woman?"
It was toward evening when the doorway was dark-
ened. On looking up, Hosah saw before him, but not
touching his house, the one called Reuben, and another
man who was a stranger. Hosah thrust the holy writings
behind him and shrank back.
"Did we startle you?" asked Reuben, smiling. "Hosah,
this is my friend Philemon, a physician and a very learned
man.
""
Hosah stood up, the Torah behind him, his body rigid
with distaste and dismay, one long arm hanging as though
broken, his eyes staring at the men as he might have
looked at a serpent.
With a low laugh Reuben said: "Hosah doesn't dare
ask us into his house. We would defile it. It would take
him a long time to get it clean again. See the parchment
on his doorpost? If I were to touch it he would try to
kill me."53
"What do you want?" asked Hosah.

"We will when we're ready. Where is Judith ?" Reu-

"Of course," said Philemon.
Hosah had been looking at Reuben because, of the

to all men that he is wealthy, and can offer a very

might be willing to share with you. Besides that, he is,
as I have already said, enormously learned. All in all,
he is the kind of husband that an ignorant little chit like
Judith could hardly expect to come by."

"No."
"You see," said Reuben to Philemon, "what I meant?
He doesn't think you're a fit mate for his sister. You

Sabra spoke. "What did you mean," she asked, "when

"Just that. And I very probably shall be the very

with kawwanah, that is, with attention and intention,
for words without thoughts never go to the Place." He

“Judith, my sister, may the Holy One bless you and
guard you, may he make bright his face toward you

Rising, he again sat on his stool, took the Torah out
of its chest, and looked at a verse in Leviticus. It said:
"And the Israelitish woman's son blasphemed Yahweh
and cursed." Hosah blotted out Yahweh. Having done
that, he murmured the three maxims of the Great Synod:
"Be slow in judgment, set up many disciples, and build
a hedge around the Torah." A moment later a shadow

She was only thirteen but girls matured early in this
world, in both body and mind; and Judith was more
matured for her age than most of them. She was taller
than the average woman among her people, standing
only a little under five feet and a half; and she carried
herself with spirit and pride. She was more intelligent
than most of the women, and the lucid quality of her
mind was a light in her large eyes. And she had more
fire than anyone, including her parents and her brother
Hosah, had been able to direct and confine.
Hosah arose and laid aside the scroll and stepped for-
ward to peer at her more closely. Brother and sister
looked into the eyes of one another, and what Hosah
saw in Judith's eyes he did not like.
"Where have you been?" he asked.
"Taking the goat to water."
Hosah looked at the sandals on her feet, which were
only soles held by thin straps.
"Have you seen Saul today?"
"No."
"Have you seen Hepzibeth?”
"No."
"Has Enoch talked to you today?"

That was true, but Hosah suspected that his sister

Hosah had been looking at the floor and considering.

She hesitated, glanced up at the sky, drew a sharp

Zoma had tried to be a good father. He had had his
sons circumcised, he had redeemed them, he had taught
them righteousness and a trade, and he was determined
to buy for them the best wives he could afford. But he
was not a happy man. Of all the commandments the
weightiest of the weighty was filial piety; for it was
written, "Honor your father and your mother that your
days may be long upon the land which the Holy One
has given you." Enoch had sometimes been rebellious,
for which the punishment was death by stoning; for that
was written also.
The sun had set. Wrapped in tattered garments, Zoma
was shivering in his cold hut. His hair, falling down
from his kerchief, was inextricably tangled: a devout
man, Zoma would not use a comb, lest some of his hair,
which was precious because divine, be dislodged.
When Hosah appeared, Zoma bowed low, for he saw

"Zoma, son of Zabdiel, I've come to speak to you

"Ahh!" cried Zoma, and he made frantic movements60

"Ah no!" cried Zoma tragically.
"Only today he came to me-”
"No, no," said Zoma sadly. If Hosah had been ob-
serving him closely he might have seen in Zoma's eyes
a flash of light and sudden cunning—for if Judith were
taken by another, he would not have to raise the mohar
for Enoch. Still, Enoch would demand a wife, and wives
cost more than a poor peasant had.
"Give me a little time," he said. "Holy-holy-holy is61
the King of hosts, but I'm only a poor man with barely
enough to eat. If the blessed Goodness will allow it I
will find the mohar-but do not press upon me like a
nochri!"
"I can't give you much time. Remember that my sis-
ter is a woman whom many men covet. There will be
one coming-"
"Ah!" cried Zoma hopefully. "Is there one?"
"There are many.'
""
"She has the beauty of the lilies of the field. She
has-" Hosah turned impatiently away and Zoma ran
after him, crying: "I remember what you taught us, that
four things are inconsistent with reason, a poor man
who is proud, a rich man who hides his wealth, a gray-
beard who is adulterous, and a leader who vainly raises
himself. I'm a poor man, Hosah, but I'm not proud!"
Hosah turned, looking back. "You have very little
time, Zoma. This son of the no-gods will be after my
sister day and night."
"May the Holy One forbid!"
Hosah entered his house and recited the shema and
then ate a little supper. Sabra kindled a light for him,
that he might continue his studies; but after washing
his hands and taking up the Torah, Hosah sat in thought.
He was afraid that the wicked Greek would have his
way with Judith. Women were giddy, vain, capricious,
and instructed from birth by the powers of evil. Women
had extinquished the light. Women had brought death
into the world. Judith, though a devout girl, was head-
strong.
...
Before preparing for sleep, Hosah entered the other
chamber, where his son waited, and listened to his son's
bedtime prayer: "Blessed are you, O Holy One, King
of the world, who make the bands of sleep fall upon my
eyes, and slumber upon my eyelids. May it be your will,
O Eternal, and Father of our fathers, to suffer me to
lie in peace and to rise up again in peace. Hear, O Israel,
the Name is One. Blessed be the Name, whose kingdom
is from everlasting to everlasting, forever and ever."62
The child, a boy of six, uttered the prayer letter-
perfect; and his father was so proud that he knelt and
put an arm round him and kissed him. Then he arose
and prepared for his own sleep. He removed his left
sandal first. He did not place his garments under his
pillow, as was the custom with wicked men. After say-
ing his last benediction he would not speak again, but
would look back over the day to search it for trans-
gressions, and then would lie enfolded by holy thoughts.
Standing, with his feet drawn close together, his gaze
cast down, his hands crossed over his chest, as a servant
should stand before his master, he faced the sanctuary,
and his prayer went forth as incense before the Holy One:
"He who dwells in the shelter of the Most High
abides under the wings of the Presence. He is my refuge
and my fortress, my Father in whom I trust. May his
will be done here on earth as it is above. He will cover
me with his wings, and under his wings I will find peace.
I will not be afraid of the terrors by night, nor of the
arrows by day; and though a thousand may fall at my
side, no harm shall come upon me. For you, O Holy
One, blessed be your Name, are my tower. I will walk
upon the lion and the adder and fear them not, for
you have set your love upon me; and when you call, I
will answer, for you will be with me in trouble. Let
Israel place his hope in our Father henceforth and for-
ever."

THREE WEEKS LATER King Antiochus marched into Jer-
usalem and a part of the city turned out to give him a
torchlight welcome and parade, in the Greek manner.
Philemon saw him at close range. Antiochus was, he per-
ceived, quite young, slender, impatient, highstrung-as63
any man might well be who had lived as a youth in the
custody of the Romans. Antiochus was traveling with
a great deal of pomp, as was the way with all Syrian
kings.
Melanie had begged Reuben to present her to the
King, and when he hedged she turned to Philemon.
"Who am I to present anyone to the King?—an un-
known physician from Antioch !"
Reuben had asked Philemon to go with him to meet
Antiochus. "Why? To talk as a conspirator? I'm not
yet ready to conspire."
"By the gods and by the gods," cried Reuben, doubling
the oath to make it stronger, "I'll not be surprised to
find you with the enemy!"
"You speak with bitterness."
"Can a Greek hesitate between free men, dedicated
to freedom, and the slaves of theocratic despotism? Are
you with Hosah, who believes that wisdom is fear of
his god, or me and my kind, who hold with Zeno that
education is the basis of virtue and only ignorance is
evil? Does nature proceed by laws-"
"Yes, Reuben, yes! I agree with all that. What think-
ing man doesn't? But I've no taste for conspiracies."
"Oh, I know!" cried Reuben scornfully. "You want
to go back and sit in the Philadelphian libraries. You
want to go to the observatory and play with the globes
and armils and astrolabes. So do I. But there's a job
to be done or there'll be no Alexandrias. We'll have
a world of Jerusalems."
"But I see no point in fighting ignorance here."
"Are you a coward?"
"Oh, by the Virgin! Am I a coward because I'm not
convinced all the truth is on your side?"
"You mean there's truth in these quaint books by
Moses? But never mind. I know what it is. It's this wench
named Judith."
"Or Angela, maybe."
"Angela! She's the kind of woman who could stab a
man in the back while making love."64
On a bench under a tree on the western hill Philemon
had twice sat with Angela. This girl, who was three
years older than Judith, fascinated him-for she was
an adorable armful of dimples, mischief and flirtatious
gestures. She was not so lovely as Judith; her nose was
a little too broad at the base and her face was not so
symmetrical; but the sudden ravishing presence of her
absorbed and concealed all imperfections. Angela could
not look at a person or speak or make a movement with-
out smiling-and when she smiled, her face softened
into so many luscious dimples that Philemon thought
of her as the dimple-girl. She had many little intimate
ways that delighted a man-or in any case such a man
as Philemon, flooding his senses with the fumes and
warmths of love. Suddenly she would turn to him, her
face grave and womanly, her lips parted, and gently
touch his nose with her forefinger. Then, with her grav-
ity dissolving into smiles and dimples, she would pre-
tend to shrink and draw away, crying: "Oh! Now I'm
unclean because you're a nochri!" She spoke Philemon's
language with an accent that he found as delightful as
the dimples in her cheeks. And at once-because for
Angela the present moment always pressed eagerly on
the moment just gone-she would ask: "Do you love
me?"
They were sitting on the bench one afternoon when
she said: "What does your name mean?"
"Philemon?"
"Isn't that your name?"
He had sensed depths of slyness and possibly of
cruelty under her childlike surface. "My name means
of loving mind, or some such. Have you heard of Baucis?"
"No. Tell me!"
"Baucis was the wife of Philemon and they loved with
a love so deep that they did not die but were changed
into trecs.'
""
"Were they did they really live?"
"Did Ruth?"
"I don't know."65
"If a thing is beautiful it lives."
"Beauty! My people don't talk about that. They talk
about righteousness.'
""
"Righteousness! My people don't talk about that.
They talk about beauty."

Then her face was as solemn as that of an old woman.

"God, no," said Philemon. "I love you.'

"Me? Oh, but you don't. If you did you'd marry me

And a few days later, in the spring of the year, Angela
came with her sister. Unlike Angela, Judith wore a head-
covering, a simple napkin that loosely confined her hair.
Like her she wore a garment similar to the Greek him-

out the Hellenic world. Her girdle was a short rope

a way that the form of her breasts was visible, as well

as fine as any he had seen.
Judith came up and looked at him and said: "Did you

"No. Her father wouldn't let her. Philemon darling,
it will be much simpler for you to marry me."
"You really mean that my case with Judith is abso-
lutely hopeless?"
"Yes."
"I'll wager you that I shall marry her some day."
They had paused before a small tavern from which
came the sounds of music. Men of his social quality al-
most never entered inns, taverns, and post-houses, for
all these were little more than hideaways for thieves77
and harlots; but Philemon liked to sit and drink wine
in a place where he could study people ravaged by disease.
And besides, the food in this tavern was excellent.
"Will you go in here with me?"
"Do you have to ask that?"
Taking her arm to guide her he went inside and to
a small table by a wall. This was a Greek tavern. Phile-
mon had been here many times and knew the proprietor
well. He ordered a delicate wine from Coptos; and
when it was brought, a eunuch stood before him and
mixed intoxicating essences in a stone mortar, adding
from time to time a few drops of scented oil. This con-
coction he added to the wine, stirring it meanwhile,
and then poured the wine into two electrum bowls. Phile-
mon then ordered a basket of sweetcakes, fruits and
nuts.
Angela sipped her wine and sighed. "I like it here,"
she said. "I'm going to be a happy wife."

THE GREAT COUNCIL of learned men sat in their hall
and received the testimony of trust-worthy witnesses that
the new moon had been seen. Heralds would be dispatched
with the glad tidings and would wave torches from a
hilltop until answered with fires from distant hills; and
so, spread with flame, the news that the new moon-
month had risen would be communicated throughout
the land of Israel.
Lying in bed in an upstairs room of the tavern, Phile-
mon heard horns blowing, but whether to announce a
new month, the sabbath, or some other holy day, he did
not know; and while thinking of the strange people
here and their customs he wondered if he was making
a fool of himself. Could he hope to see the austerities78
softened in Judith's guardians? Should he proceed to
Egypt, and if he did, would it be prudent to take Angela
along?
The next morning he went to Reuben's house and
Reuben at once looked at him suspiciously and asked:
"Where were you last night?"
"I slept in a tavern."
"Don't you know those tavern wenches are rotten
with disease?"
"I had no wench," said Philemon. "I was drinking
wine with Angela and I drank too much and so went
upstairs and to bed."
"Hah! Wouldn't Angela go with you?"
"I didn't ask her to. That's no place to lie with a

Reuben was staring at him curiously. "Have you

"Once-for a few minutes. I suppose the one to talk

like that: 'Go away.'
9 99
Reuben was smiling. "Well, come on, I'll go with you

"Melanie doesn't want us. She wants a king. Princes,

"Well, there are many beautiful women among my

"Oh yes, Angela, for instance. But it doesn't seem

"Yes, God yes, I suppose we are."
They found Hosah sitting outside his house, his hands
piously folded and his gaze on the sky.79
"Hosah," Reuben cried, "what are you doing out here?
This is the sabbath day." Hosah did not speak or look

Hosah did not like proselytes. A few of the teachers
eagerly accepted them but Hosah's mind always turned
to the fact that Malachi had violently opposed the mix-

Philemon had been studying Hosah's face. "Some time
at your convenience I'd like to talk to you.'

Hosah a huge and complex branch of knowledge.'

"Epicurus in his letter to Pythocles said that a rain-

Philemon smiled. For many months he had been sleep-

"Can't we be part Greek and part Jew and leave

Greeks made poetry of religion; Hosah makes only des-

"It's worse than quibbling.'

ISRAEL HAD ADOPTED the Babylonian system of reckon-
ing the day from sunset to sunset and had divided the
day into twelve parts, the length of which varied with.
the season, and the night into three watches. Having
twelve lunar months, they had now and then to add an
extra month to catch up with the solar year. A year with
thirteen months was called pregnant. The days of the87
week were known as first, second, third, fourth, fifth,
sixth and sabbath. There was also a sabbatical year, in
which the fields lay fallow, running from Tishri to Tishri.
It was toward the end of the first watch in the second
day of the week in the month Iyyar and Philemon was
lying in bed thinking of Judith when to the left he heard
a sound and suspected that Angela was climbing a ladder.
Where she got it and how so small a girl bore it to
Reuben's wall he could not imagine, and she had refused
to tell him. Several times she had come for these night-
trysts, to perch on the wall a few feet from his bed and
talk to him. When he first met her he thought he saw
her plain. He had thought she was a mischievous and
impudent girl who, like Reuben and his comrades, was
in revolt against the burdensome customs but whose
interests were directed toward marrying a wealthy man,
either Jew or heathen. This opinion he had modified so
many times that there was little left of it. What she
was or even why she came to see him he did not know.
And now across the top of the wall came her voice,
filled as usual with laughter, breathing these words:
"Blessed are you, O Holy One, King of the world, who
make sleep fall on Philemon's eyes!"
"I hear you. Angela ?"
"It's Hepzibeth."
"Come sit where I can see you."
"Don't be silly."
“Angela darling, how are you tonight?"
"I do poorly," she said, "but-may it please the Holy
One-I don't complain."
"Come to bed with me."
"You talk like a toady."
She now came in sight and sat on top of the wall, with
her legs hanging over, and drew her cloak to her chin,
framing her lovely face with her garment and hair.
"Now," she said, having settled herself, but not com-
fortably, "let's talk."
"Sure. About what?"
"Tell me. If I go to the oracle of Daphne and dip88
a laurel leaf into the water, will she write on the leaf an
answer to my question?"
"What question?"
"Whether you'll ever marry me."
"Of course. But don't we have to be betrothed for
a whole year? And is it true that we aren't supposed to
lie with one another in all that time?”
“Oh—” said Angela, and seemed to be making a face
at the night. "They're not supposed to-but most of
them do."
"We would. And if I marry Judith also, would you live
with her?-or she with you?"
"Do you still yearn toward her ?"
"Lord yes, night and day. But they won't let me see
her. I guess they've locked her up. Is she betrothed yet
to that hewer of wood?"
"Enoch never hews wood. He spends all his time think-
ing of going in unto Judith-as the holy words put it."
"She doesn't love him, does she?"
Angela was quiet a few moments and then asked:
"What do you mean by love?"
"Well, in my language we have many words to express
love-eros, storge, philia, agape-we have sexual love,
family love, social love, natural love-compassion, pity,
ecstasy, rapture-those and more. But your people seem
to have only one kind of love."
"Only god-love," she said.
"But what about the other kinds of love? Don't you
ever experience those?"
The moon was rising, and in the waxing light he could
see that she was smiling. She said: "I thought you'd
read our holy books. Let him kiss me with the kisses
of his mouth, for his love is better than wine!"
"Yes-but men like Hosah never read those books.
A bundle of myrrh is my well-beloved unto me; he will
lie all night between my breasts. Behold, you are fair,
my love! Behold, you are fair!"
"His left hand is under my head, and his right hand
does embrace me! Oh, I am the rose of Sharon and I89
am the lilies of the valley; as the lily among the thorns,
so am I among the daughters."
"So you are. Angela, come over here."
"No!"
"Then I'll come to you.'
""
“And I'll jump from the wall and break my neck. For
my beloved spoke and said unto me, Rise, my love, my
fair one, and come away!"
"Come to me!"
"For lo, the winter is past, the rain is over and gone!
Have you ever been in the country around Jericho?"
"No."
"Oh, the narcissus is lovely there, and the crocus and
the anemone! How heavy the air is with the sweetness!
Great fields of purple iris I've seen there I've taken
my clothes off and rolled over and over in them-over

"Not all kinds," said Philemon. "Some kinds—"
"Oh, I know what you mean," she said impatiently.
"I'm not a child. Your Plato said that sexual love is
injurious. Was he queer?"
"A pretty queer old stick."
"You're a physician. Is it?"
"No. Empedocles thought excessive indulgence injures

"Does it?" she asked, looking at him. She was sitting

With a furious gesture she stopped him. "Because

and poets! And I've heard she knows all the ways to

"Is it any of your stinking business?"
"Well, I should imagine—if I'm going to marry you.
Your holy books say—"
"Oh, I know what they say! If any man take a wife
and go in unto her and hate her and give occasions of

I gave my daughter unto this man to wife and he hates

she has wrought folly in Israel to play the whore in her
father's house."
"Wonderful!" he cried.
"What is wonderful? Am I a virgin? Don't be a com-
plete fool. What daughter of Israel would dare to lose
her tokens? We'd sooner lose our eyes and tongue!"
She spoke with awful bitterness. Philemon had sup-
posed that this girl had lain with men; he now saw his
error, and when he thought of her daring in coming to
him, as now, and in going with him to taverns, he could
find no pattern of meaning, and Jews became for him
even more strange than they had been. What was it that
kept this girl's tokens, though she mocked and spurned
the Torah, though she loathed the burden that Israel had
laid on women?
Softly he said: "Lo, my eyes have seen all this, my ears
have heard and understood it. We are forgers of lies and91
physicians of no value. For man that is born of woman
is of few days and full of trouble. He comes forth like
a flower and is cut down; he flees as a shadow, and con-
tinues not. There is hope of a tree, if it be cut down,
that it will sprout again, and the tender branch thereof
will not cease. But man dies and wastes away, yes, man
gives up the ghost, and where is he? And where is he?
As the waters fall from the sea and the flood dries up,
so man lies down and rises not.
"Angela, won't you come over here?"
"No."
He thought he heard the sounds of grief in her voice.
He thought he had seen her furtively wiping tears from
her eyes.
"I know," he said, "that you won't come, that you
can't. I suppose I understand it now. And though I were
to give to Judith such love as man never gave, she would
not come.
99
"Why don't you go away from us? Only grief will
ever come to you here. Philemon, believe me, while it is
not too late. For all our righteousness there's in us some
terrible evil, some monstrous and abominable wickedness,
and even though hating we cling to one another. We're
no good for you. We hate-hate! Hosah hates ordinary
Jews, Paul hates Hosah, I hate both of them.
"Philemon, I'll go sit on your bed if you'll promise
not to touch me—and believe me, this is not guile. I
simply want to talk—and to look at you."
"All right. May I bury my wife and sons if I touch

"You have no wife and sons."
"Then what oath can I make?"
"Oh well, I'm being silly. I tell you not to touch me:
that's the way I want it. And you know that if you—"
"Yes, yes. I know."
She arose and went over and sat toward the foot of

other. This girl, usually so gay and dimpling and mis-92
chievous, was older than her years. She looked very sad,
like one with secret and insatiable sorrows.
"Do you have a house?" she asked.
"Several houses."
"Where?"
"Oh, I have one in Corinth, one in Tarsus, one in Per-
gamum, one in Antioch."
"Are they nice houses like this one?"
For a Greek of Philemon's tastes and wealth, Reuben's
house was a mean thing without comforts. But he said:
"Yes, nice like this one."
"Why do you have so many houses?"
"I inherited them. You see, my father was a merchant,
who made and sold beautiful doors and gates. In every
city where he had a factory he built a house."
"Did you inherit all his houses?"
"I was his only child."
"Oh," she said, looking at him with sober childlike
interest. "Your people don't like babies."
"Of course we like babies."
"But you put them out to die."
"Sometimes."
"We never do."
"Very well, that's one thing I admire about Jews."
"But why do your people?"
"Angela, let's not be absurd. You asked me this once
before. Only a few of our babies are abandoned. Very
obviously I was not."
"No," she said, looking at him strangely. "But all
your brothers and sisters-"
"No, great God, no! I had no brothers, I had no
sisters."
"But why do your people kill their babies?"
"Angela darling, will you listen? It's an old custom.
Why did your Micah ask in your holy bible, Must I give
my firstborn for my transgression? Why did Jeremiah
say that Jews burned their sons and daughters? Old cus-
toms. Why does your bible say that if a Jew finds a dead93
carcass he must not sell it to another Jew but he may sell
it to non-Jews?"
Angela smiled and said: "I like you better now. Phile-
mon, I'm going to move a little closer because I like to

godhome blue. But don't misunderstand me."
"Do we have to keep speaking of that? Besides, I'm
watching your neck."
"What do you mean?"
"Democritus said that a woman's neck swells when she
looks on a man with ardor. Your neck, Angela darling,
has been shrinking.'

She raised a hand and clasped her throat. "Now you
mock me
Well, some day, darling, it may swell
toward you... How silly you make me feel!"
"I like you better now," he said.
She looked into his eyes for several moments, saying

"I'll tell you a story that will summarize all that Ho-

"Hosah believes that when your god gave a set of

"Who's mocking now!"

"Well-of a second-rate princess."
"Offer Judith's parents that much and see what they
do."
For a few moments he considered a way, strange to
him, by which Jews expressed contempt for one another.
They would say, as Angela had, not my parents but Ju-

but Paul's sister Judith.
"If they took the price would Judith accept me?"
"She'd have to."
"I'd rather win her than buy her or subdue her.”
Angela wore a small charm against evil; this she now

can't be sure of that; but Judith you can never win.'
""
What a strange girl! he thought: she looked a man

"You're telling me I can't win your sister, hoping I'll

"Oh, good heavens! Don't smooth your face with such

Startled, he asked: "What did you say?"
"I will rise now and go about the city in the streets,95
and in the broad ways I will seek him whom I love. I

"Whither has my beloved gone, O the fairest among

"My beloved has gone down into his garden to gather

Again startled, he looked at her sharply, but this time

"Terrible as an army with banners.
"I don't understand and I suppose it doesn't matter.
Will you come again?"

There it was again, that strange subtlety. "When

"They are."
"Judith's brother doesn't think so."
"Musonius said the study of philosophy is as essential

"No."
"Zeno's disciples believe in a common brotherhood of

"Men and women?" she asked, doubting him.

"It will never be."
"Maybe some time."

story of Hosea. What did he mean? Hosea's wife was

"Yes?"
"You haven't kissed me goodnight."
She sat and seemed to consider. Then she came back

Something in her manner chilled him. He was looking

"When the trouble comes-the real trouble-when
Jew kills Jew-when brother murders his brother,
mother denies her daughter, and father slays his own

will you be on?"
He felt inane and confused and angry. This girl's
thought, he perceived now, had been far from love and
kissing; her eyes were frankly accusing him, her whole
body was waiting for his answer. He raised himself to

less he asked: "What sides are there?"
"You know, for Reuben has told you. Hosah's side
and Paul's side. Zeno's side and Ezekiel's side. Judith's
side and my side."
"What nonsense!" he cried. He reached out to her.
"Angela !"
"No!" In that moment she hated him. She went to
the wall and descended until she could just look across
the top at him; and she said: "This is my goodnight
kiss-that I shall come again-for your answer."
Then she was gone. After a few moments Philemon
arose, still astonished, and went to the wall and looked97
down; but there was no sign of Angela and no sign of
the ladder. He knew now that some man had been com-
ing with her. That, it seemed to him, was even stranger
than her coming, was somehow sinister and evil; and
feeling a chill of apprehension he stood by the wall look-
ing across Jerusalem. When he returned to his bed he lay
for a long time in thought.

THE WEEKS PASSED and in vain Philemon tried to see
Hosah at the scribe's house; for always when he called
to Hosah, Sabra put her ashen face round the doorpost
and said simply and firmly: "Go away." One day, feeling
desperate, Philemon refused to go away, and Hosah's
voice called: "Not now, please! I'm busy!" If Philemon
had then known what occupied Hosah's attention he
would have been speechless. For it was true, as Reuben
had contemptuously said, that before one of the Pious
could embrace his wife he had to make preparations
according to the laws. Hosah was getting ready to lie
with his wife, who had given him only one child, a cir-
cumstance that deeply grieved him.
Because it was believed that children conceived the
first three days of the week were born on the sabbath,
pious men like Hosah refused to be intimate on those
days. The sabbath eve was the most favorable time for
sexual union because the sabbath would shape the char-
acter of the child. Conception at midnight was more
desirable than at any other time; some said this was so
because all the unclean powers were then asleep; and
some that there were no night-sounds to lure the husband's
thoughts to another woman.
As for the preparations, these were many, and occupied
Hosah's entire attention the sixth day. If the union oc-98
curred in the presence of a light, no matter how small,
the child might be epileptic-that is, possessed by a de-
mon. Hosah carefully excluded all light from the bed-
chamber by stuffing pieces of cloth around the door. He
reminded himself over and over that during the embrace
he must not have impure thoughts, because the state of
mind of the parents was transmitted to the child; he
must adopt no unnatural nor even unusual posture nor
employ unusual ways of wooing, for all these were of
evil; and in the moment of release he must be suffused
with joyous and holy thoughts. Some of the wise men
said that because they were conceived in intense joy all
bastards were remarkably bright.
The death of infants, in the opinion of Hosah and his
fellows, was due to neglect of the proper thoughts be-
fore and after union. He therefore had drawn up a list
of thoughts to dwell on. In spite of all he could do, his
mind turned now and then to the trick which Jacob played
on his fatherinlaw, when he put peeled rods in the water-
ing-trough, with the result that the flocks conceived at
sight of the rods, bringing forth streaked, speckled and
spotted offspring. To neutralize a thought so filled with
evil he murmured holy words over and over; and when,
suddenly, he thought of Philemon's lusting after Judith,
he cried out: "The Holy One is the fountain of living
waters, he is the long life of Israel, he is the bundle of
life, he it is who blesses the fruit of the womb!"
During the afternoon of the sixth day he refused to
look out of his house, lest he see something wicked: it
was as necessary to protect himself from these things as
from the unclean thoughts that invaded the heart. Dur-
ing the forenoon of this day he had indiscreetly looked
out and had seen, on the brow of the hill above him,
two men, one of whom he recognized as the impostor
who called himself Menelaus. This upstart, who was
not even of high priestly lineage, was one of the most
lecherous miscreants in all the lanes of earth, being so
driven by his lustful passions that he had developed the
habit of muttering to himself and making strange symbols99
with his long fingers. When Hosah saw the rogue he
drew back quickly, crying, "The Holy One reigns!" and
following immediately with these words from Jesus:
"For many have perished by the beauty of a woman,
and lust is enkindled as a fire! Every woman that is a
harlot shall be trodden upon as dung! Sit not at all with
another man's wife. . . ."
He considered the matter, standing like one sunk
deep in thought; and when at last he felt that the holy
words had not been enough to neutralize such a mon-
strous wickedness as Menelaus he lifted other gems from
his storehouse:
"Wine and women make wise men fall off. . . .
"The lust of a eunuch shall deflower a young
maiden. .
"Tarry not among women.
"Look not on women!-look not on women!
look not on women!"
Sabra meanwhile was in similar fashion preparing her-
self; and just before the midnight hour, having completed
her ablutions, she went softly to bed. Her husband knelt
in prayer: "Cause us to lie down, O Holy One, in peace;
and cause us to rise, O our King, to life. . . .Deliver
us for your Name's sake. . . .Shelter us. . . .Guard our
going out and coming in. .Blessed are you, O Ever-
lasting, who guard your people Israel!"
·
·
Then he sought his wife and went in unto her, and
the thoughts of both dwelt on holy matters.
Hosah slept soundly and awoke refreshed. The mo-
ment he opened his eyes he said: "O Holy One, how
gracious is your Name in all the earth!" Then he re-
called that he was impure with a major impurity and
had before him the ordeal of cleansing himself. Slipping
into his garments he went outside, walking carefully so
that he would touch no part of his house but the floor.
He sat on a stone on the hillside a little distance from
the doorway; and here he would sit all day, for he had100
built a fence round the Torah and would run no risk
of breaching it.
He recalled with horror that for a few moments
during the embrace he had thought his wife was in her
sickness. It was written: "And if a man shall lie with a
woman having her sickness and shall uncover her naked-
ness-he has made naked her fountain and she has un-
covered the fountain of her blood-both of them shall
be cut off from among their people." Punishment for
sexual embrace during the menses was death. To avoid
being tempted, men like Hosah showed to wives no af-
fection during the days of the sickness, nor touched
them, nor handed to them anything that might cause
their fingers to meet, nor ate out of the same dish, nor
sat with them on the bed, nor looked at any part of
them unclothed save the hands and face. It was foolish
of Hosah to suspect his wife-and this, after a few
moments, breathing more freely, he realized.
·
All day Hosah would sit here. He must not read, or
even touch, the holy writings-and he was not sure that
it was not wicked to think about them. But he could
dwell on some matters about which the wise men held
different opinions: if a Jew bought utensils from a heath-
en he must purify them by dipping those to be dipped,
boiling those to be boiled, heating those to be heated;
but of knives it was said that they needed only to be sharp-
ened, and were then clean. Hosah was not sure. He was
not sure whether a clean Jew traveling in foreign lands
ought to eat the food grown there, provided the heathen
had not touched it. He was not sure whether a stove
ought to be smashed to pieces or left to cool after wood
from a heathen grove had been burned in it.
He was wondering why any Jew should wish to leave.
Israel when, on looking up, he was startled to observe
the impudent and malicious face of Hepzibeth. The
knowledge of why Hosah was sitting here was a wicked
light in her eyes.
"Unclean again," she said. He looked at her with
cold disapproval and was silent. "I'm always unclean,101
and you now are, so we can talk. We might even touch
one another!"
"Go away," said Hosah.
"Philemon wants to talk to you-" she began but

her presence. This so infuriated her that she seized a
handful of dust and hurled it full into his face. Turning
gray with rage she shrieked at him: "I've heard you
say that some people strain out the gnats and swallow

ridiculous preposterous monkey-man!"
"May you live in peace," said Hosah quietly. "May
your days be long upon the earth—'
""
"Ohh!" she cried, loathing his pious face. "You toa-

if two, put a halter on yourself."

When Philemon came an hour later Hosah said sharp-

If you must persist in thinking of yourself as a holy

There were those who said that Hosah ben Jorah had
never smiled in all the years of his life. He was smiling

"The best qualities of both," he said; and for a mo-

natural, as the Greeks had done.
But it was only for a few moments. While Greek and

"That big awkward dolt doesn't have the marriage-

"I'd rather she were the bride of righteousness than

"Ask her."104
"I've no need to ask her. Judith's first love is for the

And I might become a proselyte."

"No of course not. But I'm going to the synagogue,

"I'm always there."
At dinner Philemon turned to Reuben and Paul and
said: "Have the Jews a deeper truth than the logos?"107
"I'll answer that," said Reuben acidly, "when some

"Maybe." Turning to Paul he asked: "Have you ever

PHILEMON WOULD HAVE BEEN a stupid man had he not
sensed that he was pursuing a course that could only
lead him deep into trouble; but he played with the no-
tion that he might win, thinking: I'll wait a little longer,
I'll go hear Hosah, I'll ask him a question. And so it
was that he went to the simple mudbrick structure and
entered; and after observing that the women were in
a room apart, he went to the door opening on this
smaller chamber and looked in. All the women stared at
him, because with his lighter hair and blue eyes and fair
skin he obviously was not a Jew; and they looked at him
all the more intently because only rarely did a man ever
come over to look in at them. Philemon saw Judith and
met her eyes, and to the casual observer her face might
have seemed cold; but he sensed something round her
mouth or in the way her hand came up, with two fingers
touching her throat. He knew that her interest in him
was thriving.
When he turned away to find a seat he remembered
that seating was according to rank and distinction and
that he belonged in the rear. There he sat, and looked
round him, and saw, off to the left and alone, two lepers.
The far end of the room was elevated and was furnished
with a table, a lamp, and a trumpet; and beyond these
was a curtain which seemed to screen off an alcove. In
the alcove, Philemon supposed, was the ark, which
housed the sacred rolls.
When Hosah entered from the east and walked the
length of the chamber, proceeding to the west, where109
the ark reposed, all eyes were fixed upon him. Hosah did
not wear fine raiment; his clothes were coarse and com-
monplace, including the kerchief that bound his hair.
But he was clothed with wisdom and he walked proudly
in the holiness of one who interpreted the words of Is-
rael's God. In a later time the synagogue would have a
general supervisor, an almoner to take up offerings, va-
rious persons to read from the scriptures, and a minister
to bring forth the scrolls, as well as to scourge the con-
demned. Upon Hosah rested all these.
He ascended to the platform, stretched his arms sky-
ward and said: "Bless the Holy One who is to be bless-
ed!" And the deep male voices of the congregation re-
sponded:
"Blessed be the Holy One who is to be blessed forever
and ever!"
"Hear, O Israel, the Lord is One! And you shall love
the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your
soul, and with all your might. And these words, which
I command you this day, shall be in your heart; and
you shall teach them to your children, and you shall talk
of them when you sit in your house, and when you walk
by the way, and when you lie down, and when you rise
up..
"Blessed be the Lord, whose Name is Holy!"
Now, at a signal from Hosah, everyone stood and

him and saw that some lips were moving and some were

swaying gently to and fro like one enveloped by soothing
and, it seemed to Philemon, erotic emotions. When the

"The law of the Holy One is perfect, restoring the110
soul; the testimony of the Holy One is sure, making

rejoicing the heart; the commandment of the Holy One

is clean, enduring forever; the ordinances of the Holy
One are true, they are righteous altogether; more to be
desired are they than gold, yes, than much fine gold;

warned; in keeping them there is great reward."
The congregation responded: "In keeping them there

Hosah was looking at Philemon. He now called by

"Who set his heart to seek the law of the Holy One

"Ezra."
"What is the word for the second day?"
"Great is the Lord."
"What is the word for the third day?"
"The Presence is in the congregation of the mighty."
"What is the word for the fourth day?"
"Our God is a God of vengeance."
"What is the word for the fifth day?"
"Exult aloud unto the Holy Name who is our strength."
"What is the word for the sixth day?"
"The Presence reigns."
"Well spoken. The high praise of the Holy One is in

"One is our God, great is our Lord, Holy is his Name!"
"Despise not a man in his old age for we shall become

"Holy-holy-holy is the Name!"111

Some in the audience were now staring at him and

"Sit down."

"Learn what? That a god commands his people utterly

There was a rumble of astonishment and anger in the

"Read the story of Ruth or the story of Jonah but

Philemon looked over to the entrance to the women's

Addressing Hosah, Philemon cried: "Boaz was one
of you, yet Boaz took Ruth, an alien and the daughter

is one of your stories-and I like to think that it belongs

Alarmed, Hosah cried: "Don't blaspheme the Holy
One in his place!"
"Who is blaspheming? You-who make your god no

-are these warriors?—is no better than Zeus, and may

He turned again to Judith, who still stood in the

"It was written by the Prophet," said Hosah, "that

"No. That's more arrogance. But if I were to go with

god who commanded you to destroy the old, the sick,

Men were looking at Philemon and asking: "Who is
this who comes among us and disputes with our wise
men?" Or they were saying: "He's a spy from the
King's citadel."
"Will you sit down?" Hosah asked.
Philemon sank to the bench. The eyes that had been
upon him now turned to Hosah.
"Our lesson today is the creation of the world. With
ten words the world was created. Nine times in our
holy books it says, And the Name said. But it is also
written, In the beginning the Name created the heaven
and earth. So ten times the Name uttered, and the world
and the universe were created; for it is written, By the
word of the Holy One were the heavens made.
"Could all this have been created with one word? No.
There were ten generations from Adam to Noah, ten
from Noah to Abraham. Abraham was tried with ten
trials. In Egypt the Name wrought for our fathers ten
miracles. And what were they? No woman miscarried
because of the odor of holy flesh. The holy flesh never
became putrid. No flies were seen in the slaughtering-
place. No uncleanness ever came upon the High Priest

There was no defect in the omer or in the bread of the

Philemon was looking round him to study the faces.
It was plain that all these people were listening with
bated breath to every word Hosah uttered-and accept-

on and on; there seemed to be no end to the marvels
and wonders.
But at last the lesson was said, the final prayer was
uttered, the meeting was adjourned. Outside, small groups

but moved quickly over to her brother, her eyes down-
cast.
Philemon went over to Hosah and said: "I'll walk
with Judith to the house of her parents."
"No. She'll walk with me."
"Then I'll walk with both of you.'
"No. We prefer to be alone."
"All right," said Philemon angrily, looking at Judith,
"then I'll be on my way to Alexandria. I won't stay
here and be put upon and made to feel like a leper.'

Then, with her gaze upon him, Judith shook her
head, moving it slowly, gently from side to side, twice.
The gesture was barely perceptible, but her brother per-
ceived it, as well as Philemon. Philemon did not know,
but some day was to realize, how much that faint gesture
of courage and encouragement cost her.
"Come!" said her brother sharply, and took her away.
An hour later, Philemon sat with Reuben and Paul,
drinking a sweet wine mixed with aromatics, and nibbling

"If his stones ache let him go to Melanie."
"Well, you went to the synagogue. Did you learn any-

"No."

You push on him when he's about to pray. You want

Philemon arose and went to the wall and looked over

Isn't it enough that the barbarous Romans are enslaving

Has he never heard of Rome? Even now the King of

ways only one side-the side that cures and heals.'

"You have great skill," said Reuben coldly, "in self-

"Soon."

"Well, yes--a miracle that will take the fanaticism
