PHILEMON WAS SHOCKED, confused and filled with grief.
He went to the Greek tavern and engaged a room, tossed
his belongings into a corner, sat on the bed with his
hands clasping his face and wondered what he ought to
do. That affairs in Israel were rapidly building to a tre-
mendous explosion he did not doubt at all; and he would
have set out at once for Alexandria or Antioch but for
that one fateful moment when Judith slowly shook her
head. That moment changed the course of his life.
He would stay a few weeks or a few months longer
but should he go to the synagogue again? Was there
anything more in Hosah's steadfast devotion than ugly
fanaticism? Was there any more in the apothegms of
Jesus ben Sirach than the stale scholia of discredited
sages? Had the great men among the Jews gone deeper
than Greek philosophers into the truths under appear-
ances?
"You sound awfully silly," said Melanie, to whom he118
was asking these questions. "There is no wisdom in
these people. There's only passion."
"Only passion?"
"All their thinking is passionate. If they were to look
at an icicle in freezing weather they would melt it. Show
me a saying anywhere in their writings that doesn't turn
soft when you touch it."
"A virtuous woman is a crown to her husband."
"What do they mean by a virtuous woman? One so
fenced around that she can't breathe. I'm a virtuous
woman. So was Aspasia. Try to make them understand
that."
"As a jewel of gold in a swine's snout, so is a fair
woman without discretion."
"Other people said it first."
"A soft answer turns away wrath."
"Reuben explained that to me. They mean the evil
eye. Have you found anything good in all their com-
mandments?"
"Oh yes but I think Solon did better."
"Honor your father and your mother! Even bastards.
are commanded to!"
"A whore, says one of their proverbs, is a deep ditch."
"And hope deferred makes the heart sick-but Reuben
says the Egyptians said it first. Philemon, why don't you
cover yourself with a prayer-shawl. You've been around
here over a year and still you don't understand these
people. I do."
"You do?"
"It's very simple. They expect to be rewarded for
being good. Think about that long enough and you'll
understand them. They remind me of a story Reuben
told me. Euclid was teaching geometry to a young man
when the fellow asked, What do I get for learning these
things? Euclid said to an assistant, Give this lout a
penny, since it appears that he must be paid for learning.
If people have to be paid to learn or to be good they'll
never be either good or learned. I was taught that the119
virtuous person is happier than the evil person. That's
all."
"That's enough."
"But these pious Jews think people are utterly evil
and must struggle day and night against sin. It's the
most horrible thing I ever heard of. Our philosophers
say that people are naturally good, but Jews say they
are naturally evil."
"Some of them say it."
"And they think the corpse will be filled with life
again!" Melanie shuddered and made a face. "Where
did they get that awful notion?"
"I don't know."
"Do they mean the dust ?" asked Melanie, blinking
with horror. "The decayed bones and flesh? That these
somehow will take up their former shape? And you find
wisdom in a people who believe such nonsense? Well,
darling, for me, I am the creature of a day and a summer.
That is all and that's enough."
For Philemon also that was enough. He was sitting
in his room one evening thinking of Judith when he recall-
ed her odor. While studying medicine in Alexandria he
had become interested in the intimacy between body
odors and carnal passion and had pursued this interest,
particularly in Egyptian lore. Greeks for a long time had
been skilled in the blending and employment of per-
fumes. They used them in their dining-halls; saturated
foods and wines with sweet scents; stored their clothes
in chests of aromatic woods; and used many essences in
their subtle art of love. Philemon knew that the sense
of smell was the most intimate, the most private, of all
the senses. There were people who did not kiss by pressing
their lips together but by placing their noses side by side
and breathing the odors emanating from the cheeks. For
a sensitive man, he had learned, every woman had an
odor peculiar to her. Indeed, some peoples beyond the
Euphrates classified their women according to their
odors.
Here in Jerusalem all people but the Pious and the120
poor drenched themselves with sweet scents. Women
like Angela carried with them sachets and scent-bags-
and what the body odor of Angela was, Philemon had
never learned. She assumed, like so many vain and fool-
ish women, that the scents she chose were pleasing to
the men she sought, whether these were musk and cham-
pak, jasmine, sandalwood, bergamot, or blends of these
and others. Angela seemed to have a great fondness
for jasmine and lily.
As for Judith, her odors were her own, and in the
only two moments when he had been close to her Phile-
mon had found them ravishing. He had learned from
medical writings, and then from his own experiments,
that some women, when placed close to a man toward
whom they yearned, suffered a rise in body temperature,
which in turn heightened their emotional tension and
intensified their body odors. He had found the smell
of Judith delightful, whereas the lily and jasmine scents
on Angela made him feel enervated and slightly sickened.
As a physician and a curious man observing people
he had also learned that the natural odor of some men
was almost irresistible to some women. But not, of course,
to all women, nor even to any but a few women. Vari-
ations in odors and preferences seemed to distinguish
all peoples. Melanie-she had told him this-found
his natural odor quite intoxicating; but on the other hand
some women had found him repulsive.
"Do you like the smell of me?" she had asked. He
had lied. He had said yes. He did not find Melanie's
odor offensive, but neither did it swim over his senses
like the fragrant warmth of a vineyard.
While thinking of Judith and the way she affected him,
he recalled having read in the Jewish holy writings that
upon King David, when stricken with age and disease,
the people had thrust a virgin. The King had tried to
get heat by smelling her. Philemon had read various
papyri on which old men had left messages-one of them
saying that he had lived a hundred years, happy and
virile and free of sickness, because whenever possible he121
breathed the odors of young girls. Some physicians said
there were invisible essences which passed between the
bodies of a man and a woman enamored of one another.
Philemon doubted that: but Hippocrates himself had
pointed out that not until puberty and the inception of
sexual ardor did the body, of either boy or girl, acquire
an odor of such quality and strength as to be appealing
to the other sex.
Philemon's ardor had been captured by a beautiful
Jewess. He spent restless nights; and during many of the
afternoons he sat on the western hill, thinking of himself
with mirth tinctured with bitterness. The explosive na-
ture of his emotions rocked him now and then or fetched
from him a sudden snort of dismay. Had he become
enamored of an odor? That would be nothing new in his
life. In Antioch there was a tavern whose kitchen odors
he could never pass, less because of the appetizing smells
than for the presence of an odor which he had never
been able to identify. Whatever this was it went into
him subtly and deep. There had been a girl in Tarsus....
But was he going to stay here and again make a fool of
himself? Infatuated with a child!—for surely he had no
honest wish to marry the girl-or had he? Why did he
linger in this ill-smelling and accursed city, whose odors,
food, and comforts were the worst he had ever known?
"Why?" he asked Angela one afternoon when she
came to talk with him. She had a red ribbon in her hair
and was drenched with jasmine.
"For Judith," she said, and shrugged.
"My God, did you bathe in stinkhorn or peccary?"
She lowered her lids at him and sneered: "Polecat..
You bastard, don't you like it? Does everything that I
do displease you?"
"Why don't you let me smell you as you are?"
"Zeus darling!" She had picked up the habit of calling
him by the names of Greek gods. "My natural odors
aren't nice."
"That's because you have an evil temper."
"Or eat the wrong foods?"122
"Both."
"Dearest Apollo !" Half in whimsy and half in derision
she was addressing him. "Gorgeous Hermes!"
"Wonderful Athene!"
"Poseidon darling, when are we going to walk on the
waters and get married?"
"When you bring Judith as your dowry."
"Would you find it impossible to be content with me?"
"Quite impossible."
With the toe of a sandal she was turning a small stone
over and over. "You know," she said, looking at the
stone, "when the fighting comes, if you're not on our side
I shall hope that to me will fall the honor of killing you."
"Good Lord!" he said.
She looked up and met his eyes. "Eros darling, did
I wound you?"
"Why did you say that?"
"Have you been listening to Hosah?"
"Yes-in the synagogue.
"Then you ought to know why I said it."
Several times Philemon had gone to hear Hosah. With
astonishment he had heard:
"Why is rain withheld from us in times of drought?
Because we neglect the Torah....”
With amusement he had heard:
"Three great leaders have arisen in Israel: Moses,
Aaron and Miriam; and for their sakes three precious
gifts were given us: the well, the pillar of cloud, and the
manna. The well was given for Miriam...."
With incredulity he had heard:
"A mourner is not allowed to bathe in warm water.
Why is this? Because bathing in warm water is a pleas-
ure. A mourner is not allowed to put even a finger into
warm water. It's an indulgence. ...
And again with astonishment:
""
"Why is it written, The righteous shall flourish like
the palmtree; he shall grow like a cedar? Why is he com-
pared to both? Because if the holy writing had mentioned
only the palmtree and not the cedar, one might have123
said that as the palmtree does not produce young shoots,
neither does the righteous. If only the cedar had been
mentioned, one might have said that as a cedar bears
no fruit...."
Invariably, when his remarks were in any way applic-
able to Philemon, Hosah looked at him, sometimes ac-
cusingly, sometimes with gentle interest. Members of
the congregation then turned to look at him. Most of
them, aware of his interest in Judith, condemned him
for his presumption; a few, of hotter blood, would have
driven him from the city.
One day after leaving the synagogue, Hosah said to
Philemon: "Are you storing up treasures?"
Jews spoke so commonly in parable that Philemon did
not answer at once but looked at Hosah and considered
the words. "I suppose you mean wisdom."
"Wisdom," said Hosah, "is the greatest of the treas-
ures." Hosah went on, gently, patiently, as though speak-
ing to a very dull disciple: "In the afternoon of the
sixth day my comrades and I meet to dip the sop and dis-
cuss matters; and once in a while we admit someone-"
he was choosing his words with the utmost tact "--who,
though not one of us, seeks understanding. If you would
like to come-'
"Well, I suppose. But Hosah, tell me how long must
I listen to you and dwell on your words and take your
counsel before I'm to be allowed to talk to Judith? I'm
almost thirty. I'm not-well, for Judith I must seem
to be an old man now. Are you going to put me off for-
ever ?"
Hosah said, gently chiding: "It is not I. If you learn
quickly, the time will go by like an eagle; but if you are
obstinate and hold back, then you will indeed be old
before you are wise."
"That's not much of an answer, Hosah. Is it your
plan that I shall have Judith ?"
99
"All such things rest with the Holy One-blessed be
he. You ask me questions to which I have no answers.'
"Why don't you let me see Judith now and then? How124
in the name of Hades are we to know whether we want
to marry one another?"
"It will be revealed to you in due time."
When he told Angela that Hosah had invited him to
the holy supper of the Comrades, her astonishment was
boundless, though whether for him or for Hosah he
could not tell.
"So let's go to a tavern and eat and drink and be merry,
for soon Israel will have another son."
"Oh no!" she cried, shrinking from him. "If you mean
that, I certainly won't eat with you."
He moved to take her arm but she flung herself away,
her eyes loathing him. "Angela, if you were to command
me, what would you have me do?"
"You know well enough. Marry me and take me to
Alexandria."
"Where would we live there?"
"Among your people."
"I don't think you'd be happy among my people."
"O God, how I hate you!" she said, and never had he
seen such passion in her face. Then suddenly she burst
into tears, sinking to the bench, and bowed to her arms
and wept.
Her grief was so spontaneous and overwhelming that
Philemon was looking at her with wonder and pity when
a voice behind him said:
"Why do you torture her?"
Philemon turned and saw before him the tall hand-
some youth who was called Enoch.
"Why do you say I torture her?"
"That's all you've done ever since you met her. You
would also torture Judith if we would let you."
"You lie!" said Philemon. He glanced at Angela and
observed that she was listening, though her face was
hidden.
"You fling insults carelessly," said Enoch, and deliber-
ately made a show of clenching his big hands.
"You're young," said Philemon impatiently. "You don't125
understand this girl. You know even less than a ghost
why she weeps."
99
"On the contrary," said Enoch coldly, "that's some-
thing that only a Jew can understand."
Philemon looked at him more sharply. Enoch, he had
heard, was only seventeen, but he looked much older.
With a fine torso which he managed with casual grace-
fulness, with strong features and magnificent curly hair,
he was a handsome figure. But his eyes Philemon did not
like. They were small and they shone with evil.
The two men were looking at one another, eye to

"When are you going to get out of our city?"
Angela raised her head and spoke sharply. "Enoch,
you idiot!" And then to Philemon: "Don't mind him,
Zeus. He's jealous."
"Why should I get out of this city?"
"Because we don't want you here. Is that plain
enough?"

knew by the way she moved that she was afraid. “Enoch,
go away!"
Then anger came up in Enoch and his eyes opened wide
and flashed. His lungs filled with air as he brought his
clenched hands before him. For a moment he stood like
a man ready to spring, but then his eyelids fell, he
shrugged, his hands relaxed. The anger in him had
drained his face white.
Angela took Philemon's arm and then released it. She

doesn't get out of our city."
"Your city?"
"Our city!"
"Enoch, shut up!"
No longer trying to hide his feelings, Enoch openly
sneered. "Do I make myself clear?".126
"What do you propose? Shall we go to the gymna-

It is impossible to say what might have happened if

"Hah!" cried Enoch, his voice choked. "Marriage-
price! Why that?-when our women throw themselves

"Oh, by the gods !" she shrieked. "You beast! You—
you dog! Go! Did you hear me? Go!" When he made
no move to go she stepped close to him and breathed
up into his face these words: "Go!-or I will curse you
with the Name!"
For one who lived in terror of the magic in words
that was more than enough. Nothing else under the sky
could have so quickly reduced this angry and powerful
youth to the meekness of a feeble old man. Philemon
saw his face blanch. Philemon watched him turn away,
trembling, abject, terrified, and go down the hill and out
of sight. Then he went to the bench and bowed his head
to his arms.
After a few moments Angela came over and sat by
him. "Philemon?"
"Yes?" he said, without looking up.
"After that, are you on our side?"
"I don't know. Maybe."
"Judith-you'll think I'm malicious in saying this--
Judith is much like him."
"I suppose all people are much like him." He stood
up and the disgust that he felt for himself was in his
eyes. "Come-let's go to the tavern and get drunk. You
know, Angela, maybe we do belong together. I don't
think either one of us is worth a harlot's scent-bag."
They turned away and Angela took his arm. "We're
two superior people," she said, poking him in the ribs.127
"We're worth all the Enochs and Judiths in the whole
wide world."

PHILEMON EXPECTED to be wearied with the conceits
and sophistries of Hosah and his comrades but he was
glad for the chance to see Jesus ben Sirach, a very old
man, whose most striking feature was his mane of snow-
white hair. His broad full dome was bald save for a
few hairs that stood up, curly and white, like frosted
grasses. On either side of his head was a great fluff of
hair, clean, windblown, loose, and as white as wool. This
stood out above his ears and fell in curls down over his
ears and came around in soft bangs almost to the heavy
gray arches of his brows. His brow was high and full,
with seven deep lines across it, each in the shape of two
inverted crescent moons joined at the inner tips. His
nose was huge and bulbous and on either side of it was
a line so deep that it formed a recess under the cheek.
Jesus was known for a book which his comrades called
Instruction or Wisdom but which in a later time among
other peoples would be known as Ecclesiasticus. He held
the Greek philosophers in contempt; dismissed specula-
tion as a waste of time; asked, "Why should a man
speculate when all truth has been given to Israel?"; and
looked with open scorn upon Amiel and his strange be-
lief in resurrection of the body and the coming of a
savior-king.
When all the comrades were seated, Hosah said they
had a guest and he presented Philemon; and then ex-
plained the position which Israel occupied among the na-
tions. As Philemon recalled it afterward, the substance
of what Hosah said was this, that Israel was the chief
vehicle of history, whose God-ordained task was the128
conquest of nature, with a group of righteous men serv-
ing as the divine instrument, these being independent of
nature and standing therefore as an example to all people.
It was necessary to segregate the chosen people lest by
mixing with others they should adulterate and dissipate
their priestly powers. Jews had to be Jews contrary to
all men. They also had to be Jews for all men—by which
he meant teachers and exemplars.
Philemon said: "If I'm circumcised and baptised and
make a sin offering, will I then be a Jew?"
"In the sense in which I used the word. You'll become
Israel. You will not go three days without the waters of
the Torah."
"But you never accept the non-Jew on equal terms."
Jose ben Johanan spoke up impatiently. "How could
we? Israel did not reject the Torah. All other nations
did, through no fault of ours."
"Look at me," said Jose ben Joezer. "I'm poor. All
my life I've studied at the Torah's mouth, having no
time to gather possessions. If we give our lives as exam-
ples, do you ask us to give you our blood?"
"You say the Holy One offered wisdom to all the
peoples of the world—"
"To seventy nations," said Jose ben Johanan.
"Where was this?"
"On the Holy Mount in the wilderness."
"And you tell me my people were there?-and rejected
the Torah?"
"Yes. Only Israel accepted it. That is why we are a
chosen people."
Philemon thought it the most incredible conceit he
had ever heard; but masking his thoughts and emotions
he asked: "If you're the chosen, why did your Holy One
allow you to be dragged off into exile and slavery?"
Several men were eager to answer the question. Hosah
said: "So that our sufferings would be a sacrificial offer-
ing to all other people—and in the hope that our suffer-
ings would move other nations to repentance and bring
them to the only true fountain of living waters. We129
shall suffer yet many times, for we are the chosen in-
strument."
Philemon was aghast. He listened with amazement
while one and then another spoke to him; and of all the
things that they said he afterward remembered these:
that if three men ate together and did not talk about
the Torah it was as though they ate offerings of the
dead....The bastard who knows the Torah is above
the ignorant priest, even the High Priest. . . . The Torah
was revealed in four languages, that of the Roman, the
Arab, the Aramean and the Hebrew.... The Jews had
been a very sinful people and had been punished with
many punishments....
After Philemon left them and stood outside he drew
a deep breath and thought of Epicurus, who had devoted
his life to the freeing of men from superstitions and
fear. Were the beliefs of the Jews nothing more than
preposterous conceits, or were these grotesque excre-
sences nourished by some profound truth? In one moment
he was ready to abide by Reuben's judgment; but in the
next he found some subtle beauty that delighted him.
One day for instance he announced himself at Hosah's
house and Hosah came outside, smiling and looking up
at the sky. "How beautiful," he said, "is the firmament!
How splendid the home of the One who laid the beams
of his chambers in the waters and walked on the wings
of the wind; whose angels are his spirits, whose minis-
ters his flaming fires!-of him who laid the foundations
of the earth, that it may not be removed forever!-who
covered it with the garment of the deep until the waters
stood above the mountains!-who watered the hills
from his chambers until the whole earth was sweetened!"
What magnificent poetry! Philemon was thinking when
Hosah did a strange thing: he came close to Philemon
and touched his arm and then gently clasped it. Look-
ing up at the sky he asked:
y?"
"Do you ever pray!
"No."
"Do you know any prayers?"130
"Grant me that which I deserve, said Apollonius.
Grant that I may become of noble mind, said Socrates.
And Pindar prayed, May I walk in the guileless paths
of life and leave behind me a fair name for my children."
"Those are good, yes. Would you like to hear one
of my favorite prayers?"
"Of course."
Hosah turned to face the temple.
"The Holy One is my shepherd, I shall not want. He
makes me lie down in green pastures, he leads me beside
the still waters, he restores my soul. He guides me in
straight paths for his Name's sake. Yes, though I walk
through the valley of the shadow of death I will fear
no evil, for he is with me. He has anointed my head with
oil and my cup runs over. Surely goodness and mercy
shall follow me all the days of my life and I shall dwell
in His house forever!"
He looked at Philemon. "Did you like it?"
"Yes. We Greeks love beauty."
After another gentle pressure on Philemon's arm, Ho-
sah said, "Hellas loves beauty, Israel loves righteous-
ness," and went back into his house.
Philemon went over to the western hill to sit on a
bench. He still believed that Heraclitus was right when
he said that the world of appearances is one of con-
tinuous change, and Aristotle when he said that forms
are not transcendent but immanent, and Epicurus when
he said that illusions are not of the senses but of judg-
ment, and Zeno when he rejected Plato's belief in innate
ideas. Ah, holy heavens, he had spent years trying to
arrive at some unalterable goodness and was now lost
again in confusions worse than those of his youth! How
could Hosah utter such a beautiful prayer and at the
same time believe in a god who ate, slept, sneezed,
belched, snored, blew a trumpet, was jealous, spiteful,
vindictive....
And then one day in a flash it came to Philemon. A
son of Hellas believed that you could not step in the
same river twice, that all appearances were continuously131
changing; but the son of Israel believed in the unalter-
able, the immutable, the changeless. For the Greek it
was the mutable, the inconstant, the vacillating, the
transient, the ever-changing kaleidoscope. For the Jew it
was the steadfast, the firm, the stable, the permanent,
the deep-rooted equilibrium. To reduce the matter to hu-
man terms, for the one it was the chameleon, for the
other the stereotype. Could there ever be a common
ground, a meeting-place, between the two?
In these weeks one question troubled him above all
others. Was it capriciousness and fickleness in his nature
that had driven the Jew to his doctrine of the immutable
and changeless? Was it a deadly earthbound tendency
to changelessness that had driven the Greek to the light
and fanciful? One had righteousness, the other had rea-
son. The Jew had an amazing faculty for conceits,
parable, metaphor, symbolism—indeed, for all the prod-
ucts of the imagination. But he had cast upon all this
the blight of his Torah-the stultifying routines and
destroying formalisms.
Every afternoon he sat on a bench under a tree and
talked with those whom his smile welcomed or read from
his books. Angela came often, and Judith now and then.
One day Judith came and stood before him in her
simple robe and worn sandals and asked: "Are you busy?"

"No."

then study the quiet mirth in her eyes. She appeared al-
ways to be rather stiff and aloof, but that, he had learned,
was a pose. Certain questions brought the light to her
eyes and the smile to her lips.
"Yes, I'm happy."
"Do you have any reason to be?"

WONDERING IF HIS life was in danger or if Angela had
invented the story to serve some mischief of her own,
Philemon went to Melanie to learn what was happening
in the city. She astonished him by saying, "Of course
your life's in danger. So is mine. There are spies and
assassins everywhere."
"But Reuben protects you, doesn't he?"138
"When half the people in the city carry concealed
daggers how can anyone be safe? How can I know which
of my lovers may be bribed to slay me?"
In an effort to understand the forces behind the gath-
ering struggle, Melanie had been looking into Israel's
recent history. What she had learned amazed her and
it amazed Philemon.
There had been a Joseph, a grandson of one Simon
the Just, who left his inheritance to sons who loved Hel-
lenism and hated one another. Two of the brothers had
been slain when, acting together, they had tried to ambush
and kill Hyrcanus, their half-brother, and the youngest.
Thereupon Hyrcanus fled. Then two ambitious and ruth-
less kings, Antiochus and Philip of Macedon, seized
Egypt and her colonies and divided them; and Joseph's
sons, to be known as the Tobiades, had embraced the
cause of Antiochus, the present King's father, opened
Jerusalem's gates to him, and helped him crush the fol-
lowers of Hyrcanus.
And so the city had come under the rule of a Syrian
king.
Shortly thereafter one Scopas, an Aetolian, placed
himself at the head of mercenaries, roused a revolt,
stormed and captured Jerusalem, parts of which he de-
stroyed. Leaving a garrison in the citadel he marched
against the Syrians and suffered crushing defeat. Jerusa-
lem was torn asunder by two contending factions among
the Jews.
The events in the next year or two, Melanie said, she
had not been able to make out. She picked up the story
when Antiochus marched on Jerusalem, captured it, and
set men to work to rebuild it. He laid siege to the citadel.
And then, with the boldness of a madman, he marched
against the Romans, believing that he could conquer the
world. Overwhelmingly defeated, he sent his own son,
the present King, as a hostage to Rome.
During all these years Israel had been disintegrating in
the violent quarrel between Jews who wished to live by
the Torah and those who wished to live by the ways of139
Greece. On all sides were Israel's enemies. The High
Priest until recently had been one Onias, a devout man
who had kept in touch with Hyrcanus in exile. Besides
the Tobiades, he was openly opposed by three brothers,
Simon, Lysimachus, and Menahem, who called himself
Menelaus. Simon he had banished. The Tobiades he
exiled, thereby arousing the Hellenists to vigorous re-
sistance. Simon had gone to one of the King's generals
and told him that in the temple was great treasure. Then
the King died, and there came to the throne his son, the
present King, who for thirteen years had been a prisoner
in Rome and was consumed with hatred of the Romans.
The moment he ascended the throne the Jews with
Greek sympathies asked him to interfere in the affairs of
Israel. They told him-whether this was true Melanie
did not know that Hyrcanus in exile had fraudulently
accumulated great riches. Upon learning of this, Hyr-
canus killed himself. One Jesus, who called himself Jason,
then bribed Antiochus to throw Onias out and make him
High Priest, and this had been done. For some reason
the Hellenists, among whom, of course, Reuben was a
leader, distrusted Jason and conspired against him. They
had brought Menelaus forth, bribed the King, and now
Menelaus was High Priest.
What a sordid tale of conspiracy, treachery and mur-
der it was!
"And now we're right in the thick of it and can't get
away. Do you know that spies are watching you day and
night?"
"Me!"
"I know the name of one of them—a girl called
Angela."

After all, my life's also in danger."
"All right, I pledge it."140

The next afternoon he was sitting on the bench when
Angela came up and without a word sat by him. He was
reading, and he ignored her until she spoke.
"The sky will fall any day now," she said. "Is there
any place in the world where there's peace? ... That
way, beyond Rome?"
"That's all water."
"That way, beyond Egypt?"
"That's all sand."
She moved close to him and looked into his eyes, and
there was no dimpling or mischief or slyness in her face.
She said: "If we were like other people we'd not be per-
secuted."
"Maybe."
"Hosah is weeping."
"Why?"
"Because the High Priest has taken most of the treas-
ure from the temple."
"What did he take it for?"
"So the King can pay his soldiers, so his soldiers will
march against Egypt."
"To kill people.
"Haven't men always been trying to kill people?" With
something appealingly sad in her face she was looking141
at him. "When fighting comes to our city do you know
what I'll do ?"
"Don't all beautiful women become spies?"
"Will you be a spy?"
"I'll try to live somewhere like a sane man."
"You'll hide somewhere and I'll find you."
"I don't doubt it."
"Philemon, I once asked which side you'll be on when
Ezra the priest fights Epicurus the philosopher. I said
I'd come for your answer."
"I have no answer."
"You'll have to have an answer. Soon."
He looked into her eyes and did not like what he saw
there.
A few days later he was talking to Angela and Judith
when, on looking up, he saw four men coming toward
him. He observed that Angela shrank back and the
thought occurred to him that she was acting a part in a
drama which she had foreseen. He was never to know
whether in this hour she betrayed him. He heard her
gasp, "It's the High Priest!" and then she seemed to be
trying to hide behind Judith.
Philemon would not have recognized the man who was
several paces ahead of his three companions. Priests
when off duty did not wear distinctive dress. If this was
Menelaus he looked like a very ordinary fellow, in a
chiton with double girdle and sleeves, over which was
carelessly draped a cloak. His head was bare. On his feet
were sandals. These details Philemon observed before
Menelaus came up and stopped; and he saw then that the
man was drunk.
What Menelaus did next would not have astonished
Philemon if he had known the man well-or if for that
matter he had been familiar with his reputation as a
lecher. Venery was his profession. In him the warm sen-
suous nature of the Jew was unlessoned and unrestrained.
Emotional, quick-tempered, explosive, violent in passion,
he was a son of the East who had discarded the shackles142
and discipline of the Torah. Behind his back his friends
called this debauched seducer the goat. ..
What Menelaus did was to lurch forward, ogling and
tonguing his lips, and reach out to grasp Judith. Swiftly
she moved away from him, and Angela with her. Phile-
mon stood up. Looking at the three brutal men who had
come with Menelaus, he saw that they were delighted.
"Seize her, my Lord!" one of them cried.
When, weaving a little, his hands outstretched, his
eyes aglow with lickerish eagerness, Menelaus moved
again, intending to possess Judith, she ran to the bench
and stood behind it. Then Angela went to her and the
two girls stood together, their eyes on the High Priest.
Philemon saw that Judith was trembling. When Mene-
laus moved to go behind the bench, Judith did what for
Philemon was an amazing thing: she ran quickly to him
and made frantic efforts to conceal herself within the
folds of his garments.
Rocking on his heels and swaying and looking as
though the organs of his chest had fallen into his ab-
domen, Menelaus glanced round him like one observing
the enemy's position; and at last fixed Philemon with eyes
in which lust mingled with anger. The three men—it
was plain that they were his bodyguard, though on them
no weapons were visible-moved forward a little, their
gestures menacing. Judith had managed partially to cover
herself and now snuggled against Philemon. Angela
stood behind the bench. The guards looked at their mas-
ter and waited. Menelaus stared at Philemon. And Phile-
mon, thinking fast, knew that this was no playful non-
sense. He knew that he and Judith were in danger, for
women were cheap in this world and human life was
cheap.
The High Priest was terribly drunk. When he tried
to speak he seemed unable to, and then moved his lips,
pushing the lower over the upper, thrusting the upper
down over the lower, and all the while drunkenly rolling
his eyes. When at last he found his voice it was that of a143
man who held power over life and consigned his enemies
to death.
"Who are you?" he demanded. The sound of his voice
had a magical effect on his eyes. They opened so wide
that they bulged.
"I'm Philemon, a friend of Reuben."
"Eh?" said Menelaus. He seemed sobered a little.
He glanced at his guards and then up at the sky. Phile-
mon meanwhile managed to whisper to Judith, telling
her to go behind the bench and be prepared to flee when
he signaled. She slunk over and stood by Angela.
"Is she your woman?" asked Menelaus.
Philemon was on the point of saying no when he saw
that Judith was making signs bidding him say yes.
"Can you speak?" asked Menelaus angrily. He rubbed
his red lips over one another. He was not so drunk as
he had been when he first came up. Making a face so
comical that Philemon smiled in spite of himself, the
High Priest of Israel drew himself to his fullest height,
with his abdomen like a fallen paunch and his chest sunk-
en, and spoke as a lord, saying: "Are you a ger-toshab?"
Philemon did not know the meaning of the term. Since
Antioch was the King's city and this creature before him
was the King's lackey, he said: "I'm a citizen of Anti-
och."
"Who isn't?" asked Menelaus scornfully. He thrust an
arm at Judith. "Is that woman your wife?"
"No."
"You say no? Is she betrothed to you?"
Again he saw Judith beseeching him. "She's my be-
trothed."
"You lie!" said Menelaus. He looked round him and
saw that at a distance some curious persons were watch-
ing and listening. He smiled faintly. Under his volup-
tuous grossness he was a crafty fellow. He knew that
Philemon had lied, and his eyes, looking at Philemon,
said that he intended to do something about it.
As for Philemon, he knew that the moment was swift-
ly approaching when he must act. Could he pretend to be144
playful and, by clowning with Menelaus, give Judith a
chance to flee?
Menelaus was behaving queerly. He kept lifting his
chin and making a movement of distress, but Philemon
could not tell whether he was trying to belch, or was
shuddering as the lustful emotions in him settled to
quiet. He peered at the girls and turned to Philemon,
and his eyes popped wide open and seemed glazed. They
stared as though a blow on his skull had fixed them in
changeless amazement. He appeared to be unaware of
what he was doing, but when he spoke it was clear that
his sly mind had been busy.
"You've lied to me, the High Priest of Israel, ap-
pointed by his Majesty the King—”
Philemon knew that the moment had come. He gave a
signal to Judith, and instantly the two girls turned and
ran. In the same moment Philemon flung himself upon
Menelaus, crying in a loud voice: "You're my friend be-
cause you're Reuben's friend and Reuben is my friend!"
He pretended to be overjoyed. He kept crying, "My
friend! Reuben's friend!" The guards were bewildered.
Their first impulse had been to overtake the girls and
drag them before their master, but before they could
leap forward, Philemon had flung himself on Menelaus.
Though drunk, Menelaus knew that he had been out-
witted. He shoved Philemon away from him and cried:
"You idiots, seize this man!" The guards leapt upon
Philemon, their daggers flashing, and bore him to the
earth. Then, waiting, they looked up at their master.
With outraged dignity Menelaus brushed his robe and
stared at Philemon.
"What shall we do with him?" asked the spokesman
for the guards.
"Bind him with chains."
Half-carrying and half-dragging him they took Phile-
mon away. When they had gone with him a hundred
yards or so they stopped and blindfolded and gagged
him, one of them hissing in his ear: "If you try to escape
I'll dig your eyes out!"145
Menelaus sat on the bench and picked up a papyrus
script which Philemon had left there. It was a letter from
Epicurus to Pythocles. Squinting and staring he made out
these words: "The rainbow is caused by light shining
from the sun on to watery atmosphere." Unsteadily he
arose, crying to nobody-for he was alone: "This man
is my friend!" He looked round him, shook his head
sadly like one betrayed, and sank again to the bench.
At some distance two girls, sheltered by a wall,
watched the guards drag Philemon out of sight. "It's for
you he's dying!" said Angela bitterly. "Not for me!"
Judith ran to find her brother. "You must save Phile-
mon!" she cried. "They're going to kill him!"
"Who?" asked Hosah, looking up from his study.
"The High Priest's men!"
"I have no influence with this man."
"Hosah, please! Do something! He did this to save
me!"
"To save you from what?"
"The High Priest !"
"An imposter and a scoundrel," said Hosah quietly.
"The Holy One-blessed be he-will cast him down."
"Hosah!"
"Don't shriek at me," said Hosah. He glanced at his
sister and felt in his bowels a sensation akin to evil.
"Don't interrupt my studies. The whole world is not
equal in worth to one commandment in the Torah."
"Hosah, if you don't help him—”
"Silence!" cried Hosah. "Go to your father's house."

PHILEMON COULD NOT TELL whether he was chained in
a man-made dungeon or in a natural cave. In the pitch-
black of the night that enveloped him he could see noth-146
ing at all but by the darkness and musty odors he knew
that he was in an underground vault. Listening, he could
hear no sound.
The guards had brought him in and bound him with
chains. The chains were made fast to an iron stake that
seemed to be driven into the rock floor of the cavern, and
were bound round and round his legs. There were two
chains, he thought; the ends of both had been carried
away into darkness and made secure, possibly to other
stakes far beyond his reach. "How long are you going to
leave me here?" he had asked the guards in the darkness.
A hand felt down toward him and found the back of his
head and grasped his hair. Another hand played over his
face, determining the position of his eyes, nose, mouth.
Then suddenly this hand smote him a terrific blow across
his teeth. And a voice said: "That'll teach you to keep
your mouth shut." Now another of the men said: "You'll
stay here till your bones rot. Feel around you and you
can find the bones of other men. We're leaving your
hands free so that you can feel them."
How far into the cavern they had brought him he
had been unable to tell; they had borne him, stumbling,
cursing, with one guiding the other two, feeling his way.
After the guards left him Philemon reached out and
found bones, though whether of man or beast he could not
determine. He sniffed them. He made a pile of all the
bones he could reach. For one with a sense of smell so
acute the odors in this cave were a vivid and not un-
pleasant experience. He became interested in trying to
separate and name the odors. Among them he could dis-
tinguish that of distintegrated shale, of rust on iron, of
bones, and of something which he took to be the litter
and droppings of birds or beasts. Animal smells were
unmistakably present but he had heard no sounds. The
air, saturated with all these odors, was that where no
breeze had ever blown; it was thick and heavy-laden
with the tiny dust particles and gossamer and spidersilk
of accumulated centuries. Indeed, Philemon realized af-
ter a while that he had been sneezing.147
Now, he thought, I shall learn how a man dies. He
moved to explore his surroundings and found that his
ankles were snugly bound to the iron stake. He felt over
this stake, over the chain and his feet, over the earth. He
found that he was not able to move his body to the right
or the left without putting pressure on his ankles and
causing pain. He could explore to the length of his arms.
He could move his torso up and down from a prone to
a sitting position. That was all.
Hours later, while bathing his cut lip with saliva, he
felt that night had come, though the gloom around him
could not have deepened. It was something that he sensed
in the earth. Lying back, with hands under his head,
his eyes wide open, he turned his thoughts to death.
Why fear death? asked the disciples of Epicurus. It was
the end of sensation, it was a blessed relief. It seemed
to him that Jews had a morbid attitude toward death;
their holy literature was filled with outbursts of gloom
and despair-with chambers of death, the bitterness of
death, the terrors, dread, sorrows and shadows of death.
What great grief or great fear hung like a sinister over-
cast above their lives? Why was there so much night-
damp on their spirits, such cheerless and somber murk
in their souls? Jesus ben Sirach was proud because he
had never laughed. The interior of his life, like that
of Hosah and so many, was dark, comfortless, and as
melancholy as this cave. For the disciples of Epicurus
life was full of light and merriment, of emotions flowing
like hill-streams, of buoyant uprushings of the spirit.
As for death, Philemon was not afraid of it, but he was
afraid of the ways by which men often forced men to
die-the digging out of eyes with iron or flint chisels;
the cutting off of tongues or the dragging of them out
by their roots; the castrations, scourgings, crucifixions,
flayings-there was no end of the ingenious and horrible
methods with which men tortured men. . . .
The picture of Tantalus everlastingly tormented by
the menace just above his head; of Tityus sprawled
across nine acres with vultures feeding on his bowels;148
1
of Sisyphus chained to an eternity of labor, trying vain-
ly to roll a stone up a hill-these were the imaginings
of men but what kind of men could imagine such things?
What deep wounds of agony and hate and fear did
these things reveal? The soul of a people was to be
found, a philosopher had said, in the myths they made.
In recent times men had gone so far into lunacy or sor-
row as to invent demons-monsters of craftiness and
cruelty to do the work of torture. These demons im-
mersed souls in boiling gold and dragged them out and
hurled them into pools of chilled lead. They abraded
them over fields of short iron nebs and spikes. They
goaded and convulsed and broke them in every fiendish
way that demons could think of-which meant only-
and he shuddered at the thought-in every way that
men could think of. The so-called supernatural world
had become a thing of dreadful evils; it was peopled
with millions of these demons, in form and substance
amazingly various, who spent all their time devising
new and subtler forms of torture. What kind of minds
could create them but minds overthrown by fear?
He felt an impulse to shout but thought it would be best
to keep his mouth shut. It would be a simple and for
many guards a pleasurable matter to seize his tongue
with iron pincers and tear it from his throat. Job asked
the wrong question. He should have asked his god why
he created men who devoted their imaginative faculties
to the cruel, the fantastic, the ludicrous, the horrible. . . .
Would Menelaus leave him here to die? Would he
remember that Philemon had mentioned the name of
Reuben, and ask Reuben about him? It did not seem
likely. Sotted men did not recall from their hours of
deepest drunkeness. Would Judith try to find him? That
was a sweet thought! He closed his eyes and after a
while he slept.
When he woke he thought it was the forenoon of an-
other day. Realizing, then, that he had no evidence to
support this opinion, he was amused, and fell to think-
ing of the different approaches to truth of different phil-149
osophers. Was there an extra-sensory faculty? He had
slept and waked and slept. He had nothing with which
to measure the passing of time, yet he would have wager-
ed his life that it was mid-morning of another day. Later,
he thought it was mid-afternoon when far out in dark-
ness he heard a sound. In a few moments he saw the
light of a torch and a dark form behind the torch. Some-
one was approaching him. The person seemed to be
meandering down the cavern, for twice the light disap-
peared. With the torch held at one side and back, so that
the light could not reveal his face, the person came up,
and without saying a word set something within Phile-
mon's reach. Then with the torch before him he went
away.
When there was silence again Philemon reached out
and took the parcel and by smelling and feeling he learned
that he had a small skin-pouch of water, and a piece of
bread the size of his two hands. Well, if they intended
to appease his hunger and slake his thirst possibly they
did not intend that he should die. Unless-and the
thought made his surface nerves tingle-unless the water
or the bread was poisoned. He sniffed at the water but
could smell only the staleness of Jerusalem's cisterns.
He tasted it. It had the flavor of stench, but many in-
sects and small animals fell into the city's cisterns and
drowned. He turned to the bread and sniffed and tasted
it. Nobody knew what foul hands had mixed and baked
it, or handled it since baking. It was unleavened, and,
being so, as flat and insipid as wood shavings. But it
would keep him alive.
The next day the person came again to bring bread
and water, and this time Philemon was prepared for him.
Not to speak-he did not dare make a sound, lest he lose
his tongue or eyes-but to smell him. He wanted a firm
impression of this person's odor; and so he was sitting
up, balanced on his rump and hands, when the person
bent forward and set the parcel within reach; and in that
moment Philemon drew a deep slow breath through his150
nostrils. He exhaled and breathed again. Then the man
was gone.
Trying to keep a cheerful mind, he spent most of the
third day thinking of himself as a Jewish proselyte. If
he were to become a proselyte to win Judith, curcumci-
sion, baptism, and a sin-offering would not be, after all,
the worst part of it. He would be called upon to de-
nounce family, relatives, friends, customs-for no trace
was to be left of any of his old ways. If his motives were
impure he would belong to the gerim gerurim, but if they
were pure, he would be a ger sedek. The degree of Phile-
mon's righteousness he would leave to philosophers and
holy men. Smiling, he thought: Now I'm a ger toshab
and Jews may practice usury on me. When I become
ger sedek, sharpers won't dare to fleece me!
As for circumcision, Philemon found the thought re-
pugnant. It was a mutilation. Even babies, for whom
it was so much less severe, sometimes died, and adults
quite commonly. In the act of baptism, the pious Jews
believed, the water literally and actually washed away
sins and uncleanness! Sin! But what did they mean by
it? Among other peoples Philemon had found a sense
of guilt, of baseness, of evil-but not of sin, as the Jews
used that word. While he was wondering where they
had got the idea, and turning over in his mind the ma-
terials and concepts of his reading, there was formulated
one clear and luminous thought: This that they call sin
is growing in them because of their separateness!
By the fourth day his ankles were paining him and
his legs were beginning to swell. And he had become
afraid. He had been comforting himself with the thought
that tyrants never fattened those whom they killed-
but now he became aware that he was receiving only
enough nourishment to keep him alive, and so prolong
the agony of his dying. On this day the mysterious one
came in like the very image of stealth, set the skin of
water and piece of bread within reach, and went softly
away. His piece of unleavened bread was as tasteless
as carob husks and as hard as barley grains. The odor151
of the water sickened him. He was losing flesh and be-
coming weaker, though he faithfully ate every crumb
and slept as many hours as he could. He tried to force
his thoughts to dwell on cheerful or humorous or philo-
sophic matters, but the precepts of Epicurus were be-
coming sophistries and by what he took to be the morning
of the fifth day he felt over his legs and soberly con-
sidered his situation. Again-though he knew his efforts
would be vain-he tried to loosen the mass of coiled iron
on his ankles. He crawled forward the length of his
body, his hands feeling along the two chains as far as
he could reach. As far as he could reach all around him
he explored the rock floor of the cavern for something
to use as a weapon-or for something with which to kill
himself if his pain became too great. He thought of
seizing the mysterious one who brought food and water
and trying to drag him down and subdue him; but this
notion was so foolish that he wondered if he was going
mad. No doubt the man was armed with daggers. Did
the lecherous Menelaus intend to starve him to death
little by little, until his agony would be intolerable? Or
had everyone forgotten him but this one who came daily,
this one who came like a beast chained to a task, like
an ox on a threshing-floor!
By the sixth day—or seventh or whatever it was—his
pain had become so intense that he thought it best to
speak. He might lose his tongue or his eyes or both; but
he knew as a physician that his strength was rapidly eb-
bing, that in a few more days he would be unable to
make an effort toward freedom. There were sharp pains
under his lower ribs on either side. There was a pain,
even sharper, at about the third rib in his back. He
would speak....
But when he saw the first sign of torchlight he was
frantic, for he had formulated no questions. What would
he ask this person? His name?-who had sent him
here?-how long this torture was to last? Or should
he ask him to take a message to a friend? Could the
man be bribed? Could he be frightened?152
The person came up softly, hiding the light, as was his
habit, so that Philemon could not tell whether the figure
was man or woman, still less make out its face. And
when, desperately forcing himself to the issue, Philemon
spoke he was appalled. Upon the silence of a week his
voice crashed like avalanches of tumbling sound. He
did not himself understand his own words. Even while
waiting, astounded and aghast, he thought the cavern
rang from end to end with strange echoes. He had said,
"Tell me who is your master who sends you here." After
recovering from his fright and amazement he said, "Tell
me.... "But the person had gone away. The light had
vanished.
Philemon reached out to find the bread and water.
The skin-pouch was of the size of the first, the second,
the third....it held about a pint. The piece of bread
was neither larger nor smaller. About half as thick as
his little finger and as large as his two palms spread
open, it was not enough, he now realized, to sustain the
life in a small child.
Alarmed, fully conscious of his danger, convinced that
he would die here if he could not with his own efforts
or cunning free himself, he tugged madly at the chains
and then shrugged and fell back. Vainly, angrily, with
hot tears falling to his cheeks, he searched round him
for a weapon. He thought: I must learn to talk nor-
mally....God, I must have shouted!—or did I actually
speak at all? Aloud he said: "Tell me your name." In
the stone walls the words came back to him like shat-
tered bronze bells. He thought his next words were little
more than a whisper: "Does Menelaus send you here?"
He understood the words. He began to talk, not only
to accustom himself to his voice, and his voice to its
prison, but to give himself courage and strength. "Am
I to die here," he asked, "like a dog thrown into a pit?...
Death I can take manfully: it is only the end of sen-
sation, the end of worry and struggle and pain. Death
I can take cheerfully if it doesn't come like a harlot with
a bellyful of worms! . . . You dirty, ill-smelling, base153
and lecherous.
• •
." His voice was under control now.
It echoed in the walls but the echoes did not confuse him
except for a moment when he ceased speaking.
"I'm a wealthy man," he said. "I'll give you a great
treasure to take a message to a friend. I'll give you a
thousand golden darics. . . . I'll give you a thousand silver
shekels... I'll give you . . . I'll give you. ." Certainly

Over and over for hours Philemon formulated phrases
and tried to weigh the appeal of each proposal. Toward
morning he slept. He woke covered with sweat and knew
that it was the sweat of exhaustion. He now concluded
that he would not be in possession of his senses more
than another day or two; and while munching what was
left of his bread he lay with arms outstretched and tried
to relax. He thought: Thinking costs energy; I must not
think but sleep... sleep. But long before noon he sat
up and looked in the direction whence the light would
come. He waited, staring, and when at last it appeared
he was so exhausted, anxious and terrified that he began
to shake all over. He tried to speak. As the noiseless
figure approached he felt panic, for he could not be sure
which of his plans he had decided to act on-Menelaus?
Reuben?-Melanie?—Judith? He saw the figure set
the water and bread within reach. In the same dim way
he saw the figure straighten and turn to go. Then all
the accumulated anguish of the days of darkness and
waiting, all the loneliness and pain were borne down upon
-154
him; and in a voice that was less that of man than of
beast chained in a dungeon he cried:
...
"Don't go!" And then, almost at once: "Go to the
one called Melanie.... tell her where to find me-tell
her . . . she will give you treasure—all that you ask for.
Send her " The figure had turned away but it paused.
It was waiting and listening. In a voice a little less
shaken he said: "Reuben, the friend of the High Priest―
or Paul, the brother of Hosah and Judith—or An-
gela....Tell Melanie to give you a hundred shekels.
Tell her...." The figure still waited. Why? he wildly
asked himself. Was the bribe too small? "Tell Melanie
to give you a hundred darics.... Please-please send
her soon!...”
The person went away. Philemon lay back, his blood
racing and his body moist. Why, he wondered, did a per-
son fight so to live? Above him, people were dying; every-
where in the world people were helpless with loathsome
disease, everywhere they cried out all night from pain.
He could die here. Here there was peace. Did not Job
say, My soul is weary of my life? Did he not say to his
god, Show me wherefore you contend with me? And if
he got out of here alive what would it be for but to be
the slave again of a blind passion?
Would the man go to Melanie? Would she come?
After further thought he believed that he would die
here, and he strove to quiet his wild clamorous hunger
to live. He said, There is peace here. It is good to die.
He said, In the world around me, men fight against men,
slaying them for no reason but superstition, fear, or
lust for power. All around me is a world of creatures
tormenting themselves with fantastic and dreadful no-
tions of gods and demons. Up there are the fevers and
insanities called life, here is the mausoleum of the deep
and blessed death.
"It is good to die," he said, and wondered whether
with an effort of will he could hasten his end. He would
relax. He would surrender to that vast beneficent peace
wherein there were no vulgar clamorings of the human155
ego, no longer its shoddy pretensions, no more of its
pathetic strivings and sorrowings and heartbreak . . .
Hours later a person entered this cavern. A light
flashed and went out. Then for a few moments he could
hear the person groping toward him and at last an
anxious voice which said in a loud whisper:
"Philemon?"
"Here!" he tried to shout but made no sound. It was
Melanie! With all his strength he tried to shout, to cry
her name, to bless her, but not even a whisper came
from his lips. With feeble hands he grasped his skull and
shook it; with wild unreason he shoved a finger into his
mouth and against his throat; he tried to sob, to weep,
to gurgle, to make any kind of sound at all....
"Philemon?"
Ah, great God, the snapping of the tension, the awful
joy, the beautiful wonder of it had destroyed his voice!
Still unable to speak, even to croak, he seized some old
crumbling bones and smote them one on another....
"Philemon ?"
She was coming toward him, flashing the light now
and then; and with every means he could think of he was
trying to make sounds. He strove to jangle the chains.
He slapped his cheeks. And at last with an effort that
left him sick and fainting he was able to croak:
"Here!"
She came up and flashed the light in his face. "Great
Zeus!" she gasped, horrified. He was weeping but he did
not know it until, trying to speak, he babbled. "Poor
darling!" she said. She knelt and explored round her and
propped her torch. She then grasped the chains and fol-

listen, could hear her fumbling with the chains. She re-
turned and began to unwind the chains from his legs,
but paused, and her fingers began to feel over his flesh.
She cried out with horror.
"Philemon-”
"Yes, I know!"
"I don't think you can walk-can you? I must go now.156
I've risked my life-"
"I know it. God bless you!"
"But you can't walk!"
"I can crawl out."
"Do you know what has been happening in this city?
But of course you don't!"
"What?"
"Is there no pity in men? Why are they born?"
"What has happened?"
"Jason came back."
"Where is Menelaus?"
"Who knows?"

"I'm weak."
"Ah yes, forgive me! I'd kiss you, darling, but you

"I know."

"Yes."
"Come to my place. I'll hide you." She took the torch

her to come back and help him. It seemed to him to be
a long way out. It seemed to him that for an hour he
crawled, or, drawing his knees up, tried to roll over and
over like a stone. He was bruised and bleeding and ex-
hausted when at last he saw with blind eyes a paler gloom
and what he took to be a star. He was looking at Je-
rusalem's night-sky. "How beautiful it is!" he thought,
lying on his back to rest. "Though I walk through the
valley of the shadow of death I will fear no evil!" A
faint breeze was moving over him. The air that he now
breathed seemed to have been thrice cleansed and puri-
fied.
In miserable plight, swollen, stinking, half-dead, he
lay at the cavern entrance, thinking that if he was quiet
a little while he might recover a part of his strength. Man
that is born of woman!-for all his days are sorrows, and
his travail grief; yes, his heart takes no rest in the night!
He was still thinking of those words, among his favor-
ites, when he fainted or dozed off; and when he came to,
rousing himself with a feeling of dismay, there was no
star above him. The sky was that of Jerusalem in early
morning, and a bird was singing. Away from him, up a158
hill, and by a house which was unfamiliar, he saw a
woman, and he thought the woman was Melanie. But
the woman was not Melanie.

JUDITH WAS SUCH A HORNET in her brother's quiet,
stinging him with reproaches, giving him no rest, that
Hosah was forced at last to give some thought to the
matter. It was no news to him that Judith was infatuated
with this outlander. He asked her if she was and she
said no, but color suffused her cheeks. She said that
Philemon wanted to be one of them. Hosah did not be-
lieve that but the words that came up from his heart he
did not utter. They were bloated with sin, like gluttons
with food,
For Hosah, the marriage of pious Jews with other
people was shameful and wicked; no casuist, no matter
how subtle, could dodge that certainty. To be ceremoni-
ally clean a Jew must be sexually undefiled. But Judith,
the persistent wench, had learned-Hosah could not
imagine from whom-that some verses in the holy books
welcomed the heathen; and one morning, though he cried
out that he was too busy to see her, she marched in and
faced him.
"I'm busy," he cried again. "Will you go away?"
"No."
Hosah shrugged. In times of travail and heartbreak,
when schisms were rife and customs were challenged,
and the core and navel of the world threatened to disin-
tegrate and crumble, women became more difficult to
manage than the blind sheep.
"Then speak!" he said.
"It says in our holy books--this is what it says, that159
the proselytes will join themselves to us and to the house
of Jacob!"
"It is written," said Hosah patiently, "that the time

But that time is not yet."

"It is answered, Who can dwell as a guest beside the

to make them rejoice."

fire." Judith was a hot soul. "Go away now," he said.

The next day the sky fell and the world here was en-
gulfed by horrors. The struggle that some had foreseen
had come. Jason, the former High Priest, had leapt out
of exile, and with him had come hundreds of armed
men, who had fallen as a horde of assassins upon the
city. Menelaus had fled into the citadel. Dead men lay
everywhere in the streets. Worse than the outpouring of
passions or the cries of the dying was the confusion, the
darkening, the scrambled values and purposes. Had the
sanctuary been desecrated, the altar defiled? On whose
side was the Holy One? Unable in many instances to dis-
tinguish between enemy and friend, the pious Jews did
not dare to leave their houses, even to go to their privies.
Jason's murderers, many of them drunk, prowled in
every crooked lane and pathway, peering into houses and162
courts, mounting to roofs, searching, seizing, looting.
But women they did not molest. Judith, sometimes with
Angela, usually alone, moved freely through the city in
the daylight hours, trying to learn what had been done
with Philemon. It was her brother who told her, the
Saul who called himself Paul.
She found him one day on the eastern hill, staring
away toward Alexandria. He seemed very weary and sad.
"Why are you here?" he asked. Paul, unlike Hosah,
looked full upon the beauty of his sisters.

"In your father's house."

"My name is Paul."

That was in the afternoon of Philemon's fifth day in
the cavern.
Before daylight of the seventh day, while her parents
still slept, Judith softly left the hut that was their home
and slipped away. The hillside which Paul had indicated
lay west of and between the temple mount and the old
city of David, and downward to the vale of the cheese-
mongers. Parts of it were terraced, and on the terraces
were mud huts and tiny vineyards. Parts of it were too
stony for cultivation; and here, as over this whole area,
and most of southern Israel, were many caves, some
being little more than recesses, some opening into the
limestone at considerable depth.
Judith knew the mouth of the largest cave on this hill-
side. Hiding behind an olive tree she waited and watched.
After a few minutes there was a sound above her, and,
on looking up, she saw a figure approaching. She thought
at once that she recognized Melanie. With amazement
she saw this person go to the entrance and stand there,
looking in; saw the figure bend forward and peer, like
one trying to distinguish form from shadow; saw the
figure draw its cloak snugly about it and enter.166
Feeling faint with shame and jealousy, Judith thought:
So she has been seeing him all the time! . . . But if she
had been seeing him, why had she left him there in
chains? . . . The sky overhead was filling with the light
of morning when the figure came out and went up the
hill and disappeared. Knowing that she ought to return
to her father, yet drawn by fascination stronger than
will or duty, Judith left the shelter of the tree and
went toward the cave, moving slowly, with her flesh.
tingling and her ears expecting a challenge. Below in
the vale was the movement of men with their goats.
Uphill from the cave, and at a little distance from its
mouth, was a small house, of mudbricks, with a dirt
roof, and a goatpen behind it fenced with brambles.
This hut was almost in the direct line of Judith's advance.
She knew the people who lived there, a pious Jew and
his wife and their small child. He was a violent and head-
strong man who thought his people ought to rise and
throw off the burden of the Syrian tyrants. Judith had
heard that he belonged to a group of conspirators . . .
She came slowly down the path, feeling sand between
her toes, feeling the cool dust, breathing the morning
air in which was the odor of grapes, and all the while
glancing anxiously round her for signs of the enemy.
When abreast of the hut she turned to look at it and was
so appalled that she screamed. She lifted both arms to
hide her face. Then, too weak to stand, she sank to the
earth and began to sob. But Judith was a high-spirited
girl with extraordinary courage, and so at once she felt
angry because she had cried out and been afraid. "I'm a
coward!" she whispered, trying to still her sobs. "What
a fool I am!" And her sobbing ceased. She sat very still
for a few moments, recovering her presence of mind;
and when at last she arose, strengthened, and with her
courage in her hands, she turned and looked at the hor-
ror on the wall.
A dead mother was hanging there. Her head had
been forced grotesquely up and back because the mur-
derers had hung her on the doorpost by thrusting a167
shaft of wood into her throat under her jaw. Hanging
from her neck and suspended from a piece of rope was
her child. The killers must have hurled it against the
wall; its head, it seemed to Judith, was badly crushed,
and blood from it had run down over the mother's gar-
ment. Of the father, there was no sign.
How long Judith looked at this awful picture, she
could never have said. When brought at last to herself
she heard a voice calling to her; and when she looked
toward the cavern she saw a man, or a figure on hands

THEY MET FACE TO FACE, two women in whom lived the

"WHERE HAVE YOU BEEN?" asked Jorah sternly when

Turning away, Judith thought to put the matter be-

she must return to the safety of the cheese-valley before

When Judith left the tavern her arms were so bur-
dened with odorous things that she had to walk with
care. She expected to be overtaken and robbed. Such a
houseful of evil men, of untidy and unwashed women,
of profane cries and obscene jests she had heard of but
never seen; and as she walked away swiftly she kept
looking back. When at last she dared to pause she sat
and rested her aching arms. She was trembling from
head to foot, and only now realized with shock and hor-
ror how bold she had been, how reckless, to go to such
a place at nightfall. If her father knew what she had
done he would sink in dismay to the mat of his bed and
never rise again!
In spite of her stubborn and righteous indifference to
the food in her arms the smells flooded her mouth with
the waters of appetite. Among the parcels was a jug of
wine. Most of the food was unfamiliar to her; all her
life she had lived on a simple fare of bread, milk, cheese,
a few vegetables and fruit, with meat very rarely. Here
on her lap were hot sausages highly seasoned with pep-
pers; omelettes wrapped in fig leaves; hot spiced pud-
dings made of many fruits and seasonings; honey-cakes
and preserves and pickles and . . . She drew a sigh that
came up from the very heart of her being. Why was it
wicked to eat rich foods and drink fragrant wines!
She arose, carefully arranging her parcels, and crossed
the vale and climbed the hill. As she walked she breathed
protective words from the holy writings, and drew in
deep breaths mixed with the smells of hot juices and
spices. She prayed as she climbed, inaudibly, her lips mov-
ing like Hannah's but her words reaching only the Lord.
And then, quite unknown to herself, she slipped from
prayer to these words: "The names of the proselytes,182
said the Holy One, are as dear to me as the wine of li-

less and some things have more meaning for us. One

was walking in darkness and there was no light any-

JUDITH CAME THE NEXT DAY to bring milk and cheese
but she would not tarry to talk with him. Several days
passed, and Philemon was walking, though not without
limping, when the King's army came. Then for two or
three days there was only violence and terror-only
looting and raping and murder, while the mercenaries of186
Antiochus overwhelmed the city. Hiding in his cave Phile-
mon saw nothing of what happened; but afterward he
pieced the story together and learned that during the
reign of terror these things took place.
First, Jason and his men were slain or driven from
the city, and Menelaus, coming out of the citadel, served
as guide to the King. He took Antiochus into the temple,
where the King seized all the treasure of gold and sil-
ver-the altar, candlesticks, table of the Presence, cups
and other sacred vessels-which had been cherished for
generations. Worst sacrilege of all, he then invaded the
Holy of Holies, which was never entered save by the
High Priest on the day of atonement. There, the King
afterward said, he found a figure of Moses riding an ass.
Whether this was calumny or truth the people had no
way of knowing.
Having plundered the temple, going so far as to scale
gilt off the walls, the King sent the treasure to Antioch
under heavy guard, and placed Menelaus in absolute
power in Jerusalem, assisted by Syrian officials and sup-
ported by Syrian troops. Then, to the amazement of
everyone, including non-Jews, he abolished Israel's wor-
ship, making observance of the laws of circumcision and
the sabbath punishable with death. As though such in-
solence were not enough, he decreed that heathen altars
would be built in all the cities of Judea, and that an
altar to Jupiter would be erected on the altar of burnt-
offerings in the temple court. Those who opposed Mene-
laus in carrying out these orders, including priests, were
to be slain or sold into slavery, the choice resting with
the High Priest. Before leaving for his capital city, An-
tiochus contrived one last insolence. He said that Jeru-
salem would be known hereafter as Antiochus Hieroso-
lyma.
And so matters stood on the morning of Philemon's
eighth day in the cave.
Though now able with the aid of a stick to limp to
the tavern and find lodgings, Philemon remained in the
cave, hoping that Judith would come again. Just after187
dark of this day she did come again, her face looking
bloodless and sick. She had seen soldiers beating old men
to the earth and scourging them; she had heard the
screams of women and children, and had seen bodies
hanging from the walls. She did not weep when telling
him about these things but she looked like one who had
wept for days.
"Are your parents safe?"
"They're alive."
"And Angela?”
"Why do you worry about her? She is not on our
side." Then, looking at him with deep sad eyes, Judith
added: "Do you know that this is the holy sabbath day,
yet we dare not light the lamps?"
"When children are being torn from mothers and their
fathers are being sent like cattle to the slave-merchants,
is the sabbath day important?"
"Oh, you don't understand! You never will!"
"Maybe not. But in comparison to a man with a Syrian
boot in his guts—'
""
"Don't say it! You're impious."
"All right, you're stupid."
"I hate you!" she cried, and a moment later was gone.
Anger burned in Philemon all that night. Could any-
thing, he asked himself, be more fantastically insane than
this pious concern for rules and rules when terrors were
loosed on the city, and brothers raised swords against
brothers? The sabbath day! But Philemon had not
sensed the depth of the pious Jew's feeling for this day,
though the time was to come-and he would stand ap-
palled before it when he would know.
After Judith left he wondered what he ought to do.
Deciding the next morning to leave his hiding-place and
take lodgings in Simon's tavern, he crossed to the west-
ern hill, there bathed and shaved, put on fresh garments,
took note of some letters of credit that had come for
him, and considered his problem. Whether the hand of
Menelaus was still raised against him, he did not know.
And what was Reuben doing now?188
To Simon he said: "Have you seen Angela?"
"Not for many days."
"How many?"
Simon counted on his fat fingers. "Six days."
"Did she ask about me?"
"Ah yes, my friend! All the women ask about Phile-
mon."
"Are you allowed to leave the city?"
"Let's not talk about such things. If it's a succulent
joint you want, or goose eggs with cheese and oil and
coriander, or karuke-ah, my karuke and my kandaulos,
there is none like them! But these these troubles, they
are not mine."
"Very well, Simon."
"You are angry, no?"
"No. Next time Angela comes tell her I live here. Tell
her I want to see her."
"Ah, now!" cried Simon, and his face was sad. "Who
is Angela?" In a whisper he added: "A spy maybe? I
don't talk to spies. I don't know Angela.'

"You don't have to say anything except I live here."
"Philemon lives here. Doesn't that sound like a mes-

about them. I can say, Philemon likes my karuke. That

"All right, say that."

"Yes, I know."

Whispering, Simon asked: "Don't you think Angela

Philemon looked into Simon's eyes. "Oh? So it's that
