For several days Philemon spent most of his time eat-
ing and sleeping and recovering his strength. He applied
hot baths and poultices to his feet and lower legs, draw-189
ing out the pain and swelling; he read Epicurus; he spent
much time thinking of Judith; and he hoped that Angela
would come, so that he might learn from her what Judith
and Hosah were doing.
When she did not come he resolved to cross to the
eastern hill, though he knew that it would be at the risk
of his life. Menelaus and his assassins were there. But
Philemon disguised himself, putting a chlamys over his
chiton and drawing it up over his head from behind so
that he would partially conceal his face. Because his feet
were tender it was easy to dissemble the walk of an elder-
ly man, and this he did, assisting himself with a cane
and proceeding in leisurely fashion to the palestra
grounds. From there he went toward the temple mount.
At the temple court his attention was caught by the labor
of many men and by a great hue and cry. They were
building an altar to Jupiter upon the altar of the Name,
and a throng of Jews stood in the outer court and watched
them, silent and weeping.
The temple altar, of enormous size and of massive
unhewn stones, stood in the open with no roof above it.
On it a fire had burned day and night but there was no
fire now. From it daily had risen the heavy odors of burn-
ing carcasses, mingled with the fumes of incense. Now
Syrian laborers stood upon it and voided their bladders
and laughed. And to outrage the Jews watching them
they not only emptied their bladders and made obscene
gestures. They flung taunts.
"Sons of Jacob!" they yelled. "Look! Here's another
rainbow for you!"
"Come over, and we'll squirt incense in your face!"
A man with a powerful voice now addressed the multi-
tude of Jews. "You pig-haters! We're going to roast a
pig right here on your altar!" Other workmen took up
the taunt.
Then, it seemed to Philemon, a few of the men were
laying a plan before their overseer. They were talking
and gesticulating, and presently one broke away and ran190
down the altar steps and vanished; and other men yelled
to the Jews: "We're going to roast a pig!"
Though no Jew lifted a weapon or made a move to
advance, Philemon sensed the deep heartbeat of fury in
this multitude. He had been standing with a group of
non-Jews. Now leaving them he went toward the Jews,
walking alone. The distance was a hundred feet and
he had traversed a part of it when he was stopped by
insane shouting. In the front line of the Jews he now
recognized Jorah and his wife, and Judith and Hosah,
who were standing close by them. Turning to the altar,
Philemon saw men dancing with frenzied glee; and while
he was looking at them he heard the frightened squealing
of a pig. The thought came to him that Antiochus must
have brought pigs to the city; and if that was so, he had
foreseen this desecration and had planned the blasphemies
which Philemon was now witnessing. The pig, so small
that two men carried it, each grasping it by a leg, was
borne toward the altar and up the steps. Philemon now
turned to look at the Jews and was horrified to observe
that they were surging forward, moving like deep water
across a beach. None of them flashed a weapon, none
made a sound. The squealing of the pig had ceased.
Clouds of smoke rose from the altar. Men there held
aloft bloody and dripping pieces of swine, calling on the
sons of Israel to come forward and watch their Holy
One eat pigflesh. In the faces of the Jews Philemon saw
such frenzy and passion that he was sickened. The multi-
tude was still moving forward, and Philemon joined it
and moved with it, his emotions so aroused and his
thoughts so scattered that he had little sense of what
he was doing. He tried to keep an eye on Judith and
he pushed toward her. Then something happened that
went through and through him with shock and horror.
The Jews had pressed forward until they verged on
the altar. Now some armed men came rushing toward
them, and one, running beyond his comrades, drew a
dagger and flashed it in the sunlight. A moment later
he came to the Jew who happened to be farthest ad-191
vanced and buried the dagger in the dimple just above
the man's collarbone. Philemon saw the Jew standing
there with the dagger haft under his chin. The man who
stood next behind him was Jorah. With an unearthly
scream he lifted both hands and shook them, clenched,
at the sky, his features, usually so mind and gentle, dis-
torted beyond recognition. Philemon looked to see where
Judith was, and then a half-dozen men fell upon Jorah.
Philemon was conscious next of Jorah's wife, screaming,
and wildly struggling to reach her husband, and then of
Judith's crying and of her face, ugly with passion. Swiftly
then he moved, thrusting his way toward Judith and
seizing her and shoving her back and still back out of
danger. It was all such a nightmare of shrieking and
horror, and his own emotions were beating so like ham-
mers in his breast and skull, that Philemon moved with
blurred senses, but kept his grasp on Judith, shoving her
back and back.
Judith fainted or flung herself down to pray. For a
few moments Philemon stood guard over her; where-
upon, observing that there was desperate fighting up
front, and hoping to find Hosah and his mother and
bring them to safety, he pushed forward until he could
see what was taking place. He saw Jorah lying dead in
his blood, and Hosah kneeling by him, wailing; and
then Rachel in the hands of armed men, fighting like a
beast as she was dragged away. Other men were dead
or dying. Other wives were fighting to reach their hus-
bands, or were bowed before them, weeping. Philemon
now swept Hosah up into his arms and carried him away,
taking him to Judith and setting him by her and telling
him to remain there. He looked over the scene, seeing
the smoke from the altar and men dancing wildly on
the steps; seeing the flash of weapons; hearing the cries
of gloating and triumph, the commands, the jeers and
screams; smelling the burning flesh of pig and the heat
of battle, smelling the odor of blood; feeling along
every nerve in his body the horror of it, the obscene ug-
liness of it...192
Then he knelt, saying to Judith: "We must go."
In a voice so quiet that it astonished him she asked:
"Where is our father?"
"Dead."
"Where is our mother?"
"Dead."
"Wait a moment," said Judith. She was kneeling, and
he felt at once that she was making a vow. With her
hair tumbled over her she put her forehead to the earth,
and Philemon wondered what terrible words she was
saying. When she arose, white and still, and looked at
him with rational eyes, the thought occurred to him that
the child in her had died, the woman had been born.
"We'll go now," she said. "Hosah."
Philemon thought Hosah had fainted. But Hosah now
arose, and in a voice as calm as his sister's he said:
'Among us it is written that the son must bury his father.
I must go find him."

"Shall I go with you?"
"No."
Hosah went away to find his parents and Philemon
stood by Judith and waited. He looked at her white
face and asked: "Were you making a vow?"
"Yes."
"I feel like making one too. I feel like swearing by
all that is good in the world that till the day of my death
I'll fight for the right of every man--Greek or Roman,
Egyptian or Jew-to believe as he will and worship as
he must. I will fight against this brutal degradation of

Judith moved over and clasped his arm. "Those are
brave words," she said. "Maybe they are the lonely
light you saw in the darkness."193

IN JERUSALEM the next twelve months were a year of
terror. Most of the priests deserted the temple, and a
few, of whom for various reasons Menelaus was afraid,
were slain or sold as slaves. Most of the pious Jews fled
to the country or to outlying villages but a few remained
in the city and hid in caves. Among these were Hosah
and his family, Judith and Amiel. They went into a
gloomy ill-smelling cavern over by the northwest wall,
and there they slept in the only garments they had, on
the stone floor, and lived on little besides goat-milk and
barley broth. For weeks Judith was so sunk in brooding
and obsession that she would barely speak to Philemon,
but Hosah let him come into the cavern and share their
fellowship; and in later weeks and months he took Phile-
mon with him to visit the sick, the dying, the insane,
though Hosah had little use for physicians.
During the first part of this year Hosah seemed un-
able to dwell on any thought but the death of his father
and his own filial duties. All pious Jews wished to die
with these words on their lips: "Hear, O Israel, the
Lord our God, the Lord is One." If too weak to utter
so many words the dying man said, "The Lord is One."
If these overtaxed his strength he gasped the word One,
and kept repeating it if he was able to. If he was not
able to speak at all and had a friend near, the friend
uttered the words for him. Whether his father had
spoken the words or had been stricken suddenly and
mortally Hosah did not know, and in an effort to make up
whatever loss there might have been he not only rent
his garments and kindled a tiny light far back in the
cave, which would burn for thirty days, but said over194
and over those words which his father would have wished
to say.
There were other holy words which devout Jews spoke
in preparation for death, and these Hosah also repeated
endlessly. "Bless the Lord, O my soul, and all that is
within me, bless his holy Name! Bless the Lord, O my
soul..." Or he said: "I acknowledge unto you, O Lord,
the holy Name, and the holy One of my fathers, that
both my cure and my death are in your hands! May my
death be an atonement for all the sins and iniquities
and transgressions of which I have been guilty against
you! Father of the fatherless and Judge of the widow,
protect me and mine!" He repeated, morning, noon and
night, until the words seemed to have more life than
Hosah himself: "Praised be you, O Judge of truth!"
The other adults in the cave also uttered words of heal-
ing, protection and faith from the moment of rising until
they slept again.
Sometimes Philemon thought he would go mad in this
oppressive atmosphere of piety.
Hosah had not found his father's body; it had been
taken away by the murderers. This loss was for Hosah a
dreadful shock. "Peace be upon him !" he said. Others also
invoked peace upon the name of Jorah.
“A devout man who feared the Holy One-'
"Noble-"
"Wise-"
"Righteous-"
"Kind-"
"Let peace be upon him as a blessing forevermore!"
The son of a deceased father was expected to say the
kaddish for him every morning and evening in the synago-
gue for a year, but Hosah did not dare to approach the
synagogue. He did not dare to go to the temple. But in
every possible way he took up his filial duties. And when
not busy uttering holy words or studying the Torah he
counseled with those who came under cover of darkness
to bring their problems to him. There was Enoch.
Enoch had a feeling which he could not throw off that195
death was approaching him. Some Jews believed that an
angel of death came to one chosen for death, with his
name written on the summons. The person might escape
death by changing his name and outwitting the angel.
Enoch wanted to change his name. He came at a moment
when Philemon was sitting with Hosah, and Philemon
heard what was said. Enoch came in, white and trembling,
and gasped out, "I'm dying! Our Lord is One! I'm
dying!"
"Cease that muttering!" cried Hosah.
Enoch fell to his knees, his hands supplicating, his suf-
fering face begging Hosah to understand his terror. "I'm
dying!" he said.
"Get up!" said Hosah. "You coward, you're not
dying."
"Where is Judith?" asked Enoch, turning his stricken
face to Philemon.
"I don't know," Philemon said.
"Have you married her?"
"No."
Turning back to Hosah and still kneeling, his long fing-
ers clasping one another convulsively, Enoch cried: "Give
me another name! Call me Samuel! Call me Benjamin!"
In his office of physician Philemon had been studying
the effects of the crisis on these people whom he saw daily.
Hosah, so long absorbed by study, had risen to leader-
ship, and to a quality of courage that was all the more
impressive for being so gentle. As for Judith, Philemon
was afraid that the shock had unseated her mind. She
looked at him with eyes that did not see him; or, when not
sitting in the cavern brooding, she disappeared into the
city, and sometimes was gone for two or three days. She
would not tell him where she went or whom she had seen.
And there was Zoma, the father of Enoch, a garrulous
man with shallow convictions, and piety that had seemed
to be little more than sham. He came one day, exclaiming
and rubbing his hands. He had been talking with Jose ben
Joezer and was not at all convinced by what Jose had
said; for if it was not true that the Holy One had been196
temporarily overthrown by Zeus, why was the temple ab-
andoned and the altar desecrated?
"Because," said Hosah, "Israel is a wicked son. The
Holy One is angry and will not raise a miracle to protect
him."
"He's been defeated by Zeus."
"No!"
"By Jupiter?"
"There is no Zeus, there is no Jupiter. Go back to your
house."
Zoma looked at Philemon and saw that he was smiling.
Philemon, in fact, could not restrain a smile when reflect-
ing on the childlike belief of some people that the gods
warred against each other. If Yahweh, the mightiest of
the gods, allowed his chosen ones, his elected children, to
be plundered and slain and persecuted with all manner of
evil, it meant for poor confused Zoma that he had been
defeated, for Hosah that he was punishing his wicked
children because they lived in sin.
Their only sin, so far as Philemon could make out, was
neglect of some of the innumerable rules. Was sin actually
so simple a thing as failure to kindle the sabbath light?
What kind of god, Philemon wanted to ask, could punish
his children with degradation, enslavement and death be-
cause they failed to observe laws which had nothing to do
with the moral life? He had once said to Hosah, "If he
protects those who obey his commandments, why are your
parents dead?-for surely there was no wickedness in
them." Hosah had been offended.
For Philemon Hosah was a pathetic figure but he ad-
mired him. He stood in awe of the man's courage. Hosah
not only continued to observe the sabbath; he went to the
homes of friends to circumcise the newborn-and for both
of these the penalty was death.
At first Hosah had refused to let Philemon go with
him, even to minister to the sick and dying; but after a
while, as his own formulas failed him, and Philemon's
taunts disturbed his quiet, he took him along. They went
only at night and usually only in the darkest nights. Phile-197
mon had a few medicines and instruments which he carried
in a pouch.
They went one night-or one morning really, for it
was the third watch-to a cave in which, Hosah said, a
man was suffering the most horrible agony. Philemon
knelt to feel over the man's body and there came to him
the words of Hippocrates: "A swelling in the hypochon-
drium that is hard and painful is very bad, provided it
occupies the whole hypochondrium; but if it be on either
side, it is less dangerous when on the left." The pain and
swelling in this man filled the whole lower chamber.
After examining the man Philemon arose and stepped
back, and in a low voice said to Hosah: "There's nothing
a physician can do for him. These cases always die." He
asked for a light, and when it was brought he looked at
the man's face and saw in it all the signs of approaching
death which Hippocrates had named-the sunken temp-
les, hollow eyes, cold ears, parched tense skin, livid eye-
lids and open loose mouth. "I can do nothing," he said.
Hosah was impatient. Why had he been so foolish as
to put faith in a physician? The wise men of Israel said.
that if a man feel sick he was to produce for divine con-
sideration all his worthiest deeds, and so obtain forgive-
ness, release and healing. If he became so ill that he had
to remain in his bed, it should seem to him that he had
been brought to a scaffold for punishment. Those with a
sick person in their house were instructed to go to the
wise men and ask them to plead for mercy; for it was
written: "The wrath of a king is as messengers of death,
but a wise man will pacify it." It also assisted the sick
person toward divine compassion if alms were given to
the poor. And, when this was possible, the sick one should
be taken to the synagogue to be blessed.
Hosah now gave instructions to the wife and son of
the dying man. He told the man himself to pray, and to
announce to the Holy One all the good deeds of his life,
and to repeat over and over certain verses from the
sacred books.
Philemon went with Hosah to see a mother who was198
suffering great agony in childbirth; and at once Hosah
demanded: "Has she been saying the holy words?" Some-
one said that she had indeed, and those attending her;
but it was not known in this humble house which words
had the greatest power for a woman delivering a child.
"These are the words," said Hosah. "The Lord hear you
in the day of trouble; the name of the God of Jacob de-
fend you! Send us help from the sanctuary, and strength-
en us out of Zion....Save, Lord: let the King hear us
when we call!" These words he repeated until the woman
knew them; and he told her to say them nine times, and
if she was not relieved, to say them nine times again; and
if she was still in pain, Amiel would come to her and con-
jure one of the angels who presided over childbirth.
For Philemon this was all so incredible that he doubted
his ears; and his amazement grew as he went with Hosah
night after night. He asked Hosah if he thought words
were things, and Hosah gravely replied: "A word created
the world." For Hosah, the words in his holy books were
indeed divine things, with an existence of their own.
Words, laden with magic, could go forth, upon being ut-
tered, and fulfill for good or evil the commands which
they bore. A curse, for instance, was as real and as tangible
as a leper. It was not merely a hope, a wish, a prayer; the
words themselves were a curse, and when spoken were be-
yond the power of the speaker to annul or recall. Phile-
mon knew that other peoples were superstitious about
words, and held speech, both oral and written, in awe;
but he had known no people for whom words had such
fantastic powers as for these pious Jews. To understand
the difference between Greek and Jew, he now perceived,
it was necessary to grasp the difference in their attitude
toward language. Little wonder that a devout son of
Israel did not dare to pronounce the secret Name, or that
he died with the word One on his lips!
As a matter of fact, Philemon learned that the Pious
sometimes used the Name to cure, combining it with in-
cantations, prayers, or with various other words from the
holy books. An amulet with the Name on it, Hosah assur-199
ed him, had more power than armies or whirlwinds.
After watching Hosah use the Name in an effort to ease
a mother in childbirth, and a man dying while coughing
blood, and a third, a child who had wasted away until he
was only skin and bones, Philemon concluded that Hosah
was using magic. He did not dare to tell him that. He
did not like to offend him.
Philemon knew there were many diseases which phy-
sicians could not cure, but he was convinced that he could
have saved these mothers who died in childbirth. And one
night he lost his patience when Hosah began to breathe
sacred words over a man who was cadaverous and bathed
with moisture, whose joints were thickened, whose lips
were drawn sharply over his teeth, whose shoulder blades
were as unfleshed as stones. He would have moved the
man to a dry warm place. He would have prescribed
certain nourishing foods, and sunlight and rest. But Ho-
sah said the man was sick because he was wicked, and
he gave him foolish formulas to murmur while he died.
"Your confounded formulas are no good!" Philemon
cried angrily. "Let's take him out of this wet place."
"No," said Hosah. "If the Holy One wants him to live
he will live."
But none whom he saw among the feeble, the old, the
sick so astounded Philemon as Izhar. Izhar had fallen ill
after reading the maledictions which Jeremiah had heap-
ed upon Israel. Then, compelled by a morbid fascination,
he had read them again, and collapsed. He seemed to be
dying when Hosah and Philemon came to him. Stretched
out on a mat, he was gasping like a beached fish, the cords
in his neck swollen and rigid, his eyes glazed with the ter-
ror of one who felt himself to be in the clutch of evil.
When light was cast on Izhar, Philemon looked at him
and thought he had the 'sacred disease'-that he was an
epileptic who had not been able to come out of his last
seizure. But his friends said that Izhar had always been
a normal person until that time when, reading the Pro-
phet, he uttered a dreadful cry and fell over. Since then
he had not been able to rise or speak.200
While Hosah murmured over Izhar certain holy words,
Philemon stared at the man and thought: Here is one,
in actual terror and madness, whom belief in the magic
of words is killing! Never had he seen anything like it.
Never would he have believed that the mere reading of
a wild prophet's curses could strike to the earth a strong
man, and make of him this pathetic and quivering creature.
Izhar never recovered. Paralyzed, rigid, his eyes stand-
ing out from their caves, he lay on the mat and died, slain
by the power of words. That is what Hosah believed.
"Good God!" said Philemon. "He was killed by ignor-
ance and superstition!"
"No," said Hosah gently. And he explained. In the
writing of Jeremiah were the divine words of the Holy
One, the Eternal. Izhar had sinned. When he read the
curses that had been laid upon sinners he collapsed, for
the fear of the Almighty had been in the midst of his
secret chambers, and had gone like fire into his bowels.
For was it not written, "If I go forth into the field, then
behold the slain with the sword!-and if I enter into the
city, then behold them that are sick with famine! Have
you utterly rejected Judah? Has your soul loathed Zion?
Why have you smitten us, and there is no healing for us?
-we looked for peace and there is no good!"
"Hosah, you people are driving yourselves crazy with
these morbid idiocies of the men you call prophets!"
"You are profaning!"
"I'm going mad. I understand now what Reuben
meant-"
"Reuben, he is wicked."
"Sometimes I try to look down the future and imagine
a world with Israel triumphant-a mad, insane world-"
"If you have vision," said Hosah gently, "you see the
Holy One enthroned upon the praises of Israel, and Israel
upon the world."
"Oh, great gods, no! What I see is the awful slaverings
of Amiel ben Micah, whose fantastic visions are making
fools of all of you. Hosah, you're mad-mad in a gentle
friendly pious sort of way."201
"Amiel is the wisest of the wise men.'
"Wiser than you?"
99
"Wiser than all men. Wiser than all the wise, for the
Holy One is in the midst of him and he is the suffering
servant."
"You mean Israel?"
"Israel is the suffering servant. We are punished more
severely, because we stand as an example to all people.
We have always been punished more. We shall be."
"You're mad, utterly hopelessly mad-and Judith is
becoming just as mad as you are."
Hosah gave him a faint smile touched with pity. "When
you are able to understand Amiel you will understand
the divine purpose."

AMIEL BEN MICAH, wisest of all the wise men, was
dreaming a stupendous dream, and this was true of him
awake or asleep. He was so filled with the spirit of holi-
ness, that he was unafraid, and did not hide away, like
most of the Pious, in gloomy underground rooms, or flee,
as so many had done, to the barren places, but sat above
the earth and in the holy city, undisturbed and undaunted,
while the divine materials of his vision filled the chambers
of his heart.
When awake and dreaming he was a sight to hold the
attention of anyone. A small and inoffensive man, with
large luminous eyes in a pallid face, with delicate hands,
usually clasped and resting in his lap, he would sit for
hours at a time without stirring, proud to know that he
was a divine instrument. His life was the inner life,
deeply buried, enriched with all the furnishings of an ex-
travagant imagination, shaped by the artist's subtlety and
care; and when he sat, dreaming, there seemed to be little202
life on his surface, because all his heat and vivacity were
drawn inward to his thoughts. He might then have passed
for a man half-asleep or half-dead. But one observing him
would now and then have seen him light up, as though
lamps had been kindled within. His whole face awakened
and glowed. His eyes shone like dark stars. Sometimes the
light in him was so radiant and intense that he leaped up
and down and uttered strange little cries of rapture like
a bird in dusk-shadow at a watering-place. Or he would
run round and round, softly leaping, twirling, dancing,
clapping his hands—and all these things he did because
his inner revelations overflowed his being and spilled in
light and song.
What was Amiel's vision? It was of many things—of
the eternal life of Israel, the future glory in which all the
saints would share. . . . and the spread of Israel's ideals
and customs and laws over the whole earth. . . . and the
conversion of all the heathen people to Israel's god, of
the destruction of Israel's enemies and the coming of a
rod from the root of Jesse, a scion of the house of David,
a king more splendid than Solomon, who would rule over
Israel, enthroned upon the world. Such were the principal
wonders of his dream; but recently he had been leaping
and dancing in rapturous frenzy because he understood
that there would be a resurrection of the bodies of all the
saints, and for them eternal life. For he had found it
written, "Your dead shall live, my dead bodies shall stand
up."
Amiel liked to dwell on the subject of angels, their ap-
pearance, doings and powers; and one day there came to
him the thought that every righteous Jew had a guardian
angel, to watch over him, to plead for him before the
Father and he had danced for a long while. He was the
first among his people to individualize angels and name
them—and this he was delighted to do, giving to attri-
butes and qualities the most loving care. Michael, he de-
cided, after naming him, was the guardian angel of Israel.
His face, like that of all the good angels, shone like the
sun; his dress had the appearance of feathers, for he had203
wings and could fly, and his wings, flashing in the sun,
were of the color of gold; and his hair was whiter than
the cleanest and whitest wool; and his voice had the
sounds of many musical waters. It was, Amiel had divin-
ed, the principal duty of the angels to chant the praises of
the Holy One. They stood before his glory and extolled
and blessed him, crying: "Holy-holy-holy is the Lord of
angels!" They also brought before the throne of the
Presence the prayers of the righteous ones, and carried
the Name's messages to Israel. It was the angels who
whispered to Amiel. . . .
"You mean they bring the messages straight to you?"
Hosah asked.
"Always—when I sleep-when I'm awake."
"And again and again they tell you that Israel will
triumph over his foes?"
"Yes, Hosah!"
"And that King Antiochus will die a horrible death?"
"Abominably horrible."
"He will fall down and burst open?"
"Yes."
Hosah, who believed that Amiel was a prophet, had
been asking these questions because he wanted Philemon
to hear the answers. Philemon stared at Amiel and
thought him ten times as mad as Hosah.
Amiel would have said that the only madness lay in
doubting him. When an angel was communicating to him
a message from God there was in his whole being an al-
most suffocating sense of light and glory. Truth became
palpable: he could feel it, he could hear it ringing on his
inner ear, and he could smell the incense of it. . . .
"When shall we triumph?" Hosah asked.
In the Holy One's appointed time, said Amiel; and
added that when all the nations of the earth laid siege to
Jerusalem, God would descend to the Mount of Olives,
and there direct the war. That would be a great day! But
before then there would be extreme suffering and the holy
city would be plundered; but ah! the Presence would then204
come in power and in splendor and usher in his reign, and
night and day and heat and cold would be no more.
"When will the son of David come?"
"It is written, Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion!
Shout, O daughter of Jerusalem! Behold, your king comes
unto you, he is triumphant and victorious, lowly, and rid-
ing upon an ass, even upon a colt the foal of an ass !"
"Will he ride into the city on an ass?"
"Yes," said Amiel, looking into his vision.
"Will he smite with the sword?"
"All nations will bow before him."
Sometimes Amiel thought of the coming messiah-King
as a son of man-as the ancient one, half-celestial, half-
human; or sometimes as the suffering servant, symbol of
all the righteous sons of Jacob.
Save when he slept or took care of his few needs, Amiel
sat in his tiny mud-brick hut which clung to a hillside, and
dreamed his dream. He had a copy of the Torah hidden,
but he seldom looked at it, having got most of it by heart.
He was a learned man. Even more, he was a sensitive in-
strument in which divine messages vibrated and formu-
lated themselves, as various notes could be gathered up
to make a song. He sat close by the door, which faced
the east; and now and then in passing Philemon looked
in on him. One day he shouted:
"Amiel, you mad monkey!" But Amiel did not hear.
He was sunk deep in his vision. Only after Philemon had
cried his name a half-dozen times did the prophet come
down from his ecstatic soarings.
"Why do you delude your people with this mad notion
that they will rule over the whole world?"
"Because so it will be."
"You think this David-king will rule over the earth?"
"Yes."
"You're mad. All this stuff you've taken from the
Persians. . . ."
But Amiel was not listening. He had returned to the
fountains of his wisdom.
"Amiel, you blockhead! You're duping and deceiving205
your people. You're teaching them to seek martyrdom....
Amiel, are you listening?"
With a voice so quiet that Philemon was startled Amiel
said: "The Holy One fulfills his purpose in allowing us
to be persecuted, for he is preparing us-"
"Never mind that! I've heard it before."
"He's preparing us to be the priests-"
"Man, you're absolutely mad. You people are all mad.”
"The Holy One—”
"And you're driving me mad. Amiel!"
But Amiel was dreaming the dream that would become
the belief of all the righteous among his people. For a
rationalist like Philemon, for a Jew like Reuben, Amiel
was mad indeed; but for Hosah and Jesus and the Joses
he was the voice of the Father in heaven.
The Syrian troops under command of Menelaus, who
now lived in the citadel, heavily guarded, continued to spy
and torture and kill. They tried to seek out for death all
parents who had their baby sons circumcised, all people
who kept the sabbath day holy, and all who owned a copy
of the Torah; but when, prowling through the city, they
passed the open door of Amiel, they merely glanced at
him and sneered and taunted, having no doubt that he
was mad. Did he not leap up and caper like one demented,
kissing his hands to the sky and singing wild songs? And
so it was that Amiel was allowed to move deeper into his
vision, like a man drawn toward fires on a bitter night.
There came to him Jose ben Johanan.
"Amiel, how long will our punishment endure?"
"Until Israel spews out his wickedness."
"The time will be long then, for Israel grows more
wicked by the hour."
There came to him Jose ben Joezer.
"Amiel, beloved brother, should we all leave the city
and find homes where the Beast is not?"
"The Beast," said Amiel, meaning Syria, "is every-
where."
"When will the king come to deliver us?"
"In the season of the Passover, for it was then that a206
great horror of darkness fell upon Abraham; that Sodom
was destroyed; that the walls of Jericho fell; that the
captains of Sennacherib were overtaken by the hand of
Power."
Jose was beaming. There was no warmth like the words
of a wise man. "What signs will announce his coming?"
"The sun and the moon will darken, and swords will
appear in the sky, and vast herds of horses will rush
through the clouds. All of nature will be convulsed. The
sun will appear by night, the moon by day, and blood will
run from wood, and stones will speak, and salt will be
found in fresh water, and the seeded fields will be barren,
the wells and springs empty, and sin will rule over the
world. The father will fight against son, brother against
brother, daughter against daughter, nation against nation,
and to war will be added famine, earthquake and fire.
There will be lewdness and blasphemy in the synagogues.
Learned men will be hated, righteous men despised. Boys
will insult graybeards, old men will stand abashed in
the presence of children, sons will curse their fathers."
All those, Jose reflected, were worthy signs. He said:
"It is written, I will ransom them from the power of the
grave; I will redeem them from death: O death, I will
be your plagues! O grave, I will be your destruction!"
And Amiel said: "In that day Michael will stand up,
the great prince: there will be a time of trouble such as
there never was since there was a nation; the people will
be delivered, everyone found written in the book."
"May the memory of the righteous be a blessing!"
It was a practice with oracles and fawning courtiers to
tell kings that they would conquer the world and restore
the golden age. In foretelling a deliverer from the seed
of David, Amiel had adopted a wish and a prayer com-
mon to oppressed peoples in all lands, but he was giving
to the wish a holy sanction. Jose was now remembering
that some of the priests were saying that it was Moses
who would come; and some, Elijah; and so it was that
he asked:207
"Has the angel made it known to you that the King
I will be of the house of David?"
"Yes."
"Let us pray that he will come now!"
"He will come when sin sits like a leper in every syna-
gogue.'
99
They fell to talking about the angels, these two wise
men-the heavenly messengers, the hosts of heaven.
Highest in rank were the seven archangels, Uriel, Raph-
ael, Raguel, Michael, Saraqael, Gabriel and Remiel.
Among the seven, Amiel explained, Michael was pre-
eminent, he "that is set over the best part of mankind.”
Gabriel was second in rank. In appearance they were like
torches, like burning coals of fire; the color of their body
was whiter than the snow or redder than the rose; their
eyes were like burning lamps; from their mouths and
nostrils issued flame. They were angels of white, angels
of light, angels of fire, wearing golden girdles and speak-
ing with the voice of many waters. As for demons, of
which there were millions, they were the loathsome and
treacherous servants of the Wicked, who at will could
assume the form of toads, lizards, frogs, ants, snakes or
mice. They had once been sons of God, as it was written,
but had rebelled and come down to earth, landing upon
Mount Hermon. Then they married mortal women and
taught to mankind the evil arts.
The two wise men, in comradely rivalry, were display-
ing to one another their great learning. Jose said:
"It is written that the angels are the sons of the
mighty."
"It is written that they are the host of the high ones."
"It is written that they are the princes."
"It is written that they are the morning stars."
"They are the channels of divine revelation."
"They are the guardians of the righteous."
All day long these two men might have engaged one
another in antiphonal responses; but Jose had another
matter on his heart. He had once sat at the feet of Jesus,208
the son of Sirach, and it troubled him that Jesus scoffed
at the belief in another life.
"Jesus says the dead merely rest, for there is no de-
light in the underworld. He says mourners should not
mourn too long; remember him not for he has no hope:
so it is written. When the dead is at rest, let his memory
rest; for so it is written."
"But it is written, My bodies shall stand up. Those
who sleep in the dust shall awake, some to everlasting
joy, some to everlasting pain. And the righteous shall rise
a month earlier in Israel than in other lands."
That thought Jose had not heard before. He was
startled. "Do you say those buried in Israel will rise a
month earlier?"
"It is so."
"And you say the dead will pick up their bodies, that
have fallen apart from decay?"
"It is so."
How wonderful it would be! Jose looked at this pro-
phet in whom the will and purpose of the Holy One were
manifest; and he said: "May the Presence bless you and
guard you, may he make bright his face toward you and
be gracious unto you, may he appoint you for well being!"
After news of these greater wonders came to Philemon
he was sitting one night in the cave and in the dim light
of one lamp was looking at Judith. As usual she was sit-
ting with her head bowed and her face hidden. For
months she had been like a woman wholly lost to life but
he had thought: She grieves for her parents, she is shock-
ed by the horrors she saw, I must give her time. Now,
looking at her, he said:
"Judith."
"Yes?"
"Have you heard these fantastic things which Amiel is
now saying?"
"I have heard."
"Do you believe them ?"
"Yes."209
"Then you must be mad also. Can't you understand
that these are utter lunacies ?"
Judith raised her head. "It is you who don't under-
stand. The purpose of God is revealed in his prophets.
You will never understand until you humble yourself."
"Oh, I'll be damned and cursed and torn apart limb by
limb before I'll listen to much more of this madness!
Either you're going to come to your senses or I'm going
to try to leave the city!"
And Judith said quietly: "Leave the city? You couldn't
now. Haven't you known that Reuben's spies watch you
day and night?"
21
YES, PHILEMON HAD KNOWN, in a vague and troubled
way, that he had been spied on since the day he came out
of the cave. For weeks he had sensed the presence of only
one spy, of a short agile man who wore Parthian trousers
and a loose coat that fell sacklike to his knees. His head-
piece was caplike and was tied roundabout with a string
in the fashion of the Scythians. It usually covered so much
of the person's face that Philemon had never seen the
features, though distance alone would have prevented
this, since the man was always fifty yards or more away.
Of late Philemon had become aware of two spies. The
second wore the garb of a peasant Jew but kept his face
covered. This spy, he had observed, seemed to follow
him only when he went to the eastern hill, where the
temple stood deserted and the palestra was loud with re-
joicing athletes.
Having again taken lodging in the tavern he left his
room one morning and was on his way to the eastern hill,
where he hoped to meet some of his old friends, possibly
Reuben or Paul or Malanie, when, on pausing and look-210
ing back, he made out the figure of the second spy. Sur-
prised when Philemon turned, the spy moved quickly to
find shelter behind a tree; and in the movements Philemon
recognized what he had known before. In some way the
person was familiar.
Spying was so common in the city that those who were
not conspirators paid little attention to it. Philemon sup-
posed that most spies were armed. He did not know that
the factions opposed to Menelaus had organized an un-
derground espionage. "That is one of Menelaus' spies,"
he mused. "He has set two upon me"-and he glanced
round him for some sign of the other. He knew that per-
son. Was it Paul? After considering a moment he went
briskly back down the hill.
The spy was too much the master of himself to confess
guilt with flight. He stood by the tree, his posture that of
one sunk in contemplation. When at a little distance from
the tree Philemon stopped and cried out: "Hey there,
my good fellow!" He saw no movement in the silent
figure. Advancing a little he called again, demanding,
"Why do you follow me?" There was no response. Un-
willing to proceed, lest the person fall upon him suddenly
with a dagger, Philemon considered the matter, wonder-
ing whether to risk attack or to retreat. Speaking more
kindly he said: "I don't know who put you to spying on
me or why. I'm a physician-why, then, do you follow
me ?"
The person turned now and showed her face for it
was Judith; and if the sky had opened and Amiel's angels
had descended in legions, with hair like clouds and eyes
like fire, Philemon could not have been more astounded.
He tried to cry her name but his voice had been silenced.
He went forward until he was close enough to touch her
and looked into her eyes.
"Great God! Why are you spying on me?"
Her gaze wavered and fell and for a long moment re-
mained downcast. Then, looking full upon him, she gave
her answer: "Why do you go to the hill of the mur-
derers ?"211
"The-the-" he said and was flabbergasted. "Up
there—you mean. Well, if I were to tell you you'd think
I lied."
"Do you lie?" When he did not speak she asked: "Do
you go to see Melanie ?"
"Melanie! If you've been following me you ought to
know. Judith darling, are you jealous?"
"One isn't jealous of what one despises.

"You wouldn't care, I suppose.'

shall be impotent if you spurn me much longer; for denial
of conjugal rights unreasonable restriction of your free-219
dom-such as keeping you from weddings and funerals.
You could cast me aside because of an offensive occupa-
tion-if I were a tanner, for instance-or anything with
a stink about it. The contract says I must provide for
you. You are to grind the meal, bake, wash the clothes,
cook, nurse the children, make the beds, work in carding,
spinning, weaving-but unless the Roman barbarians steal
my wealth you'll do none of those things."
"I wouldn't nurse my own children?" she asked, lift-

"Oh, if you insisted. I'd rather keep your breasts love-
ly so I could put my face between them."
"How many children would I have?"
"How many
would you want? a dozen?"
"The Holy One commands us to have many, but if
we have many we become unattractive and our husbands
don't like us."
"Your Holy One doesn't seem to have figured things

"Oh, the marriage-price! Ah, what darlings are daugh-
ters!" She was smiling.
"Keep smiling," he said. "When you smile you're more
beautiful than heaven. Could you laugh for me?"
"No."
"Laughter is better than wine. But Jews laugh only to
express derision or incredulity. Then Abraham fell upon
his face and laughed, and said in his heart, shall a child
be born unto him that is a hundred years old? Therefore,
Sarah laughed within herself, saying, after I am waxed

have you never laughed just for the simple joy of being

"No."
"Will you ever?"
"Some day—if you will teach me how." She had laid

her face grave at first, but softening and smiling when
she saw the laughter in his eyes.220
"Judith, before you go you must promise me one
thing."
"What?"
"To give up this mad idea of killing Menelaus."

"Give it up! I made a vow. When we make a vow—
"Yes, I know. The words go forth and you can't recall

"So you can stop me?" she asked, mocking him.

"Let me think about it. Then I'll tell you what I'll

Philemon stood up. "You'd better kiss me before you

She shook her head. "At this moment we're being
watched by a spy. How foolish to think of kissing!"
Then, stepping close to him, she maneuvered with her

under the fold and took his hand and clasped it, looking
all the while into his eyes, her lips parted, her own eyes
full of strange sad things. "You must no longer go un-

22
AFTER SHE HAD GONE Philemon looked round him but
could see no sign of Angela. He went to Simon's tavern
and ate, and after dark slipped into the cavern to find
Judith. While inquiring for her in the gloom among those
who lived there he was touched by a hand; and Hosah
suggested in a low cheerful voice that it was time to sub-
mit himself to the rites.
"The rites? Has Judith told you I want to?"221
"You want to marry Judith, don't you? Then you have
to be a proselyte."
"Well, yes, but first I'd like to go with you when you
circumcise a child."
The circumcision of infants was punishable with death,
but after almost a year the reign of terror relaxed, in-
formers were not so intent on spying out the guilty, and
Menelaus turned from the rigors of persecution to his
soft delights. During the year Hosah had served as god-
father and priest many times, in secret, far back in the
darkness of caves, or in stables among beasts. It was
to a cave that he went with Philemon.
While they were groping in the darkness a light ap-
peared, a feeble wick burning in a tiny clay bowl of oil.
The light went ahead of them and they came to a group
of persons. Another and larger light was burning there.
Philemon could see anxious faces peering at him, and
lips formulating questions; but Hosah, when made aware
of all this, said only, "He is our friend," and proceeded
to his task.
From somewhere back in darkness a baby was brought
forth. Those assembled uttered a low cry: "Blessed be
he who comes in the name of the Lord!" A tall man then
spoke. "Behold, I am ready to perform the command-
ment of the Creator, blessed be he, enjoined upon us,
to circumcise my son, as it is written in the holy books."
The infant was then placed on a chair, which symbolized
the throne of Elijah.
A voice said, "Behold, I will send my messenger and
he will prepare the way before me!"
Hosah then spoke. "This is the throne of Elijah-may
he be remembered for good. For your salvation, O
Lord, I have waited! For your salvation, O Lord, I have
hoped! Your commandments I have obeyed! I re-
joice...."
And those assembled responded: "Let us be satisfied
with the goodness of your house, your holy temple!"
Hosah, acting as the God-Father, now sat on a stone
and took the infant on his knees, saying: "Blessed be you,222
O Lord our God, King of the world, who have sanctified
us with your commandments and enjoined upon us this
covenant." He then grasped the foreskin and stretched
it and cut it off close and tossed it into a box of sand
which had been prepared; for it was written that the
children of Israel would be as the sands of the seashore.
The father intoned: "Blessed are you, O Lord our
God, King of the world, who have sanctified us by your
commandment, and enjoined on us to bring our sons into
the covenant of Abraham!"
The people responded: "And he has entered the co-
venant, so may he be permitted to enter the study of
the Torah, the marriage state, and the practice of good
deeds!"
Hosah took up a goblet of wine. "Blessed be you, O
Lord our God, King of the world, creator of the fruits
of the vine. O our living God, our Portion, our Rock, O
God and God of our fathers, preserve this child for his
father and mother, and may he be called in Israel Gama-
liel the son of Zaccai the son of Joseph. Let the father
rejoice over him that came from his loins, and let his
mother be glad because of the fruit of her womb. . . ."
Wine was sprinkled on the wound and the child's
face. Hosah took a mouthful of wine and sucked blood
from the wound, and then sprinkled the wound with
powder. Philemon was leaning forward for a better
view. He saw Hosah drink of the wine and give a few
drops to the babe. He saw him hand the wine-skin to
the father.
Having other infants to circumcise, the sick to visit,
the dead to bury, Hosah did not wait for the feast. At
the cave entrance he said to Philemon: "I will leave
you now. May the Holy One bless and protect you."
Thinking of what he had seen and heard Philemon
made his way to the tavern and called for hot sausages,
Bithynian cheese, a kind of bread-cake made of coarse
flour, honey and spices and milk, and a pitcher of wine.
Most people mixed their wine with water, diluting it
two or three parts to one, but Philemon always took223
his neat. While eating and washing the food down with
Simon's finest beverage Philemon reflected with a shud-
der that if Hosah were to see him now he would be ap-
palled.
"If Judith were here," he thought, "we'd feast to-
gether"--but he knew well that Judith would not again
enter a place like this. Would she ever lay aside her
suffocating holiness? He doubted it.
He was sitting back in a corner, hidden from the re-
velers, imagining that nobody was aware of him, when,
looking out across the top of his cup, he started and
spilled his wine. There, sitting at a table in plain view
of him, was the person, the strange small man, who had
been spying on him the past year. Judith said it was
Angela. He strode to the table and looked down at the
figure. The face was hidden. The figure seemed to be
any lonely little man who had come in from the night.
"Angela," he said. There was no response. "Come
over to my table and drink with me." Then he wondered
if this was Angela. With one sweep of his hand he tore
away the caplike garment that fell over the face, and
Angela, an Angela who seemed to have grown old,
looked up at him.
He returned to his table. In her outlandish male
garments Angela followed him. Her headdress was that
of a Mede-a large hood that enveloped not only the
head but the face and throat also, with two strips of
cloth hanging, one down the front and the other down

the tops of which her trousers were tucked. All her gar-
ments were much too large for her.
"What you need," said Philemon, amused by her ri-
diculous appearance, "is some laurel leaves. You could
chew them and get as drunk as Dionysus."
Angela said nothing. She was looking at him, her
lovely face pale and quiet.
"Will you eat and drink with me? It has been a long
time since we sat here together... May I order for you?"
She did not speak.224
"Come!" said Philemon. "Drop your pose. I know
all about you. You're the one who brought the bread

You're the one who has been spying on me ever since
I left the cave. But you're also the one who used to
come to me on Reuben's housetop. We were gay then.
I loved you. God, how I loved you."
Philemon drank wine and held the cup close to his

and very fragrant wine from Heraea.
"According to the poets," he said, when the wine

She neither accepted her wine nor rejected it. It stood

How's Reuben? . . . Angela, if you're going to act like

Her eyes told him too late that he had blundered.

""
"You should starve a while in a cave." When she

She had been looking at the food. She now raised her

"How stupid it all is," he said. "Human beings differ
only in their customs and habits, and these like sandals
wear out. Yet people are everlastingly killing one another
because their customs are not the same. One cannot step
in the same river twice. Does it matter to anyone but
fanatics? Is it so important that you must hate your sister,
and Reuben must hate me? Can it matter to anyone but
fools that Homer sang praises to one god, Moses to
another?—or that they are the same god, or that neither
is any god at all? When we see persons hung to spikes
on walls, or crucified, or burning alive, or having the guts
pulled out of them, or being blinded with lime, or slowly
pulled joint from joint; when we see babies with their
skulls broken and their fathers in chains; when we see the

Zeno, if what they said can produce nothing better than
all this? When we see the face of one who has just died on
a cross can we have patience with Greeks who call all
other people barbarians, or with Jews who think they are
chosen to rule all nations? I speak as a physician, Angela,
who knows there are wounds besides the wounds we can
see.228
"One day I was traveling through my country. I saw
a man dressed in sacred garments and covered with holy
branches, being led through a city. People were transfer-
ring their sins to the head of this man. Because a plague
or something had struck their city the people had taken
this man, a volunteer, and fattened him. They fattened
their scapegoat-that is, the goat who would be cast
from them, with their sins on its head. Your people call
it the goat for Azazel. On your day of atonement your
High Priest lays upon the head of this goat all the sins
of Israel, and it is cast off a precipice.
"Well, Angela, my dear, if there is sin, it can't be
transferred to the head of a goat. It can't be atoned for
in the death of a man-god or god-man on a cross. Yet
this piece of ignorance is only typical of the ignorance
that is to be found everywhere. Ignorance, Angela, ignor-
ance! Jews are killing Jews because of ignorance. My
people fought one another for generations because of
ignorance. Because of ignorance I'm thrown into a cave
and chained and starved. Is your High Priest going to
destroy Ezekiel's imbecilities by killing me?"
"He wanted Judith."
"Of course. Well, among your people is a saying that

"Mine."

"You know well enough," said Angela angrily, "that
I won't tell him."

She was looking at him, her face weary and sad.

picture of Socrates. Because he said the gods of Athens

speaking to his friends, he took the cup of poison and230
drank it. Some of his friends were then weeping, and

"That's what Epicurus said."

"But you've forgotten Epicurus. He said that if there

looking at him, and in a low voice she said: "I'd kiss

kissing her fingers to him, and said, "Philemon, fare-

Philemon sank to his chair, feeling lonely, angry, de-
serted, afraid. Only now did he grasp the implications
of her visit and her words. Only now did he understand
her own loneliness and heartbreak, for he knew that,
with some new and greater menace impending, she had
come to warn him for the last time and to say farewell.
23
FOR A FEW MINUTES after she left him Philemon sat at
the table drinking wine and recalling Angela's words.
Wondering if among the people in this tavern there was
anyone spying on him he moved to another table, taking
a position that would allow him to observe all the per-
sons in the room. Then, casually, while sipping wine, he
studied them one by one, but he saw nobody who seemed
to be lurking. The Parthian with the frizzed whiskers
was romping back and forth with a sad and decrepit
old harlot. A younger woman, who also seemed to be a
whore, had her hair filled with flowers: drunk, and with
a fixed and stupid leer on her face, she was nodding her
head toward a brutal-looking wretch, who possibly be-
longed to a caravan that had stopped for the night.
Just beyond these two sat a man alone, a melancholy
fellow, beardless, his hair cropped close, with moisture.234
running from one eye. With a dirty rag he wiped at this
eye, and after several movements across his eye he
gulped from his wine-cup.
Socrates had said that nobody, not even a slave, would
risk the shame and scandal of eating in a public-house.
No one here seemed to be above the level of ragpicker
or vendor of dunghills; and among all these pariahs there
certainly was none who resembled a spy. And yet, Phile-
mon thought, coming up with a start, would he know
a spy if he saw one? Did he expect the man to wear a
sign? A spy might pretend to be a beggar or a harlot, a
wandering tramp from some Decapolitan town, a dull
lout from Idumaea. Thinking that he might outwit the
spy if one was present he went to the rear of the tavern,
where a stairway led to sleeping-chambers. He made
a racket going up the stairway but then quickly slipped
his boots off and came down, concealing himself behind a
wall and peering out of the darkness; and at once he
was surprised to observe the change in the sad fellow
who had been wiping his eye. The man was no longer
using the dirty rag. He seemed to be listening, and in any
case he was looking obliquely and furtively toward the
doorway through which Philemon had vanished. After
a few moments he arose from the table, carefully set his
wine-cup back, and adjusted his cloak like one preparing
to enter the night. Philemon wondered if the sly rogue
had put a caustic in his eye to make it water.
Whose spy was he? In his room the next day Phile-
mon asked himself the question. In almost the same mo-
ment the Roman general, Popilius Laenas, in Egypt, told
King Antiochus the Magnificent to go back to Antioch.
When Antiochus, stalling for time, said he would need
a few days to consider the matter, Popilius took a staff
and made a circle around the King where he stood, say-
ing: "Declare your intentions before you step out of it!"
Having no choice then, Antiochus said he would leave
Egypt, and he left in a fury that verged on insanity.
Looking round him, in the way of tyrants obsessed by
lusts and thwarted in their ambitions, for a scapegoat235
on which to spend his rage, he thought of the Jews, and
resolved then and there to wipe Judaism off the face of
the earth. Summoning Apollonius, a regimental com-
marder, he told him of his plan. They would return to
Jerusalem and he would leave Apollonius there with
enough troops to throw down the walls, fortify the old
city of David, build a polis south of the sanctuary, and
erect theaters, gymnasia, public baths, museums, libraries,
and possibly a university. Every Jew who did not re-
nounce the faith of his fathers was to be slain. Those
who renounced the faith would be called upon to attend
a festival in honor of Dionysus, wearing wreaths of ivy
and feasting on the flesh of swine. "The gods have
given us swine to eat," he said to Apollonius. "Why don't
the idiots eat it?"
While the enraged King made haste toward Jerusa-
lem what were the people doing?
The wife of Hosah was unclean because she had
given birth to a child. It was written that for her puri-
fication she must offer a lamb to burn, and a dove or
pigeon for her sin; or, if very poor, two doves or two
pigeons. She had managed to find two doves; and on the
day Antiochus entered Gaza she went to the temple
court by the gate of the firstborn and waited at the in-
ner gate, standing at the top of the steps of the Levites.
There a priest was supposed to take her offering, and
then return to sprinkle her with blood and declare her
cleansed. But there was no fire on the altar, there was
no priest in the inner court, and no priest came..
...
Enoch ben Zoma, desperate because for months Ju-
dith had eluded him, appeared before Jose ben Joezer,
crying, "You must tell me the Name!" Jose was appalled.
The blasphemous fellow was asking for the secret name
of the Holy One. And he would not be put away. He
seized Jose's garment and fell to his knees, beseeching,
making fantastic promises, and saying that if the Name
was not given to him he would throw himself off a

cumcised dog Philemon-and this must be done because236
the angels had promised Judith to him, yet she loved the

. . . ."Go from me!" cried Jose, recoiling from such

created worlds and could destroy worlds? "It can kill

"Dog of wickedness, go!" And Enoch, turning away,

man have my woman? With the Name-" "Shut up!"
"Ah, what a bloody man I am!" said Enoch, beside him-

At that moment Antiochus was crossing the hills be-
tween Gaza and Hebron.
Amiel ben Micah, sitting alone, with a childlike smile
on his features, had been soaring on the wings of his
vision, accompanied by angels and holy lights. He was
now thinking, with sweet and tender pride, that the
wisdom in Israel's sacred books was far superior to that
in all others: the one came from God, the other from
mortal man. What folly it was in any man to read the
Greeks when all truth had been given to the Jews, and
waited on their wise men to interpret and expound! The
more deeply Amiel looked into the holy writings the
more brilliantly shone for him their gleaming treasures.
During the previous night, while reading by a feeble
wick, he had come upon this: "And it shall come to pass
when 70 years are accomplished that I will punish the
king of Babylon, and that nation, says the Lord, for
their iniquity, and the land of the Chaldeans, and will
make it perpetual desolations." So it was written. So
it would be fulfilled. Amiel saw history as a continuous
disintegration of kingdoms, until at last there would be
a universal theocracy, under the control of the chosen
people, with Jerusalem as the capital of the world.
Smiling, dreaming, warming himself in his reservoirs
of piety and holiness, he sat in the afternoon of a late
spring day, in the month of Iyar, on the sixth day of237
the week, and saw in his vision his people singing praises
to the Holy Name; and at noon of this day, King An-
tiochus, his fury unabated, marched out of Hebron. . . .
Those were punished with death whom the spies of
Menelaus found keeping the sabbath day holy; but the
bolder men, like Hosah, encouraged their people to
kindle the sabbath lights, far back in the caverns; to hold
the evening meal; to bless the children; to recite verses
from the holy books. This sabbath eve, while Antiochus
encamped between Hebron and Bethlehem, Philemon
went with Hosah to see what was done.
Among the families in one cave, living there like beasts,
with only the earth to sleep on, was a very devout father.
He had many children, who gathered round him accord-
ing to age; and over the heads of his sons he said this
blessing: "May the Holy One, blessed be his Name,
make you like Ephraim and Manasseh." And over his
daughters he said: "May the Holy One, blessed be his
Name, make you like Sarah and Rebekkah, Rachel and
Leah." In each instance he concluded with the words:
"May the Holy One bless you and preserve you, and
make his countenance to shine upon you, and be gracious.
unto you; the Holy One lift up his face toward you and
give you strength and peace." Then he placed both hands
on the head of his frail and graying wife and said: "A
woman of valor who can find? For her price is far above
rubies. The heart of her husband does safely trust in
her, and he has no lack of gain. She does him good and
not evil all the days of her life. She seeks wool and flax
and works willingly with her hands. She is like the mer-
chant-ships, she brings her food from afar. She rises
also while it is yet night and gives food to her household
and a portion to her maidens. She girds her loins with
strength, and makes strong her arms. . . . Strength and
dignity are her clothing.... her children rise up and call
her blessed; and her husband also, and he praises
her.
99
The light was very dim, but by coming close to those
who were blessed Philemon could see their faces. He238
studied them in turn. In the face of son, daughter and
wife there was richness of joy and of inner goodness that
glorified the countenance and kindled the eyes. There was
treasure here. Possibly mixed with it was much that was
superstitious and evil; but there was good here and it was
this good, this enrichment of hope, patience and faith,
that Reuben and Angela would throw away, along with
the tiresome nonsense in Leviticus.
Philemon watched them closely when they broke bread
and ate. For their sabbath meal they had only water and
unleavened bread, and the bread was stale and hard.
After washing his hands the father offered a thanksgiv-
ing: "Blessed be you, O Lord our God, King of the world,
who give bread to us out of the earth!" He then gave
a piece of bread to each member of his family; and they
accepted it, Philemon observed, as though it were in-
fused with holiness. One boy, a child about nine, with
large soft sad eyes, kept looking at Philemon, in one
moment startled and abashed, in the next smiling shyly;
and at last, secretly, he offered to slip to Philemon a part
of his hard bitter meal. . . .
Later Philemon said to Hosah: "What is good in your
religion seems hopelessly mixed up with what is bad."
"It is all good," said Hosah.

Reuben, gathered with friends in his sumptuous quar-
ters in the citadel, was saying:
"Those who won't renounce we'll destroy. I know Jews
well. Few will renounce. We'll have to kill a great many.
For those we kill we'll need colonists. These we'll bring
in from far places-dyers from Tyre, silversmiths from

persons of quality, so that we may have a city here we
can be proud of.
"We'll have a great museum here. I think the King
will let us have a university-oh, not so splendid as some,
for we can't afford so much, but the best we can afford.
We shall have a great library; that is certain. That has
been promised to us-and I have the secret wish that
here, some day, will be a library greater than that in
Alexandria. This is going to be a worldly city for en-
lightened people-for people who do not," he said, his

physiology from Moses.
"To be sure, a lot of these pious fools who spend all
their time mumbling prayers will flee. We can deal with
them later. Those who remain we'll kill, and the sooner
the better. The King will arrive about tomorrow noon,
as I understand it. All plans are in order. Because tomor-
row will be the sabbath day there'll be no resistance. No
pious Jew anywhere on that day will lift his hand to
protect his throat."
He turned to a small man with a sad face, and a cloak

looked over at Paul. "Forgive me this one weakness,” he

A voice spoke up. "Paul's brother?"

SOME TIME BEFORE DAYLIGHT Philemon was awakened
by a noise at his window. He sat up, drugged with sleep,
and listened, and after a moment he thought he heard
a voice there. On bare feet he went over to the window
and peered out. The window was not glazed; it had no
covering but a latticework of stout wooden bars secured
on the inside. Angela was pressing her face against two
of these bars and talking between them, and when Phile-
mon took two of the bars away she thrust her head inside.
"Philemon?"
"Yes?"
"Are you alone?"
"Yes."
"Are you awake? Do you understand what I'm say-
ing?"
"Yes, Angela."
"I said I'd never warn you again but here I am!"
"Is there some danger?"241
"The King's army is coming. It will be here some time
this sabbath day. If you love Hosah and Judith you
should take them and get out of the city. They're both
marked for death."
"For death! Angela, are you sure of this?"
"If you doubt me-"
"No, I don't doubt you."
"Philemon?"
"Yes?"
"This is going to be worse than the last time. I don't
think you'll be hurt if you stay here-if you don't take
up weapons against us." He could see her face dimly but
she could not see him and she kept turning her head and
peering like a blind person. "You won't, will you?"
He had knelt, so that his face was in the same plane
with hers; and he now reached out to clasp her arms,
though whether to kiss her or to put his cheek against
her cheek or merely to reassure her with a gentle pressure
he did not know. With terrible violence she struck his
hands off.
"Good heavens, Angela!"
"Keep your hands off! I didn't come to be raped. I
only came to warn you-and some day I'll probably be
sorry I did that."
"When will the army reach the city?"
"I don't know." Her voice was harsh and bitter.
"Angela, let's not part this way. I was not going to
rape you, you idiot. I touched you because I love you.
"That's an old story that you've talked to death." She
withdrew her head.
Unable to see her he thrust his head out between the
bars and heard her descending a ladder. Under his face
a moment later he saw the ladder disappearing like a
streak of shadow and he heard footsteps and then there
was silence. Returning to his bed he sat and thought of
her warning, and though he was wide awake, he briskly
massaged his wrists, his belly, the back of his neck, his
temples and his back over his kidneys. Hosah and Judith
were marked for death!242
Philemon kept his garments in a chest to protect them
from insects and mice. Kneeling by the chest he felt
around in it and brought forth a pair of boots with wide
tops, trousers and a short sturdy coat, and a splendid
dagger which he had never worn. He held the dagger,
thinking of what Angela had said, and then reluctantly
put it back, hiding it under a pile of garments. He had
slept in a loose sleeveless gown that fell from his shoul-
ders to his knees. Laying this aside, he put on first a
girdle, a long cloth about a yard wide that he wrapped
several times round his waist. Then round his waist above
the girdle he clasped his money-belt. It had been a cool
night. The air coming through the window smelled clean
and spiced. He put on the heavy trousers next and then
the boots, thrusting the trouser bottoms into the boots
before drawing them tight with leather thongs. He put
on a thin undershirt, then a light mantle, and over this
the sturdy coat. In the chest he searched until he found
a Persian cap: made of wool, it had a high crown and
an apron which could be dropped over the shoulders and
drawn forward to conceal the face. When dressed he
felt warm, snug, and ready to face the hazards.
Softly he left the room and went down the stairway
and to Damon's chamber, where he rapped gently until
the valet was awakened. Damon came and opened the
door, and Philemon whispered: "Dress in a hurry. We've
work to do." While Damon got himself ready Philemon
went outside and looked up at the sky. There was a
breeze from the northwest. It seemed to come in gusts,
being in one moment like a great hot breath and in the
next like the air off cold waters. In the breeze was the
scent of flowers and of the desert.
Damon now came out. He was a large young man,
rugged and bold. When spoken to he always smiled. He
seemed to be a gentle and inoffensive person but he had
been a soldier, he had slain a number of men, he was
skilled in the use of all weapons.
"Are you armed?"
"Yes, master."243
"Come." With Damon following him Philemon went
north from the tavern but stopped to sit for a moment

Angela. "How soon till daylight?"
"Master, I can't tell."
Damon knew of the cavern in which Hosah and his

breakfast and take some food with him and station him-
self in some sheltered spot near the cavern entrance.
When Damon had gone Philemon rubbed his hands
over the bench and recalled the many pleasant hours here
with Angela and Judith, with his books, with other
friends. Did he feel that he would soon be dead? He
asked himself that question and shrugged when the an-
swer came so sudden and plain out of his being. He
looked up at the sky, searching it for morning. . . . It was
very doubtful whether he could get out of the city, even
alone, much less as one of a party; and it was more
doubtful still that he would live long if he stayed here.
When he arose at last to go to the cavern he won-

The last question was answered an hour later. Phile-

25
ALL DAY THE WALLS CAME DOWN. A host of men at-
tacked with picks and battering-rams, undermining from
the bottom and breaching the sides, until segments col-
lapsed one by one. Other men searched the city for Jews
who were observing the laws of their fathers. Small male
children were examined, and if they were circumcised,
the mother was slain and hung on a wall, with her dead
child tied round her neck. Men searched for copies of
the holy writings, and when these were found they were
defiled in horrible obscene ways and then destroyed; their
owners were mocked, scourged and killed; their wives
and children were sold as slaves. All day long on this
holy sabbath day the walls tumbled, the mercenaries
prowled through temple, huts, goatpens and such caves
as they could find; all day long the pious screamed and
died.
Philemon hunted for Judith, pretending to be one of
the King's men. He saw things that enraged him, that
sickened him, that made him turn away, faint with shame
and loathing. He saw so much plundering and killing, so
much torture and agony, he heard so much screaming and247
wild supplications of those scourged, he smelled so many
odors of suffering and death that he was forced to look
at mankind in a fresh perspective, asking himself if hu-
man beings were actually what they so plainly seemed to
be. Under their surface of piety and fellowship were all
their deepest urges base and brutal? Certainly the men
who were killing enjoyed their work. Yet they seemed
to be ordinary and normal men, of the kind he had seen
in many lands and in many crafts and professions. Among
them there might well have been some of his former
friends. . . .
Well, there was Judith. A gentle child when he first
knew her she was now obsessed with a wish to kill. He
himself, Philemon, the physician, could with joy have
slain many this day-could have torn the guts of any
man whom he saw torturing another; could have laid
upon the throat or chest of any one of the brutes scourg-
ing with leather and chain; and looking at the ghastly
corpses hanging on walls could without the slightest feel-
ing of guilt or compassion or pity have driven a dagger
into the hearts of the men who put them there. Yet he
knew what folly it would be to punish men who had been
hired to kill and were simply earning their wages. Behind
their ugly and bloody work was an idea, a dream—the
dream of Alexander who had wanted one world and a
united people. Whether the idea was good or bad here
on the walls were the ghastly results of it, and looking
at them he was ashamed to be human. He thought: If
this is all that men are the time will come when sensitive
people will destroy themselves rather than look upon
these things. He thought: I must indeed be a coward, or
I'd either fight these assassins or go away and die. . . .
He did not find Judith. When merciful darkness came
he slipped to the cavern to tell Hosah what was being
done in the city; and Hosah murmured: "Israel has been
very wicked and his punishment is great.'
""
"It's not the wicked who are being killed."

"Can you get to see him?"
"I'm only a priest," said David. And he added with
bitter sarcasm: "I'm only a son of Aaron."
"A son of Aaron can surely get in to see the High
Priest. David, help me. Surely there'll be blessings for
you if you do."
David looked slowly and prudently round him and
then said barely above a whisper: "Come at midnight to
the court of the gentiles."
"Yes."
It was moonless dark when Philemon left Hosah to
cross the city and appear in the temple court. No bodies
hung from walls now but over the whole city was the
smell of death. He was getting used to these sickening
odors. They reminded him of the odor of serpents, of
semen, of jasmine on sweating bodies, of diseased feet.
Or it was like the smell of oldness in a city that was very
old, with generations built upon the ruins of predecessors,
until the depth of the burial-ground was greater than
that of the living..
•
He glanced behind him now and then, convinced that
he was being followed. There was no evidence of this
present to his senses-no sound of footfall, no glimpse250
of a shadowy figure; but he knew that someone was on
his path. Was it Angela ?-Paul?-or some assassin from
the citadel? Determined to answer the question he em-
ployed every deception he could think of. He loitered.
He disappeared round buildings and quickly doubled
back. He hid and waited. But he was not able to find a
sign of anyone, much less to surprise the spy himself.
He stood at last in an open space, looking at the citadel,
wondering whether to proceed or return to safety. Every-
thing around him was vivid with silence and ghostliness.
There was no moon but a few stars were out, and in their
pale light objects cast shadows that were only a shade
darker than the light itself. Ruins of a demolished wall
lay gray and spectral and terribly still. An awful quiet
seemed to have enfolded the entire city, deeper than
sleep but not so deep as death. The city was alive but not
awake and it was this strangeness that troubled him.
"I'm superstitious," he thought, and shuddered.
He was facing south. Behind him were the ruins of the
wall and the open wilderness. On his left was the dark
rounded masses of the Mount of Olives, on his right the
western hill, where he could see faint lights. Before him
was the gloomy and sinister pile of fortress in which
Menelaus lived.
For several minutes Philemon stood in helpless indeci-
sion. He was afraid to proceed to the temple court, yet
dared not admit to himself that he was afraid. He felt
angry and he felt bound. Somewhere, concealed, a pair of
eyes, amused and cynical, was watching him. Somewhere
ahead, he had no doubt, assassins waited. . . .
He turned to the left, going down among olive groves
and vineyards and scattered huts. He ran, but tried to
tread softly and soundlessly, his head and shoulders
thrust forward, his arms lying together across his stom-
ach, all his senses preternaturally awake. He knew the
paths here. Like a hunted beast he followed them until he
was under the hill at a point due east of the temple. Then,
while sheltered, he rested and eased his heavy breathing,
all the while looking behind him. He believed that he had251
shaken the spy off. When his breathing was normal he
climbed up the hill and peered over at the temple wall.
The distance between him and the gate was about a
hundred yards. Across this distance he would be fully ex-
posed to citadel sentries. He decided therefore not to run
but to walk, like one who had business here; and he went
over, unmolested, to the temple wall and stood by it
and then walked in its shelter to the southeast corner;
and there, while looking round him, he rubbed a palm
over the masonry and smelled of his palm. Seeing no sign
of anyone he rounded the corner and went to the first
of the southern gates. Stepping inside, he stood in shadow
against the wall and looked across the huge court and
waited.
He had stood here a few minutes when across the
court a figure seemed to detach itself from the masonry
and shadows and move toward him. It was loosely en-
veloped by a robe, with its face hidden. The person come
up and at once moved swift strong hands over Philemon
searching him for weapons. Then this person went back
across the court and another person came forward and
approached Philemon. He knew at once that this was
Paul.
When Paul came up, Philemon said: "How are you,
Paul?" There was no answer. "I saw the priest David.
He told me to come here."
"What do you want?"
"To see Reuben or Menelaus."
"What for?"
"To beg them to stop all this senseless torture and
killing.'
"You fancy that you have such powers of persuasion?”
"I could try.'
99
"And credentials, of course, to prepossess them in your
favor."
"What I should say is, I suppose, only what any phy-
sician would say."
"Tomorrow at the time of the full market come to
the south gate."252
Paul was turning away when Philemon cried: “Paul!
Please, one moment."
"Yes?" said Paul, turning to look at him.
"Do you know where Judith is?"
"No."
"Haven't you seen her in these recent weeks?"
"No."
Philemon was convinced that Paul was lying. "I know
you detest me. You may have good reasons. But I love
your sister and I want to see her."
"I know nothing about her," said Paul coldly.
"Is it possible for me to see Angela?"
"You're a piece of human seaweed," said Paul, "drift-
ing in the strongest currents. You have no state, no city,
no people. So you cling to the skirts of Jews. Can you
think of nothing better than that? Why don't you go to
Alexandria where you belong?"
"Why do you despise me?"
"My good man, I don't. You're beneath pity or con-
tempt.'
99
"All right!" said Philemon hotly. "You kill them. I'll
heal them."
"If you were an architect no doubt you'd want to pre-

never destroy one that you might build better."
"You're talking of wood and stone—”
"Bah! Do you find the Parthenon worth no more than
your own commonplace flesh ?”
"Very well, but you do know where Judith is. Tell me."
"I've no interest in Judith."
"Between us-'

"And I want none of your confidences or secrets. David

"How shall I know the guide?"
"The guide," said Paul drily, "will know you.
