Then Paul crossed the courtyard and vanished, and
Philemon was alone. He left the court and went west-253
ward and down across the vale, intending to go to Simon's
tavern; but while crossing the vale he smelled and heard
the beasts. He turned back, feeling a wish to sleep among
them. It had been the custom of Greek artists to enhance
the perfection of the ideal human face by suffusing it
with some of the animal forms of beauty and nobility.
This had been done with Jupiter and with other gods;
there was a good deal of the bull in Hercules; the strength
without the violence of the brute in Pan; and there were
the centaurs and hermaphrodites, in which efforts had
been made to conjoin and fuse many virtues and prin-
ciples.
...
As tomorrow he would try to conjoin and fuse the
two positions, Hosah's and Reuben's. He wanted to think
about the matter and he felt that he could come closer
to the heart of it if he sat at the animal level, feeling
himself, frankly and pleasantly, to be the animal which
he was. After all, the fusion of the human and the animal
was the most brilliant achievement in the Greek repre-
sentation of beauty. If Philemon could have looked into
the future and seen that for thousands of years this same
bitter struggle would endure, this conflict between two
points of view, two basic premises, two irreconcilable
forces and wills-if he could have forseen all this he
would have turned aside from his hopeless task. But
hope and faith stood in him like fires. He searched
among the pens where goats and other beasts were
stabled and found a pile of clean barley straw. He fell
upon this, lying on his face and belly, and half-buried
himself in the fragrant chaff and the sweet earth-
smells. His arms and legs were outflung. He slept.
And while he slept a girl, in the garb of a man, came
softly into the shed and looked down at him for a few
moments and went away.254
26
IMPORTANT CITIES OF THIS TIME had in addition to
their walls a strongly fortified citadel on the order
of the Greek acropolis, which served as a refuge and
last defense. The stronghold in Jerusalem, with its
walls and towers and gates, its underground water sup-
ply, its granaries and arsenals, and its carefully picked
garrison, was almost impregnable to attack and siege.
Within this fortress Menelaus lived, with Apollonius
and the other officers, and his own intimate advisers,
like Reuben and one Jakim who called himself Alcimus.
When the next day at the time of the full market,
which was midforenoon, Philemon presented himself at
the south gate, a man, a stranger to him, came forward
from the guards who loitered there, saying, "Come." He
entered the citadel with Philemon at his heels and pass-
ed through another gate and climbed a stone stairway,
pausing at last before a great door that rested on bronze
hinges. The man gave a signal and this door was opened;
they now entered and crossed what Philemon, judging
by the odors, took to be a storeroom for less perishable
food. Then they passed through another gate, and a
few moments later Philemon found himself amidst lux-
urious surroundings, with Reuben looking at him.
"Well, well!" said Reuben. "We meet again! Have
you come to drink the Teniotic wine?"
"That depends on you."
"Come forward," said Reuben, and with a gesture
he indicated a number of persons.
Glancing quickly at the faces, Philemon recognized
Paul, and next-with a faint start-Melanie; and then,
with a more noticeable start-Menelaus. The High
Priest was sitting half out of sight in a great chair255
piled deep with cushions. Melanie also sat but the other
persons were standing.
"We'll sit," said Reuben, and indicated a circle of
comfortable chairs which plainly had been arranged for
this meeting. "Sit, friends, and let's hear what this man
has to say. You all understand he's here because we
once were fellow-students in Alexandria and because he
has not yet lifted arms against us. But he has forgotten
Euclid, Archimedes, Eratosthenes, Hippocrates, Epicu-
rus-all of them, all of them!"
There were two thoughts in Philemon's mind: that
Reuben seemed to have aged years since he saw him last;
that hundreds of Reuben's people were starving in foul
caves while he surrounded himself with all the luxuries
he could find.
Those who had been standing now sank to chairs.
Wondering where he was expected to sit Philemon ob-
served that only one chair remained and that it faced
all the others.
"That's right," said Reuben, watching his face. "You
sit there." And he added with deliberate cruelty: "We
shall all study your face while you talk."
Philemon sat. Before him, with every pair of eyes
on his face, sat seventeen men and three women.
"Now," said Reuben, "speak. Have you come as the
betrayer of human progress and the special pleader for
superstition and ignorance? Or have you come to find
your fellowship?"
"The problem isn't quite so simple as that. I've come,
above all, to protest the killing of a defenseless people.'
""
"Defenseless?" asked Reuben, and with raised eye-
brows looked around him. "I was under the impression
that Israel's god, the mightiest of all gods, was on their
side. Would you say that Alexander's killings were un-
necessary? Do you know what his vision was? I'm sure
you don't, so I'll tell you. It was the idea of this great
general and statesman to unite the whole world under
one government, one culture-"
"No! It was his idea to bring the whole world within256
Alexander's notion of what the world should be-which
is a very good way to destroy it. Does progress come
from similarities or differences?-sameness or various-
ness? The Romans are determined to place the whole
world under one government and one culture-and prob-
ably will-and quite certainly will destroy either them-
selves or the world doing it. Vision! But I didn't come
here to dwell on Alexander's outrageous egotism. I came
for the simple reason that thousands of people live in
terror, hunted like beasts. I came because of the hun-
dreds you've slain and hung on the walls and the thou-
sands you've already sold into slavery. Since you taunted
me with Eratosthenes, may I remind you of a story that
was once a favorite with you? It is the story of Gescon
the Carthaginian who, when recalled from exile and made
a general, put his heel gently on the prostrate neck of his
enemy and said, I'll not return evil for evil, but good
for evil. It's you, Reuben, who've forgotten Alexandria!
There you had a motto above your door. Do you remem-
ber it? Diogenes, when asked how best to get vengeance,
said, By becoming a good and honest man.'
99
Menelaus came up from his chair, angrily demanding:
"Have I been summoned here to listen to the vaporings
of a lunatic? This man has no grasp of the problem."
"What is the problem?" asked a voice. "Let someone
state it."
"Reuben, state the problem," said Menelaus, and sank

"I'm sure he knows the problem. Is any advantage to
be won from telling him what he already knows?"

"Because," said Reuben blandly, "he may be worth
more to us alive than dead."257
"Well, if our task is to persuade him, let the problem
be stated."
Then another man spoke, a man with an abnormally

"That's it," said Menelaus, looking over at the man.

"Not wholly."
"Why not?"
"I think it is over-simplified."
"He's been spending his time," said Reuben, "with

Who begat the fallen angels? Ah, the daughters of men!
How many angels are there? One personally to attend

Leering at Philemon he asked: "Have you been talk-

He's of no use to you. He hides in a thicket and there

Some of the faces were grinning at Philemon. His

"We must forgive him. He's only a physician. When

him; holy he is, and righteous, and there is no other god.

A man about thirty, with brooding eyes and a thin

Rather than destroy fanaticism in the houses of flesh

"We'll grant it."
"To believe in something higher and nobler than self—
and mark this well," he said, looking at Heleb, "is to
organize the scattered tyrannies of emotion and wish

and self-regulating power. It makes no difference at all, as

-as long as the idea of god serves the interests of har-
mony and design. All people but Jews have many gods;
and they also have confusion, lack of symmetry and de-
sign and purpose, which is always found when there is no
core, no center of control. Jews, with what seems to be
superlative, even if unconscious, wisdom have refused to
accept the disorder, the complex and chaotic and littered

tors, no equals, but many assistants, as good managers
always have, including Alexander. They have a set of
instructions from this manager, which have to be fol-

giving them wonder and meaning, are various rituals.265
Now all this is called religion. From another point of

Philemon was so overcome that his senses swam when
he rose to his feet. Somebody took his arm to guide him,
and he went out of the chamber and out of the citadel, and
stood in full sunlight, with sudden tears blurring his

went over the hill and down into the valley, too numbed
to think, almost too numbed to feel. After a while he sat
and rested his face in his hands and tried to recall what
had been said. Was there no way to bring peace to these
people? Was Reuben right in believing that the Torah
and its fanatics should be destroyed?
After a while he went with no effort toward conceal-
ment to the cavern where Hosah was waiting; and he
said to him, "They know all about us, Hosah. If you want
to go out into the sunlight, go.'

AFTER HIS DISCUSSION with Reuben and his group, life
seemed to Philemon to be unreal and depersonalized
and barbarous. Over and over occurred the thought that
he would be dead but for Reuben, and now would see him
no more. But for Reuben Judith would be dead! And a
thousand times the next day while waiting for sunset
he asked himself if he was a fool. Was it true that an
infatuation for a woman was betraying him into the
hands of stupidity and ignorance? What could he expect
but death if he remained with her? But he knew deep
in his heart that he would remain. He knew that the issues
and principles involved in this great struggle far trans-
cended the love between a man and his woman.
When he entered the temple court he saw across from
him a figure by a wall. At once this person began to move
toward him, and though the garb was that of a man
and the walk was unfamiliar he knew that this was Ju-
dith. He saw that she came with reluctance, came toward
him as though drawn or pushed, her head high and dis-
dainful, her tread soft, deliberate, cushioned.
She came up and stopped. Her face looked thinner
and older.
"Well," she said, "here I am."
"Come, you're going with me."
"Oh no I'm not!"
"Judith, listen. You've been a fool long enough. Reu-

When they left the tavern he carried two knapsacks
stuffed with food. The night was dark, with neither moon
nor stars. Because cities were not lighted at night they
were dangerous; beggars and thieves prowled everywhere.
The police force in most cities was small, incompetent and
corrupt; as likely as not, a man who by day was a police
officer was by night a brigand. Philemon knew that it was
hazardous to proceed in such darkness to the cavern, which
was a mile away. Telling Judith to walk behind him and
as close to him as possible he went slowly, trying with
all his senses to detect ambush and enemies, and pausing
often to listen. Now and then he reached back to touch
Judith, and when she sensed that he was seeking her she
would find and press his hand.
Their journey was so free of trouble that Philemon
suspected after a while that they were being protected.
He was convinced that this was so when, after covering
half the distance, he heard a struggle, possibly thirty276
yards away, and then the sound of a blow and the running

did not understand what was said. Then after a few mo-

"I don't know. It sounded like a fight." He set down.

28
SOME TIME BEFORE DAYLIGHT Hosah and his family,
Philemon and Judith left the cave and the city and
trudged with difficulty through the darkness toward a
village named Modein, a dozen or so miles away. Hosah
said that Amiel and the two Joses had escaped from the
city, but not Jesus ben Sirach, who had died. In Modein
after their arrival Philemon saw a gaunt and gray-
bearded priest named Mattathias and his five extraordi-
nary sons. He little dreamed how well he was to know
these men before his life was done.
In this hamlet of Modein there was peace, though an
altar to Zeus had been built here. Word had come that
swine were being offered on the temple's altar, and that
to square himself before a world shocked by such dese-
cration, Antiochus had sent forth a story, saying that
in the temple he had found a Greek who had been fat-
tened for a year, as an offering to the Holy Name. Other
calumnies, equally base and wicked, were spread abroad.
Then, a few days later, word came that the King had
gone to the eastern part of his empire, there to wage
war or to plunder temples, nobody knew which.
During these first weeks of peace Philemon considered
his problems, his position, his future. He asked Judith
to marry him but she said no, that marriage was out
of the question until the wicked were driven from the
holy places and peace was established in the land. He278
asked her to go with him to Alexandria, where he could
resume his studies and set up as a physician; but she
said: "Not now. Darling, not yet."
Conditions for Philemon in Modein were barbarous.
The food was coarse and tasteless; the bed he slept on
was a straw mat; the bedding was his own garments.
The rainy season had come. The air was chilled, the
nights were cold, but in the huts where Hosah and his
friends dwelt there was never a fire. Hosah and his com-
rades, giving all their time to the Torah, would wrap
up in all the clothing they had and sit together and
shiver while trying to interpret the words in their holy
books. Judith had few clothes and suffered from ex-
posure. And the facilities for taking care of private
needs were so crude, not only for voiding, but for the
simple matter of keeping clean, that Philemon was ap-
palled. The whole village shared a wet and ill-smelling
privy that was so offensive that Philemon dreamed about
it. He suspected that the water supply was fouled.
He despised the flavorless barley broth and the dehy-
drated and oversalted fish and the coarse black bread.
He wanted to go to Jerusalem for clothes and food but
Judith would not listen to it. And so day by day he be-
came more wretched and ill-tempered, marveling in one
hour at the devotion of these holy men who cheerfully
endured every hardship, and in the next reproaching
Judith because she would not go with him to a civilized
place. What desperate measures he might have been
forced to it is impossible to say if a power mightier than
his own had not fallen like thunderbolts upon the land
and determined the course of his future. The forces
of revolt had been gathering in Israel.
Word was sent to Modein that a Syrian official would
appear to establish the form of worship which the King
had commanded, and to make an offering to Zeus. The
morning of the day when the official was to come, Mat-
tathias and his five sons-John, Simon, Judas, Eleazar
and Jonathan-went to the altar; and though in their
appearance there was no sign of it they were armed with279
long sharp knives. A little later, sensing that violence
was imminent, Philemon went over, with Judith and
Hosah. After studying the faces of the old priest and
his sons Philemon decided that here were six desperate
men who would stop at nothing.
"Blood will run here," he said to Judith. "Those men
intend to kill."
"You imagine things," said Judith, smiling.
"No I don't. I know that look in the faces of men."
"An old face filled with wrinkles and covered with
gray hair. Some time we'll look that way."
"I see more than that. Look at the son, the one with
the heaviest brows."
"That's Judas."
"What do you see in his face?"
"Unhappiness."
"And the one next to him-"
"Simon. He looks crafty. Next to him is Jonathan,
who looks bored; and then Eleazar, who looks tired."
"Let's see what Hosah thinks." They went over to
Hosah who stood by Amiel. "What do these men intend
to do when the King's officer comes?"
"I don't know," Hosah said.
"You'd better talk to them or there'll be trouble."
The King's men, led by one Appelles, a royal officer,
came from the south. When Appelles saw that only a
few persons had gathered to wait for him he sent
heralds into the country roundabout to summon the peo-
ple; and while this was being done, Appelles and his
assistant remained at one side of the square, looking
across at Mattathias and his sons. Philemon thought
the assistant was a Jew, and he asked Judith if this was
so; and she made a grimace and said:
"I think he is."
Now from the direction of Jerusalem came a man rid-
ing an ass and holding a lamb in his arms. He came up
and dismounted. Turning to the man standing with Ap-
pelles the rider offered the beast to him, and this man,
who was indeed a Jew, took it to the altar. The heralds.280
had returned. A few people had come in. Appelles and
his comrades made a royal group of seven.
Appelles now came forward to address the people.
He stood by the altar and said: "His royal Highness,
King Antiochus the Illustrious, has commanded all Jews
to put aside their abominable practices, to submit to the
royal decrees, and to make the customary offerings to
Zeus. Does any son of Israel here have a copy of the
Torah ?"
There was no answer. Philemon, watching the face of
Mattathias, was feeling suffocated with anxiety and
dread.
"Is there any child in this area who has been circum-
cised since the royal decrees?"
There was no answer.
"Do you people know that the penalty is death for
the observance of what you call the sabbath? I've been
empowered by his Majesty, the King, to overlook cer-
tain instances of disobedience, if henceforth you will be
faithful to his laws. You must understand at once that
disobedience on your part can only bring down upon you
the most severe punishment. I have been sent here to
instruct you in your duties and to make an offering to
Zeus on this altar. Does anyone have anything to say or
any objection to make?"
Like defiance rumbling in a cavern came the anger of
Mattathias. He cried out with a dreadful voice that
the altar which had been built here was a foul desecra-
tion and an abominable wickedness. And then, advanc-

"My name is Mattathias!"
Appelles turned to the Jew with him and they talked.
Philemon said to Judith:
"There'll be trouble here. Look at the man's face."281
He meant Mattathias. The graybeard's face was twitch-

"You're Mattathias, the local priest?"

"Silence!" cried Appelles. "Would you send me back

After listening with astonishment Appelles turned to

men were being slain. He saw other knives flashing. He
heard screams. He was dimly conscious of a group kneel-
ing in prayer. If some omniscient power had told him
that in this moment, on this spot, he had witnessed the
beginning of the first religious war in history, he would
not have known what was meant. As it was he saw no
more and sensed no more than seven dead men lying
in their blood, and a priest and his sons wiping off long
knives. Looking away from the massacre he became
aware that Judith had fainted and fallen, and he knelt
by her. She had fainted dead away, this girl in whom
spirit was the brightest flame he had ever known. But
some others had fainted. Still others, sickened, were
kneeling. But Mattathias, the priest, and his five sons,
undaunted and undismayed, were neither sick nor faint-
ing, but were looking down with hate in their eyes at
seven dead men and thrusting their knives back into the
folds of their girdles.
And almost at once Mattathias and his sons pitched
in and destroyed the altar and fled into the hills.
When darkness came, Hosah and his comrades, in-
cluding Philemon and Judith, met in counsel. Hosah was
very angry and so indeed were Amiel and the Joses.
They did not believe in bloodshed and war. They looked
with horror on their brothers whose inflamed passions
drove them to murder. Mattathias and his rash deed
would call vengeance down upon all of them, and no-
where in Israel would a man find a place to lay his head.
"What can we do? O God, O God, what can we do!"
Hosah cried, and nobody had an answer. The King's
assassins would come soon. "Must we spend all our
lives hiding like beasts in caves!"
"How long!" cried Amiel. "O Lord, how long!"283
"Are we to be hunted from cave to cave and driven

"How long, O Lord, how long!"
"Be merciful unto us, O Lord, for our souls are among

"If we go north or south where is there a place of

“O Lord, have you cast us off? You have scattered us,

News of Mattathias and his sons swept Israel like
fire. Almost at once he had a guerilla band of several
hundred desperate men who hid by day and attacked by
night, smiting the sinners and godless, razing the heathen
altars, forcibly circumcising the uncircumcised, exhorting
the Jews to be faithful to the Torah, and pursuing the
sons of pride, the blind sheep, to their death. In the few
days that followed the Modein incident, Mattathias and
his band killed a thousand men. News of his success
brought more Jews to his banner, until his ranks swelled
to two thousand, to three thousand, as men from all
over Israel and from lands beyond Israel found heart
and courage and dreamed the reckless dream that the
might of Syria could be overthrown. Every man under
Mattathias' banner was a fanatic. In hand to hand com-
bat he was worth a dozen of the mercenaries who made
up the King's armies.
When news came that the King was sending a great
force into Israel to crush the rebels, Judith dressed in

"Are we going to sit here and wait for the assassins?"

They decided to represent themselves as Syrian slave-
merchants. Able-bodied prisoners of war, if not slain as
holy offerings, were sold as slaves; and so it was that
a number of slave-merchants always went with an army.
They bought wholesale from the conquering generals,
then sorted their slaves out, much as they might have
graded cattle; because some, such as beautiful virgins,
or young men skilled in trades or professions, brought
high prices, and were sent to special markets, like the
one on Delos. The ordinary run of slaves, men, women
and children, they resold in batches to the owners of
large estates, or to smaller merchants who, in turn,
graded the wretched creatures and strove to make a
profit.
As a slave-merchant with his secretary, Philemon
thought that for a while he would be safe enough.
Mattathias and his officers would know who he was,
and Reuben's spies would hardly be able to mark him
among the scores of merchants who would now swarm
over Israel. But he and Judith did not go to Mattathias'
fast and far-ranging band. Mattathias sold no slaves.
The sons of the no-gods he killed, sparing none. The
sons of pride and wickedness, the blinded sheep among
his own people, he also killed if they did not repent
and return to the Way.
Why, Philemon asked Judith, should they join Mat-
tathias' inflamed fanatics? An army would come against
them soon enough.
"Mattathias will win."
"You talk like a child."
"Israel will have an army."
It was in such moments, when he saw the fanatic in
her eyes that he loved her least. It was in such moments

HOSAH AND HIS FAMILY and friends went away from
Modein and entered a cave. Philemon and Judith, with
rolls of bedding and knapsacks stuffed with food and
personal items, wandered over the hills to the west and
north, keeping away from people, deeply happy under
their sadness and anxiety, deeply in love. If they had to
die soon, he told her, they ought to try to crowd a life-
time into the few weeks, months or years that might be
left for them. The nights were warm, for this was the
month of Ab in the year 167. The first day they walked
several miles, eager to put Modein out of sight; and
they rested at sunset on a hilltop and there made their
supper of dried figs and dates, bread and a little wine.
They made their beds apart, spreading out mats to lie
on after removing sticks and stones and smoothing a

UNTIL THE SECOND SABBATH in the early autumn month
of Elul they wandered over the hills and were happy.
Somewhere around them, perhaps in the north, Mat-
tathias and his guerrillas were busy with their bloody
work but they saw no signs of fighting anywhere. Around
them the farms and homes were abandoned.
"Where have they all gone to?" Philemon asked.
"Into the caves.'
""
He thought she was speaking in bitter metaphor. He298
did not know that many Jews had gone underground, or
that in this area where they now wandered a thousand of
them were starving and waiting in a huge cavern. This
he was to learn in what was to be the most dreadful ex-
perience of his life.
They had slept one night as was their habit on a hill-
top and they were eating breakfast when, looking away
toward Jerusalem, Judith cried:
"Philemon, see!"
He looked away and saw an army. It was not march-
ing but had drawn up before a round barren hill. This
was about a mile from where they sat. They saw men
scurrying about but they did not know that a Phrygian
officer named Philip had found the hiding-place of a
thousand men, women and children. To the persons hud-
dled like beasts in the cave Philip was at this moment
promising safety if they would come out and submit to the
decrees of the King. The leaders refused to give promises
or to leave their refuge. Though frightened and anxious
they were determined to be true to their faith, and the
officers at last were put completely out of patience. They
began to curse these Jews. They sent men into the area
round-about to find materials for a fire. Then fires were
kindled at the mouth of the cavern and flaming torches
were hurled inside upon the people. When Philemon saw
the smoke he suspected what was to be done, and, look-
ing at Judith's face, he knew that she knew.
"Let's get away from here," he said. He made rolls
of their bedding. "Judith, let's go."
"I want to stay."
He looked away at the clouds of smoke and the flame
in the smoke. "Let's go."
"Listen!" she said.
99
There was a strong breeze and it was blowing toward
them. Philemon thought he could hear screams, faint
but distinct.
"Good God, let's go."
"I want to go closer," said Judith, rising.
He seized her arm but she threw his hand off and then299
began to run and he ran after her, begging her to stop.
When she did not stop he overtook her and seized her
arms and looked at her face. It was very white but very

"Judith, are you out of your mind?"
"No....Come."
She ran again and he followed her, and a few hundred
yards from the army they crawled under a shelter of
desert shrubs and sat. They now had a clear view of the
whole ghastly scene. When Philemon fully realized that
people in the cave were being burnt alive or suffocated
or clubbed if they staggered out, he turned away, too
shocked to look on such horror. But not once did Judith's
gaze falter. With her arms lying side by side across her
lap, her head up, her face white but resolute, she looked
straight ahead, and did not even wince, so far as Phile-
mon could tell, when she heard the screams torn from
tortured throats. Philemon was the first to catch the odor
of burning flesh; he looked quickly at Judith but there
was no change in her. Even when the odor began to
come deep and heavy, like that of great holocausts on
altars, there was no change in this woman. With the
smell of smoking flesh came the screams, and Philemon
bowed his head to his arms, refusing to look. But Judith
sat like a creature of stone, her eyes wide and bright and
her upper teeth set upon her lower lip.
For possibly two hours they sat here. Then it was all
over. A thousand Jews had died for their faith, and
there was only the smell of them and the dying fires. The
army marched away toward Jerusalem. Smoke still
poured out of the cavern but nowhere in the awful scene
was there a sign of life.
"How strange it is!" said Judith at last. "My people
are burnt alive and the odor of them has made me
hungry."
"Judith."
She turned to look at him with curious searching inter-

"Judith."300
"What are human beings anyhow? Are they no more

He wondered if she had gone mad. "Judith, let's go."
"Go where? Where can a person go and be unasham-

"I know, I know. The Romans did the same thing to

People do things to people. The philosophers haven't

.Judith."

her to the earth and flung the bedrolls down and sat on

dare to look at her face; he was afraid her grief would
be dry and terrible like that of a lunatic he had once

was weeping quietly like one whose sorrow rose from a
deep fountain. Her eyes when he looked at her were

she made no move to realize where she was or with

was asleep.
Moving gently he pillowed her head on one of the
rolls and spread out the other and laid her on it. He
looked up at the sky and the sun, and back toward the
scene of the massacre, and away toward Alexandria.
After a while he rearranged the beds so he could lie by
her and gaze at the sky and think. Reason and justice,
they had said! What empty and futile words in a world
of endless slaughter of man by man! The Romans were
now burning the cities of Greece. Alexander himself had
put whole communities to sword and flame, leaving
nothing behind him but the stink of death and ruin. The
beasts were not so cruel. Nothing on earth was so cruel
as man.
If, he thought, one could look down the future and
across the centuries what would he see? Would he see a
people with the devotion to truth which Socrates had?-
or the obscene schisms and divisions, factions and parties
and powers, cliques and sects and cults, based on ignor-
ance, prejudice, superstition, fear and hate? Everywhere
in the world a man could see the ravages of bigotry, the
perversions and dwarfings of human goodness. There
was nothing that could justify the debasing and debauch-
ing of man by man, the mutilation of his spirit, the crip-
pling of his mind. There was no reason, so far as he
could tell, why Jews should be fighting Jews. If Hosah302
wanted to believe in the simple customs of his fathers,
did that harm any man? If Reuben preferred the myth
of Zeus to the myth of Yahweh or rejected both was that
a matter to concern Hosah? Most human beings seemed
to be obsessed with a wish to corrupt other people to
their beliefs...
Here I am, he thought, drawn into the mad terrors
and strivings of those whose only wish is to kill. I'll be
killing soon, whose profession it is to save. I'll fall under
a dagger or a club somewhere on these desolate and god-
less and God-forsaken hills. I shall consider all the op-
pressions that are done under the sun, and behold the
tears—yea, I shall consider all the travail and perceive
that all is vanity and vexation of spirit; for there is an
evil which I have seen under the sun, and it is common
among men.
•
Night came and after a while he slept, but his sleep
was restless, for the words of the wise were taunting
him. He awakened, and they mocked him. Out of the
past came Epicurus: "The blessed and immortal nature
knows no trouble itself nor causes trouble to any other,
so that it is never constrained by anger or favor. For all
such things exist only in the weak." Out of the past came
Plato: "We ought not to retaliate or render evil for evil
to anyone, whatever evil we may have suffered from
him." And then the Psalmist: "My God, my God, why
have you forsaken me!" But always his thoughts settled
at last upon the words of the Jewish teacher: "There is
an evil which I have seen under the sun, and it is common
among men."
The night was rather cool. He put his coat over
Judith and in the moonlight looked at her face. Her
breathing was so faint that there was almost no sign of
it. Since he laid her down she had not moved, not even a
hand, but lay on her back breathing feebly through her
nose. She looked like a dead woman. He kissed her white
face and put an arm across her, and after a while he
slept again.

"Poor darling.'

BUT MATTATHIAS, they were to learn, was dead. Before
dying he called his sons to him and asked them to make
Judas, the third, his successor at the head of the guer-
rilla forces; but Judas, a much abler military leader than
his father, was not content to chase around with a band
of avengers. After the atrocity at the cavern, many of
those who belonged to Hosah's faction abandoned their
pacifism and resolved to give battle; and as by the hun-305
dreds his people came to his standard, Judas decided,
with recklessness that had never been equaled, to wage
war against the entire might of Syria.
Neither he nor his brothers nor anyone else in his camp
was familiar with the latest methods of warfare. Israel
had no trained army of any kind, not even a well-discip-
lined police force; but Judas said: "Men who kill for
hire are cowards. One son of Israel is equal to a hundred
of them." The Romans fought by hurling spears first
and then rushing forward to cut down with heavy short
swords.
Judas said he would fight with stones, slings, bows
and arrows-and courage.
The Syrians fought with slingers, archers, and dart-
throwers who swarmed out at the beginning of the bat-
tle, and were followed by chariots, cavalry, or the masses
of foot, armed with sword, spear and shield. Alexander
had had a splendid army, well-trained, organized and
equipped. He had had a corps of engineers, baggage
trains, and various other units, as well as his carefully
chosen personal staff; and his infantry, cavalry and artil-
lery had functioned as a smoothly-integrated whole.
Syrian generals were copying all the methods known to
them, and had equipped their men with excellent weap-
ons.
Judas said: "They are jackals who fight for the pri-
vilege of stripping the slain. We will destroy them by the
thousands and by the tens of thousands."
He knew the famous words of Xenophon, that the art
of war was the art of keeping freedom of action—and
that freedom he intended to keep at any cost. "We'll
kill them," he said, "if they dare to face us; and when
they run, and when they sit down to eat, and when they
lie down to sleep." And when one of his men asked,
"What will we kill them with?" Judas said: "We'll pour
boiling oil on them; we'll slay them with fire; we'll hack
them to pieces with knives. We'll kill them with our bare
hands."
His determination and courage were contagious. The306
men who flocked to his banner heard his words and then
went afield to find such weapons as they could. Mat-
tathias by night had visited the hamlets, destroying hea-
then altars and smiting the enemy, both heathen and Jew.
But Judas knew that the time for guerrilla warfare had
passed. Armies would soon come against him—and the
first troops to come were those from the citadel, led by
Apollonius. Judas ambushed him and cut most of his men
down and pursued the remnants to the gates of the fort-
ress. With his own knife he slew Apollonius and took his
sword and cut off his head. The booty of weapons he was
glad to get, and when he met the enemy under Seron,
some of his men were well-armed.
Seron, a general of the army, called out all the Syrian
forces in this province and marched against Judas. At
Beth-horon a pitched battle was fought and it was like
none that Seron had ever seen before. The Jews, looking
like an incredible horde of desert nomads, and screaming
like men wholly bereft of their senses, came like fire and
flood and wind against his mercenaries, cutting them
down with short knives and lances, felling them with
stone and club, or sometimes with no more than their
hands. Eight hundred of the enemy fell there and died,
and the remnant of Seron's troops, with the general him-
self fleeing in the vanguard, went like jackals with fire
in their tails. The dead Syrians were stripped of their
weapons and left to the birds of prey.
And so matters stood when Philemon and Judith
sought out the leader's headquarters and offered them-
selves.
Philemon had seen Judas at Modein and had been
impressed by his appearance. He was of rugged frame
and medium height, and seemed to be, at first glance,
quite ordinary. He might have passed for a camel-
driver or a peasant in from the vineyards. But when he
looked at you, his eyes staring out from deep caves and
shaggy overhanging brows; when next you observed that
the line of his lips was straight and thin, and that his
beard, though he was only in the middle age, was streaked307
with gray; and when at last you noted the wide flaring
nostrils and the pulsing in their lobes, you knew that
here was a man with tremendous energy and daring. And
when he spoke and you heard his voice, deep, impatient,
and fed by a reservoir of power, you sensed that he
would ask short questions and expect short answers.
"Why have you come to me?" he asked Philemon,
knowing that the King's spies were everywhere.
"To fight. Besides, I'm a physician."
"I need physicians. Who's this woman?"
"I'm Judith, daughter of Jorah, friend of Jose ben
Joezer."
Judas looked at her and at Philemon, and then asked,
turning to her: "Do you love this man?"
"I do."
"Do you expect to fight at his side?"
"I do."
He looked at Philemon. "Have you become a son of
the covenant?"
"No."
"We think," said Judith, "that there are more import-
ant things just now."
"Do you have weapons?"
"No," Philemon said.
"I've none for you."
"Assign us and we'll find weapons."
"How long do you two expect to live?"
"A day, a week, a month," said Philemon.

"Yes, I know Reuben-Paul. I know Menelaus."

that comes from above. They will come against us, this

our lives, our God. If you are to be with me, you must
follow me and fear not. Many of us will die, all of us
may die; so when we march, fight like one who would
live forever, and die like one who has lived forever."
They were taken to Simon, brother of Judas and in
some respects the ablest of the five brothers. The power
of Judas lay in his fanatical singlemindedness; of Simon,
in his imaginative grasp of the psychological forces in-
volved. He was quiet. His lips smiled, his eyes were hu-
morous. Judas, but for the pressure of events, would
have had Philemon circumcised before allowing him to
fight; Simon did not dwell on such matters. Judas saw
the past and the future coalescing in the battle that was
impending. Simon saw it as a minor incident in the past
and future of Israel. Judas looked to the next day,
Simon to the next decade.
"I've weapons for you," said Simon. He brought out
of hiding a knife with a stone handle and a short iron

"Which do you want?" Philemon asked Judith.
"You're stronger. I'll take the knife."
"With the axe," said Simon, swinging it, "you can

You should make it secure.'

"Soon. They're coming down the coast. Then they

"In Modein, maybe."

Judas was encamped in a forest. Philemon and Judith
went among the trees, looking for a place to lay their
beds. The sky was overcast and smelled of rain.
The army was scattered for a mile through the forest,
with sentinels completely surrounding it in depth, the
farthest outposts standing several miles from the center.
Signals were given with trumpets or with fire. Beyond the
last of the fixed outposts were the spies who with a code
of communication known only to them and Judas kept

where the alien army was on the coast; the next morning

only disorder, confusion, idleness and careless contempt.
"Does he intend to face that huge army with such

"What else has he?"
"No wonder he asked us how long we expected to
live."
"He destroyed Apollonius and Seron."
"Yes, but they had only small forces."
"Well, let's not talk about it.”
Philemon sat on a bedroll. "I guess we're all mad.
With a band of goatherds and fishermen we're going

"Success lies not in big armies but in strength from
above."310
Philemon looked at his crude axe. “I guess it will lie
in such things as this."
When darkness came they sat on their beds and ate a
little food from their knapsacks. They laid their beds
side by side and slept. The next morning after eating a
few figs Philemon walked around to see what kind of
army Judas had and he was appalled. There was, for
one thing, no well-organized way to feed the men nor
much of anything to feed them with. A man had come to
him and Judith with a little barley broth and a handful
of dried fruits. Could men fight on such food as that?
For another thing there was no provision for the private
needs of the men; and how Judith, and the other women
here, managed to care for themselves he was never to
know. The drinking-water was insipid and ill-smelling.
But worst of all the men seemed to have no sense of
discipline and order: after they had eaten their thin
breakfast they stretched out to sleep or brood, or wan-
dered like unhappy lost things, or gathered in groups to
listen while their more learned brothers expounded the
Torah. It seemed to Philemon that they should be de-
ploying in military formations and practicing with their
weapons. Then he remembered the distaste of pious Jews
for the gymnasium and athletics and all forms of compet-
itive sports and reflected that here were men, soon to
face a huge army, who had never wrestled or hurled a
diskos or run a race!
He was amazed and dismayed when, later in the day,
he watched this unorganized multitude gather to march.
It was true that there were three forces of a thousand
each, which in turn were formed in units of a hundred,
and each of these in two divisions of fifty. It was true
that when trumpets sounded every man seemed miracu-
lously to find his place. But how preposterous they were
in appearance! They looked no more like soldiers than
the rabble in Simon's tavern. A few had strong boots of
the kind Philemon and Judith wore but most of them
had makeshift sandals or stood on their bare feet. Their
tunics, made of the coarsest fabrics, were ragged and311
patched. Many of the men had cloaks and many had none.
It was a half-naked and shivering mob that marched
away to the ancient refuge of Mizpeh, only five miles
from Jerusalem itself. There, facing west, Judas looked
across the hills to the undulating and mounded plain of
the Shephelah, and beyond it to the shining sandhills of
the coastal area.
Now, while waiting for battle, the host fasted and
sprinkled ashes in their hair, wept and prayed. Philemon
would never have believed that such a scene could exist
among men. Some were so wild and constant in their out-
pouring of anxiety and grief and fury that they seemed
demented. Others had copies of the Torah and these they
spread out under the cold sky and knelt to worship. Some
of these copies had blood on them, the blood of relatives,
and some, recovered from the enemy, had idolatrous
symbols painted on them. Here and there men were lying
flat on their faces like dead things. Here and there men
were leaping up and down shouting praises to the Holy
One and kissing their fingers to the sky. . . .

"Yonder somewhere forty thousand hired assassins

merchants. They're coming to kill us or sell us. And
here we sit, a big lion ready to eat up the nations!"
Judith gave him a faint smile. "Darling, how do you
feel about everything?"
"You mean about being here and fighting with your
people?"
"Yes-that and everything."
"Oh, I feel happy to be with you, sad that we must
die. I don't know what it all means but for the first time313
I seem to have some kind of purpose-something to live
for-or I suppose I should say something to die for. Any
man who hires out to kill is not human. When I think
of all those thousands, who would gladly exterminate us,
leaving nothing but bloodstains and bones-well," he
said, speaking with sudden passion, "I'm glad, very glad,
to be enrolled among those who fight a monster like
that!"
He rolled the Torah together and laid it aside. Judith
nestled against him and reached under the covers to
clasp his hand.
"Did I answer you?"
"Yes."
A few minutes later Simon came along and paused to
look down at them.
"Any news?" asked Philemon.
"We know their plans."
"Fine."
"We'll destroy them."
"How do we know their plans?"
Simon smiled. "We have spies marching with them."
When Simon went away Philemon said: "Maybe our
case is not so hopeless. Spies marching with them! If
they knew it I wonder how they'd like that!"
"Do they have spies among us?"
"I suppose maybe they have. Still, I doubt they'd
bother. For them we must seem to be only a band of
lunatics with stone hatchets. These sons of Mattathias
may be pretty smart monkeys."
"Why do you call them monkeys?"
"One of our philosophers said all people are mon-
keys."
She squeezed his hand. "Monkeys created in the image

"Yes," he said, grinning at her. "Yes, of course. Or
gods created by monkeys in their image."
"Have you ever seen a monkey?"
"Have I!" He looked around to see if anyone was

Judas gave an order. It went from the leaders of the
thousands to the captains of the hundreds, and from these
to the captains of the fifties, one of whom came to
Philemon and Judith. "We're ordered not to sleep. We'll
attack during the second watch."
"Attack!" cried Philemon, aghast. "Are we going to
attack in the middle of the night?"
Darkness came and there was the cold wind, the lead-
en sky, and three thousand shivering persons waiting to
rush away through the night and hurl themselves upon
the enemy. How could those with naked feet go in
darkness over thorn and stone? How could those whose
only weapon was a club stand up to those armed with
lance and sword? It wearied Philemon to think of it. It
frightened him to think that the next few hours might
be the last on earth for him or for Judith or for both.
Wondering what her thoughts were he folded both her
hands within his and kissed her hair and then put his
cheek to her cheek. Returning the pressure of his hands
Judith said:
"What are you thinking?"
"It is written that sorrow is better than laughter, and
the heart of the wise is in the house of mourning."
"I used to think so, but no more. Laughter is better
than sorrow, and the heart of the wise is in the house of
joy."
"Kiss me." She turned her face toward him and he
kissed her lips. "Sweet sweet Judith! In a little while-"
"In a little while. Darling, we must not be separated.'
"We won't be. We'll stay right together, side by
side."
"If we must die, we'll die together."
"Yes-together.'
""
He now put his arms under the bedding that enveloped

32
SUCH AN ARMY as this had never marched across the
hills of Judea. In units of fifties and hundreds the first
host of a thousand moved into the southwest, followed
by the second, and then by the third, with the beasts of
burden comprising the baggage-train coming last. Phil-
emon and Judith were in the third host. Nobody rode,
not even Judas: bearing the sword of Appollonius he
walked at the head of his men.
Nowhere in the sky could Philemon see stars. Ahead
of him after a while he could make out the first section
of the army climbing a hill but it seemed to be only a
darkness spreading upward. This section went over the
hill and down and out of sight. Then the second host was
climbing the hill, and the third was down in a ravine,
looking up. Their journey lay up and down, over one hill
and another, endlessly. Much of the earth under their
feet was strewn with stones, and many feet, Philemon
supposed, were bleeding, though he heard no sounds of
complaint. He walked with Judith at his side, his right
hand clasping her left hand. All around them were the
fifties and hundreds that made up their thousand. There
was no sound anywhere but the muffled movement of
many feet.
Only Judas and his brothers in all this multitude knew
that while they were marching south and west, General
Georgias, with a part of his army, was hastening to
Mizpeh, expecting to fall upon Judas and his men while
they slept. Judas knew also that the other two generals,
with most of the Syrian force, had encamped at Emmaus
and most likely would be sound asleep. He was hurrying
through the night to hurl his men upon them in their
tents, while Georgias was racing to a deserted Mizpeh!316
But all that Philemon and Judith knew, all that these
thousands knew, was this blind movement through dark-
ness to a destination unknown. And what an incredible
experience it was!-for Philemon at least, an alien, an
Alexandrian, here in the hills of Israel, far from his land
and his people, clasping the hand of a Jewess and march-
ing with her to death. What was the power that drove
these half-naked and half-starved men over stones and
briars, in defense of their faith and their holy places? It
was almost more than he could think of without tears.
Now and then he squeezed Judith's hand and she always
responded with pressure that was firm and strong. He
had had nothing to eat for more than a day and a night
and the pain in his stomach became sharper the longer he
walked, and he thought of the pain in all the stomachs
around him. Bending toward Judith he asked in a whis-
per:
"Are you all right?"
"Yes."
"Does your stomach hurt?"
"A little."
"Do your feet hurt?"
"Not much."
"You'd better eat a few figs or whatever it is you
have."
"Those are for you," she said. Her hand returned
his pressure. "Don't worry about me. I'm all right."
He thought they had been marching about two hours
but everything was lost in a night like this. You lost your
sense of direction, your sense of time and distance; you
lost, almost, your sense of being a man among men. You
were something that moved like the planets, like snow-
slides and landslides, like tides. Your humanity was gone
from you; you walked and walked, one hand clasping the
hand of someone you loved, the other the weapon with
which you expected to kill. With love in one hand and
death in the other you walked through the night, stum-
bling, despairing, half-man and half-beast, with pain
consuming your stomach and bowels, and with pain in317
your mind even worse than that. Why in this night did
two multitudes scurry around in the hope of extermina-
ting one another? Because a few deluded prophets had
deceived and betrayed their people? Because fools like
Reuben and Menelaus could not let their brothers cher-
ish their superstitions, but felt called upon to enlighten
and deliver them? Or was it all deeper and more terrible
than that?
His pressure on Judith's hand brought no response.
"Judith, you all right?"
"Yes."
He thought she sounded weak. A few men had fallen
behind.
"Do you want to rest?"
"No. I wonder where we are."
"On a hill back there I saw a village on our right."
"What would it be?"
"Maybe it was Ajalon. A place called Emmaus is west
of it."
"How far have we come?"
"I've no idea. Are you awfully tired?"
"Not very."
"Does your stomach hurt worse?"
"No, it feels better now."
"Darling, I wish you'd eat something."
Her next words shocked him. "Don't speak gently to
me," she said. "We must get in a mood to kill."
What kind of a world was it in which a lovely and
tender girl walked through a night, clutching a dagger
and making her heart and her will cold for murder? What
debasement was this!
In a voice drained of all emotion she said: "Do you
have your axe?"
"Of course."
"We must be firm. We'll be fighting soon."
"I know."
"We must fight together."
"We'll die together."
"We'll not die. Not tonight." She glanced at him and318
said: "We shouldn't clasp hands now. We must think
of something else."
"It would make no difference to me."
"You're not a woman.'
Well, that was right. She was a woman: her deepest
hunger was to mother and shelter, but she was marching
with her men to kill. She took her hand from him. She
said:
"I think we're getting ready to stop."
She was right. The ragged and hungry and weary
army came to a halt. Judas wanted to speak to his men
but did not dare to address them as a multitude; and so
he spoke to the generals of the thousands, who were his
brothers, and these spoke to the captains of the hun-
dreds and these to the captains of the fifties; and these
spoke to their men, saying that in a few moments they
would fall upon the enemy, who were asleep in their
tents; saying that the Holy One of Israel would go with
them and strengthen their hands; reminding them again
that they were fighting for their homes, their families,
their lives; and exhorting them to fight in a way to honor
their forefathers, to leap with anger and courage, to
smite until not one of the enemy was able to rise.
Then the captain of the fifty among whom were
Philemon and Judith said: "We don't know how many
of the enemy are in this camp. We know that they came
with forty thousand on foot and seven thousand on
horse and with many elephants. A part of the force has
gone to Mizpeh. Before us in the camp there may be
thirty thousand men. That means ten men for every one
of us. When you have slain ten men you have done your
duty. The Lord our God, the Lord is One. We have put
on his spirit as a sword, his power is in our hands. We
now have a few minutes to speak to him privately. May
he bless and strengthen and keep you, every one!"
The men threw themselves down to pray, Philemon
among them, though he had never in his life taken a po-
sition of prayer and did not intend to pray now. And yet
he did pray, as one might who had no faith in gods and319
no belief in a supernatural world: he thought of Socrates,
that brave and golden man, and of the way Socrates
faced death. "I think," he said, slowly gathering the
words and speaking them in a whisper, "I think you
would understand and approve what I am doing. You
would know why I am fighting with these people-to
fight for their right to believe their errors instead of
yours or mine. . . ."

"I will stay at your side."
On the other side of Philemon a voice said: "And I

He thought she was lying.

"Do you know what day this is?"

General Georgias. Hot on the heels of the foe was the
army of Judas, striking down every man it could over-

Philemon and Judith ran after them for half a mile

they stared into the southwest and saw, far to the right
and left and far ahead of them, a demoralized army

pear into a ravine and rise to a hill and vanish, until at

troops were pursued as far as Jamnia, Azotus, and Ga-

He sank to the earth and drew Judith to him and al-
most at once they were asleep.

IT WAS AN ASTOUNDING VICTORY and news of it spread
quickly over this part of the world. Among his men Judas
would henceforth be known as the maccab, the hammer,
for the suddenness and power of his blows, and he and
his brothers would be known as the maccabees. All day
long during this sabbath the Syrian generals were fleeing
up the coast toward Antioch, while Israel's warriors piled
the thousands of dead upon the fires. The main part of
the camp was a ghastly scene and the odor of burning
human flesh was carried on the breeze for miles. There
was labor all day, and feasting and thanksgiving, and
prayers.
Judas set up the tent of a general as his quarters, and
there, brooding alone, he seized upon the thought that
his people not only could be freed of their enemies but
could become a nation among nations, as long ago under
Solomon they had been. This thought he laid before his
brothers. They all knew that the Pious, the faction to
which Hosah and the Joses belonged, would never fight
to make Israel a nation; they would fight, if they fought
at all, only to restore their holy places. While they were
discussing the matter there came to Simon, the shrewd
statesman, an idea; they needed a battle-cry, a war-song,
or some new holy book to unite the people. He was think-
ing of Amiel. He sent an orderly to find Philemon and326
Judith and the man found them on the hill and aroused
them from sleep.
"Lo," he said, "he who keeps Israel shall neither sleep
nor slumber! So it is written."
Feeling dull and foolish Philemon got to his feet.
"What has happened?"
The man gestured toward the fires. "Simon wishes to
see you."
Simon had put up a tent of his own, and when they
appeared he met them at the door, smiling, looking re-
freshed. "Come in," he said. "I want to talk to you.'
They went inside and sat on stools. There was a table
in the tent and on it were food and wines and silver
drinking-cups.
"When these murderers go forth they live well," said
Simon. "Help yourselves. Philemon, do you like wine?"
"Ah, for a cup of wine! What kind is it?"
"We have several kinds. We captured their entire
supply without spilling a drop." Simon filled three cups,
and gave one to Philemon and one to Judith.
Philemon sniffed his wine and sipped it and said: "It's
good wine."
"The food is just as good. Eat. I want to talk to you."
Philemon and Judith helped themselves from the
table. Philemon said: "How many were killed?"
"More than ten thousand."
Philemon looked at Judith. "My people," he said,
"never won such a victory as that."
"I want to talk to you," said Simon, "about a matter
that must remain a secret between us. I'll have to have
your pledge."
"You mean you want me to do something?"
"Yes."
"You don't propose to separate us?"
"Oh no!" cried Simon, smiling. "I have a mission for
both of you."
"Not to the citadel?"
"No. It's not dangerous."327
Philemon flushed. Judith said quickly: "We're not
afraid of danger."
"I know it. You fought well last night. May I have

"Yes," said Philemon.
;
Simon took Philemon's right hand and Judith's and
clasped them between his own. Among his people this
was not the way to solemnize a pledge but Simon was
more statesman than priest. "You are pledged to a
mission for Israel."
Philemon looked at Simon. "All right, what is the
mission ?"
"Do you know Amiel ben Micah?"
"Yes."
"He's living with your brother Hosah," said Simon,
speaking to Judith. "They're in a cave."
"We know the cave," said Judith.
"Amiel, I've heard, is writing a book. It's about some
ancient prophet named Daniel who was filled with the
holy spirit. What we need is the kind of book that will
unite our people and arouse them to superhuman efforts.
So this is your mission. First, you are to do what you can
to get Amiel to write the kind of book we need. Then you
are to help make copies, just as many copies as you can,
and get them to us as soon as you can. We need them. We
need them more than we need swords."
"You mean a book glorifying the deeds of your peo-
ple-such books as Homer wrote ?"
"I haven't read Homer's books. But we do, yes, want
a story that will arouse and unite all of Israel. From
what I've heard Amiel isn't writing that sort of story.
Perhaps you can-well, make him understand what we
need."
"We'll do our best."
"I have some knapsacks filled with food and wine
for you. I want you to leave at once. Eat all you can now,
drink all you want-and then go. And remember always
that another army will come against us as soon as it can328
be organized. Remember that we'll be fighting desper-
ately and against overwhelming odds. So lose no time."
"We'll do our best," said Philemon.
Living back in the cave, which was warm and dry
and free of rodents and insects, Amiel was enjoying
every detail of his vision and shaping it under divine
guidance. Three times each day he prayed and his pray-
ers were long. After prayer he entered into a trancelike
condition in which, coming close to God, he was suffused
and warmed by great truths. But he was not writing the
kind of book which Philemon thought was needed.
Amiel was foretelling that King Antiochus would be
seized by violent death somewhere between the Great
Sea and the Holy Mountains; and after dwelling with
rejoicing on this prophecy and expounding it to his com-
rades, Amiel became convinced that he could go to the
exact spot where the King would die. In this, as in prac-
tically all his prophecies, he was to be wrong; but he
never doubted that the whole future had been opened to
him. His story was overburdened with irrelevant ex-
cursions which his delighted fancy made. His style was
cryptic and secretive and sometimes wholly unintelligible.
For instance, at an imaginary feast of Belshazzar, hand-
writing appeared on the wall. These words appeared:
Mene Mene Tekel Upharsin
They were, in fact, nothing but the names of weights,
mene being the Aramaic equivalent of the Hebraic maneh;
tekel was nothing but a corruption of shekel; and phar-
sin was a corrupt form of a word representing a half-
mina. But Amiel filled them with strange and wondrous
meanings as each suggested to him its etymological affi-
nities. Mina was related to a root word meaning to num-
ber: so for Amiel mene said as plain as day, God had
numbered your kingdom and brought it to an end.
Shekel was related to a root word meaning to weigh: so
tekel came to mean, You have been weighed in the bal-329
ance and been found wanting. A half-mina suggested a
double-play: from this Amiel extracted: Your kingdom
is divided and given to the Persians. His mind was a store-
house of subtle and dark and abstruse images and sym-
bols and fragments of words and phrases, of parable, of
incomprehensive jargon, of homonyms and conundrums
and riddles, allegories and fables, all mixed up in a gor-
geous and dazzling illumination that enfolded him when
he settled into one of his trances.
Philemon thought the book ought to glorify the ex-
ploits of David; but Judith said: "We must not upset
him. Let's see first what he's writing."
This is what Amiel was writing. A part of his
story was hortatory narrative, a part was prophetic vi-
sions. Daniel and three other noble Jews were captured
when Nebuchadnezzar seized Jerusalem. While they
were trained in the language and customs of their con-
querors they kept themselves undefiled, eating pulse and
water instead of the King's fare. All four were distin-
guished for their wisdom, Daniel being remarkably skilled
in the interpretation of visions and dreams. So it was that
when the King dreamed dreams which his wisest men
could not explain, Daniel was summoned, and interpreted
the dreams with such ease that the King was astounded.
The King dreamed, for instance, of an enormous and
grotesque image, the head being of gold, the breast and
arms of silver, the body of brass, the legs of iron, the
feet of iron and clay. A stone, cut without hands, fell
and shattered the image, which thereupon became a moun-
tain, spreading over the earth. When the wisest men in
Chaldea were unable to tell the meaning of this, Daniel
was called; and he explained that the image represented
the anti-theocratic power that filled the earth, that the
four chief parts stood for the four empires, the Chaldean,
the Median, the Persian and the Macedonian, the latter
including the Roman. The stone, cut without hands, like
Jerusalem's holy altar, was the kingdom of God in Israel,
before which all nations ultimately would fall. . . .
It was Amiel's purpose to show, not only that the sons330
of Israel were much wiser than other men, but that, hav-
ing the only true god, they could not be harmed. Daniel's
companions were cast into a raging furnace but emerged
unscathed; and Daniel was hurled into a den of lions
but the beasts would not touch him. Through the story,
as Amiel unfolded it, the King's astonishment increased,
until at last he commanded all men to worship the God
of Israel.
For Philemon it was a childlike story written for chil-
dren, or a story by a lunatic written for madmen. He was
by turns shocked, disgusted, and wearied. He took Ho-
sah aside.
"Judas," he said, "had a great and glorious victory—
such a victory perhaps as no military leader ever had
before. Another army, a bigger army, will soon come.
Every able-bodied man in Israel must join Judas—and
even then the odds will be ten to one against us. But if
we have a story, a kind of battle-cry, a recital of glorious
deeds of the past—”
"No," said Hosah, shaking his head. "The Holy One
does not order it that way."
"Why not? We want a saga of the heroic deeds of
David. . . . or of Samson. Hosah, that is what we need."
"No."
"These men fighting with Judas aren't going to believe
that flame wouldn't burn a Jew! All this fantastic stuff-
beasts coming out of the sea, a lion with eagle wings, a
leopard with four heads!"
"Those are parables," said Hosah. "Jews talk in
parables. Besides, you forget that the Holy One of
Israel sits on a throne of Flame, his hair glorious like
white wool, his raiment snowlike, surrounded by myriads
of attendants, and with the firmanent-"
"Yes, yes! But do you mean to tell me that Jews will
believe that lions wouldn't eat Daniel or that fire
wouldn't burn Azariah?”
"Those things are true," said Hosah gravely. "They
happened."
Philemon was looking at him with astonishment.331

"Yes," said Amiel.
"All things," said Hosah, "are possible with the Holy

Amiel then interpreted the words. All kingdoms would

pass away, but the kingdom of Israel's God would not
pass away, but would endure from everlasting to ever-
lasting.
Who was the one like unto a son of man?
He was, said Amiel, a symbolic figure for Israel. The
other kingdoms were represented as beasts. In compar-
ison with them Israel was human, and the humanity of
this kingdom was indicated by the figure of one who was
like a man, indeed, like a son of man. The expression son
of man, said Ben Joezer, meant simply man.
"And how soon are all these wonders to come about?"
"Soon."
"You mean your Holy One will establish this king-

"Yes."
"Then why should Judas and his men fight and die?"
There was no need to, Amiel said; and the other
faces gravely nodded in assent.
"You men really believe that?"
"We know it," said Hosah. "There's no need to
fight. The Holy One, coming with the onrushing power

"And you tell me this is to be soon."
"Yes."
"You mean next month?-next year?—or the next
century?"
"In this generation," said Amiel.
"Then you would have me say to Judas, Lay down
your arms. The Holy One will establish his kingdom when
he gets ready. You're wasting your time and your lives.
Is that what you would have me tell him?"
"Yes."
"Then by God you're not worthy sons of Israel!"

WHILE PHILEMON AND JUDITH were with Hosah and his
comrades, Lysias, the Syrian governor between the Euph-
rates and the Nile, led a great army against Judas. In-
stead of approaching from the north or west he went
down the coast and came up through Idumea to Bethzur,
an outpost twenty miles south of Jerusalem. There Judas
flung his forces against him, and after a terrific struggle,
with heavy losses on both sides, defeated him and drove
him back.
Lysias returned to Antioch to raise another army.
Judas, after burying his dead and caring as well as he337
could for his wounded, marched into Jerusalem to re-
store the temple. Heralds went over Israel to carry the
glad tidings, and the pious, like Hosah and his friends,
came out of hiding and returned to the city. With them.
went Philemon and Judith.
Such frenzied and continuous lamenting Philemon had
never heard before. The gates had been burned, the altars
profaned, the holy of holies desecrated. The whole city
was desolate but for the citadel, in which there was still
a Syrian garrison. The people tore their garments and
filled the sky with their cries. They filled their hair with
ashes, they flung themselves to the earth and moaned
and wept and prayed; they arose from the earth, their
faces distorted with grief, and wailed toward the hea-
vens, begging forgiveness for their sins. The trumpets
of alarm were sounded. The wise men talked to the
multitudes, calling on them for courage and faith. It was
the season of rain. Most of the people were wet and
chilled and they wandered disconsolate through the courts,
looking upon the emptiness of their temple. When they
shook their heads in despair ashes fell to their cloaks.
No ark of the covenant was there, no Aaron's rod, no pot
of manna, no cherubim, no tablets of the law. The fire
that had come down from heaven to light the altar had
gone out long ago. Ruins were everywhere, and over-
looking the ruins was the citadel.
But in spite of rain and cold and wretchedness and
hunger, most of the men were as busy as men could be.
Judas laid siege to the citadel to keep its soldiers inside.
He bade Hosah and other wise men to seek among the
multitudes for priests without blemish. Men began to
cleanse the holy places, to make new vessels and lamps
and veils. The altar of the holocaust had been polluted:
it could not be used, yet Judas did not dare to throw, it
away. The wise men decided to take the altar apart,
stone by stone, and put it in a safe place, to await the
coming of a prophet who would determine what was to
be done with it. A new altar was built, a new table of the
Presence. The people labored like creatures driven to338
get everything ready for a dedication and a rejoicing,
and while some repaired the physical structures, others,
like Hosah, instructed priests and Levites in their du-
ties. Judas wanted the celebration to be as joyous as that
of the feast of tabernacles, but it was out of the question
to have the twenty-four courses of the priests on duty;
for some were dead, some were hiding, and some had
gone over to the enemy. A large staff was needed to pre-
pare the incense and the bread, to be sure that all copies
of the Torah were correctly copied, to inspect the sacri-
ficial beasts, to oversee the priests.
...
"We'll do the best we can," said Hosah to Judas.
"What do we lack?"
"White robes, a full choir, musical instruments, ma-
terials for veils and incense-"
"Do the best you can.'
""
These were the weeks just before the winter solstice;
rain fell every day and the nights were cold. The enemy
had burned so many houses or parts of them for fuel
that many people were without shelter night and day.
Many of them had little to eat. Judas' army, numbering
thousands, was encamped by the city, chiefly in tents,
The soldiers were under-nourished. Packtrains came
in daily with supplies, and cooks in improvised kitchens
toiled all day long to feed the multitudes. Though some
starved, and few had enough to eat, Philemon heard no-
body complain. He saw neither despair nor defeat in
any face. For the heroic efforts of these people his ad-
miration was boundless and he felt proud to be one a-
mong them.
Judith was assigned to the task of making a part of a
curtain. Philemon had found for her a tiny hut on a
hillside; most of its roof had been torn away, its floor
was mud, its doorway stood open to the winds. But he
brought in some stones to form a kind of brazier and
he found some pieces of wood; and once in a while,
when the weather was too bitter, he fetched embers from
a distant hearth and made a small fire. A part of his
time he prowled through the city trying to find food for339
her. Simon's tavern had been destroyed, and all the ta-
verns around it. All the merchants on the western hill
had fled.
One afternoon in the month of Kislev, when a miser-
able persistent rain was falling and a chilling wind was
blowing from the northwest, Philemon sat with Judith
in the hut and kept up the tiny fire. She was sitting on a
stone bench with a great blanket wrapped around her.
Her exposed hands were busy with a piece of curtain. Her
face was thin and white. He had found some dates and
barley and these Judith had gravely divided, forcing
him to take half. They were chewing kernels of barley
and she was weaving purple threads into the fabric. He
was watching her hands and warming his own hands at
the fire. It occurred to him that he should roast the bar-
ley, and so he placed a spoonful on a hot stone and
moved the kernels with a stick, turning them and stirring
them.
Judith looked up at him and asked: "Have you seen
Angela ?"
"No. Have you?"
"Once."

"Yes."
"Did she speak?"
"No."

The morning of the 25th of Kislev had been chosen
for the first offering and thanksgiving. According to cus-342
tom, the chief of the course and the 'heads of families'
reclined on temple benches during the night; and some
of the priests, having folded their garments beside them,
lay on the floor. But this night was so bitterly cold that
they left the temple courts, and only guards remained
there.
Two hours past midnight Hosah left his shelter and
stood in a white world. Snow was falling in large dry
flakes. There was no wind. All around Hosah the ground
was white. At the temple gate he knocked and was ad-
mitted, and when all the priests had come before him he
spoke to them, saying: “All who have washed, come and
cast lots." The priests stood in a circle around him under
the falling snow of an open court; and Hosah removed
the headdress of one to indicate that the counting would
begin with him. Because it was unlawful to count persons
in Israel, the priests held up two or five or any other
number of fingers to ten, and Hosah counted the fingers.
When he had counted to seventy the lot fell on that
priest.
And so he chose priests to cleanse the altars and pre-
pare the fires, and these went forth and washed their
hands and feet. While his assistants waited the priest
first in rank took a chafing dish and scraped the altar,
removing its embers to an area just north of it. Hosah
came to inspect the wood, which could be neither olive
nor vine.
When the second lot was cast, the one on whom it
fell, and the twelve who stood nearest him, cleansed the
candlestick and the altar of incense. Serving as the presi-
dent of the course Hosah directed a priest to climb to
an eminence and study the light. A little later this
priest called down: "The morning shines!"
"Is the sky lit as far as Hebron?"
"Yes."
Hosah knew that the only light in the sky was that
of snowflakes. "Do you announce that the morning
comes ?"

"You may descend."

Hosah now ordered a lamb to be brought from a
chamber where it had been closeted for four days. It
was watered from a bowl and Hosah himself examined
it for blemishes. Then the sacrificing priest tied it to the
second of the rings on the north side of the great altar.
Its forefeet and hindfeet were bound together, its head
was laid toward the south, facing the west, and a priest
stood on either side. The elders who carried the keys
now gave orders to open the temple gates. When the
last gate moved on its hinges, priests gave three blasts
on their horns, summoning the Levites to their duties
and announcing that morning offering would now be made.
The signal for the slaying of the lamb was the opening
of the gates to the holy place to admit priests to cleanse
the candlestick and the altar of incense.
Hosah was inwardly rejoicing. The priests had been
well-instructed and not one of them faltered or fumbled.
Though they were cold and their naked feet were freez-
ing they moved with precision in their routines, accord-
ing to the holy formulas of their fathers.
The thousands who had gathered at the gates before
daylight now came in, surging across the court of gen-
tiles and filling the court of women. Philemon and Judith
were among the fortunate ones who stood on the eastern
steps of this court, and so were able to overlook a part
of the other courts and see what the priests were doing
there. They stood close together with one large blanket
covering them like a collapsed tent.
While one priest thrust a knife into the throat of the
lamb another held a basin to catch its blood. He sprinkled
a part of the blood on the northwest corner and then
on the southwest corner of the altar, below a red line
which circled the altar midway. The remainder of the
blood was poured out at the altar base. With Hosah's
watchful eyes upon them two priests had entered the
holy place, the one to remove embers and ashes, the other
to trim and refill the lamps. In the court of priests
the beast was hung from a hook and flayed and cut up344
according to the holy rules, the entrails being drawn
and washed in the court fountain. The thigh-piece was
cut out and wrapped with the entrails and sheathed with
fat. These a priest laid on a huge platter and sprinkled
with incense; whereupon, with naked feet, he climbed
the altar steps and cast them upon a fire.
When Hosah and his assistants gathered for the third
lot he offered a thanksgiving, speaking through a great
resonant horn to carry his voice to the multitudes. "With
great love you have loved us, O Lord, and with much
overflowing pity you have pitied us, our Father and our
King, for the sake of our fathers who trusted in you,
and you taught them the statutes of life! Have mercy
upon us and enlighten our eyes that we in love may praise
you and your Oneness! Blessed be the Holy One, who
in love chose his people Israel!"
According to the custom, Hosah, or someone chosen
by him, should now have recited the Ten Command-
ments and the Shema; but for a people miserable in the
storm and cold these would have taken too long. Hosah
hastened to cast the lot for those who would burn the
incense; and a little later, when these priests struck an
instrument called a magrephah, priests from all parts
of the temple rushed forth to their duties, and the Levites
ascended the steps to take their place in the choir. It
was full daylight now but the world was blanketed and
dim.
Hosah signaled that the time of incense had come.
The multitude prostrated itself, spreading hands in silent
prayer. Taken by surprise, Philemon stood, and Judith
by him, while the thousands descended to their faces;
and when he looked at all these people kneeling in snow

"Chaos to you is order: in your eyes

With the odors of incense rising in the storm those
standing on the steps of the Levites began to sing. There
was a burst of music, from harp and lute and cymbal.
A hush followed. Philemon observed that all the priests
now faced the east, that the Levites faced the west. He
saw Hosah come forward and stand beside those who

"Judge me, O Lord, for I have walked in my integ-
