This concluded the morning service. Speaking through346
his horn, Hosah announced that all the people assem-
bled would now sing. Boys ran through the multitude,
telling those beyond Hosah's voice which song was to
be sung. When the signal was given and the singing com-
menced, Philemon was astonished, for never at the sanc-
tuary of any other god had he beheld such a scene or
heard such singing. The deep tones of the priests rose
high and full, supported by the music; and above these
was the treble of the Levite children's voices, mixed with
the purer music from the throats of women. Then, hav-
ing caught the spirit and the words, the whole enormous
multitude began to sing, and there rose to the storm
and the sky the sublime faith of a people.
Philemon did not sing. Strangely and deeply moved,
he listened to Judith's voice and to the music and the
singing, and he looked at the people and the falling snow.
The kerchiefs on the heads below him were white. The
multitude was white and the whole world roundabout.
Rising into the storm was the smoke from the altar fires
and the incense and the waves of heat. Here, it seemed
to him, was a nobler religion than he had found in any
other people; here was a more enduring faith. He looked
at Judith, and aware of his glance she squeezed his
hand. She was singing in a clear sweet voice. Over and
over, like a people drunk with joy or faith or swept up
and warmed by a great singleness of purpose, the multi-
tude sang the verses of a psalm; and when at last a trum-
pet silenced them, Hosah lifted his resonant horn and
prayed:
"You are our God and the God of our fathers, our
Rock and our salvation: appoint peace, goodness, and
blessing; grace, mercy, and compassion for all of us! Bless
us, our Father in heaven, all of us as one, with the light
of your countenance! For in your light have you given
us the law of life, and loving mercy, and righteousness!
May our God bless us and keep us! May he make his
face shine upon us and be gracious unto us! May he lift
up his countenance to us and give us peace!"
And most of the multitude responded: "Blessed be347
the Lord, the God of Israel, from everlasting to ever-
lasting!"
Philemon looked again at Judith. Her eyes were shin-
ing. Her lips were moist and red. She smiled at him, and
for the first time in many months he saw in her face
the old mischief and mirth.
The multitude now surged forward to make offerings,
or if they had nothing to offer, to touch the altar, to pray.
The storm had increased in intensity. A world of white
was falling upon a people who stood wet and shivering
under the sky and the cold, asking in this bitter year for
the blessing of goodness and peace.

FOR EIGHT DAYS the people rejoiced in the restoration
of their temple but Judas did not linger with his people.
Knowing that another and even larger Syrian army was
being gathered and that time pressed hard against him,
he moved with extraordinary vigor to rebuild the walls,
to erect towers, to refortify Bethzur on the southern ap-
proach, and in all possible ways to strengthen his posi-
tion. The arrogant citadel stood in the midst of his city
but he did not have the siege machinery to reduce it.
Then there came evil tidings. In all the areas round-
about the people hated the Jews and were taking up.
weapons against them. Judas sent a force among the
marauding Arabs and Idumeans. A second force over-
whelmed the Balanites and burned many of them alive
in their towers. A third destroyed the principal villages
of the Ammonites. These forays were interrupted by the
Passover in the month Nisan; and during this celebra-
tion Philemon again looked with amazement upon the
religious ardor of these people.
All of them by this time had heard the story of Daniel348
and the three other Jews who had been wiser than all
the men of Babylon. They had also been told that the
coming of a son of David, a mighty and invincible king,
clothed with the powers of God, was drawing near. Amiel
himself said so, and for most of them Amiel was a
prophet. Some thought that Moses was coming, some,
Elijah, and still others, David himself; and a rumor
spread that this great one would come during this Pass-
over season. The hope became a burning fever that quick-
ened pulses and lifted eyes constantly to the heavens,
and by Passover day there were few who did not expect
his appearance. But whether he would come like a light
and like thunder out of the East, or on clouds of glory,
or riding on an ass, or whether he would rise from some
ancient tomb, nobody knew. Philemon saw thousands
watching the sky above the hills of Moab, and it pained
him to think of the bitter disillusionment which Amiel's
wild lunacies were making of the insubstantial stuff of
hope and dreams.
"When," he said to Judith, "they realize that no such
mighty king is coming what will they do? Will despair
cast them down?"
"No," said Judith, who knew her people. "Then
they'll expect him on the ninth of Ab; and if he doesn't
come, then on the day of atonement; and then at the
next Passover."
"And when he doesn't come then?"
"Then the next ninth of Ab-"
"Year after year?"
"Year after year.'
"And the longer he delays the more sinful they'll think
they are."
"Until at last-?"
"God knows."
The spring and summer of this year were an unhappy
time for Philemon. His colder emotional nature and
his clearer mind were irked and sometimes shocked and
sickened by the hysterical enthusiasms, the frenzied re-
joicings, the interminable praying and the fanatical zeal349
of these people who, having recovered their sanctuary,
and taking daily encouragement from the incredible fan-
tasies of Amiel, believed that they would soon be the
masters of the world. Calmer heads, it is true, like Simon,
hoped for nothing so spectacular as that; but the great
majority thought it would be only a few months, or in
any case not more than a few years, before Israel would
stand upon his enemies, making a footstool of all men.
For so it was written. This wild zeal pleased Judas and
supported his plan. It was his plan to make Israel an
independent nation and to restore the legendary glories
of the time of Solomon. Toward that goal he moved as
a superb military genius, ably assisted by his brothers.
But the people knew nothing of his plans. Page by page
the book written by Amiel had been read to them, and
to its fantastic imaginings they added their own. They
met in groups and exchanged views, fertilizing myth and
legend with fear and hope, until Daniel in the lion's
den and his companions in the furnace became dull and
commonplace by comparison with the greater marvels
which went from tongue to tongue. Any man could be-
lieve anything he could conceive of and there was none
to dispute him. Some said that the mighty king had come
and was at the moment running Antiochus down to slay
him; and then the king was in Rome and Rome's walls
were falling. It was said that he fought with lightning
and thunderbolts, in the way of Israel's God, and rode
across the sky on a white stallion. It was said that when
he came to Jerusalem, having destroyed all the far-flung
people, the earth would split open, and the Idumeans
and Ammonites and Balanites and Nabateans and Phoe-
nicians and Syrians would vanish like rats into crevices.
There would be earthquakes and volcanoes and engulf-
ing winds, the moon would turn to blood and spill, the
sun would race across the sky. Everything that fevered
imaginations could invent was said and everything was
believed.
And standing in the mass lunacy like an indestructible
irony of beams and stone was the citadel. The temple had350
been fortified. Now the temple and the citadel faced
one another and fought skirmishes almost daily. Phil-
emon was again living with Hosah and his comrades in
a cave. He slept alone on a straw mat at a little distance
from Judith. Because the Romans were now overrunning
the cities of Greece he supposed that he had long ago
lost his fortune and his fine private library; and here he
had lost all that he had brought with him, including his
copies of Epicurus. He was a ragged and unkempt and
unhappy man. He took his turn in standing guard among
those who surrounded the citadel; and when not engaged
there he talked with Judith or went off alone by himself to
brood. His love for Judith was untouched by hardship
and despair, not in these months for her beauty, which
was fading with starvation and the bitter years, but for
her dedicated and dauntless spirit. He loved her, but
there were times when he wished that he had never come
to this city. He was devoted to her, but the fanaticism
and intolerance around him were such corrosion in his
mind and soul that he looked forward to the day when
Judas would march again.
And so he lived until a warm moonless night early in
Tishri.
A little before dark he had kissed Judith and gone
for a walk, telling her that he would be back soon. One
thing led to another, all of them trivial, until, hours later,
he stood alone on the summit of the Mount of Olives,
looking across the dim night-hidden desolation toward
Moab. He had, as was his custom, one hand on his dag-
ger haft, and he was thinking that it would be pleasant
to spend some night here with Judith among the olive
trees...
With which of his senses he first realized that he was
in danger he could never have said. Had he heard a
faint sound? Had he smelled a human odor? Whatever
it was, a moment flooded his senses with light, he knew,
he swung, dagger in hand, his arm making a wide arc.
In the next instant he saw that two persons had crept

SOME CALLED IT the Day of Horn Blowing; some, the
Day of Blowing the Alarm; and still others the Great
day or simply the Day. It was their Day of Atonement,
their day of judgment, when they passed like sheep be-
fore their God and were judged. The first of Tishri was
New Year's Day; and between this and the tenth, the
Day, the very pious did not eat at all. Trumpets and
horns were blown all day long the first day and every day
until the time of horn-blowing was past. Those who blew
horns were placed between those who blew trumpets, and
the sound of the horn, in the hands of a skillful blower,
was prolonged beyond that of the trumpet; but on the
days of fasting those with trumpets stood between those
with horns, and the sound of the trumpet was prolonged.
It was a time not only of horn-blowing and fasting but
of long and earnest praying in the synagogues, of prepara-
tions for the new year, of looking back upon the sins of
the old year. Above all, it was the time when the sins
of Israel were transferred to the goat for Azazel, which
was then taken into the wilderness and shoved backward
off a precipice.
Autumn had come again and the nights were chilly.
Hosah and his comrades were living in a dry cave in the
eastern hill, and it was there that he instructed a priest
named David, who had been chosen to act as the High
Priest, in his duties. During the whole night preceding
the Day it was customary to read or sing to him or shout
at him and pinch him or make him stand with naked
feet on cold stones; for under no circumstances was he
to fall asleep or doze. As a matter of fact, said Judith,
none of the most devout Jews would sleep that night.360
Philemon observed what was done and tried to under-
stand the symbolisms.
The High Priest bathed himself five times and washed
his hands and feet ten times. Each time he bathed, a cur-
tain of the finest linen was hung between him and his peo-
ple. When he put on his golden vestments he was suppos-
ed to wash his hands and feet in a basin of gold; and he
was to put on golden robes, with a diadem of gold on his
head and golden bells hanging from the hem of his gar-
ment. The gold, Philemon supposed, was a symbol of the
sun, and he wondered if the Jews had once been sun-
worshipers. He imagined that their obsessive interest
in water went back to their centuries in the desert. The
robe of the ephod was woven of dark blue, which ob-
viously symbolized the sky. The breastplate was in four
sacred colors; blue for the sky, white for purity, scarlet
for blood and purple for what? Why were there seventy-
two bells? Judith said it was because there were seventy-
two nations in the world.
Hosah, almost exhausted by fasting and praying, and
looking more and more as the days passed like a frail
image consumed by holiness, explained some matters to
Philemon. Before New Year's Day sweets were set on
the tables and the people ate nothing sour, thus insuring
the sweetness of the new year. They brought much bread
into their dwellings to indicate that their houses during
the new year would be well-filled. Bread was cast upon
the waters to placate evil spirits.
"And you actually believe," said Philemon, staring at
him, "that when a rooster crows in the morning he fright-
ens evil away?"
"Yes," said Hosah gravely. "Particularly if he crows
three times."
"And you think there is not atonement but by blood?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
"So it is written."
So it is written! Philemon shuddered at the power of
words over minds. The soul of every living thing, said861
Hosah, was in its blood. The blood of the sinner ought
to be shed and his body burned, but the Holy One, great
and merciful, now accepted a substitute.
"You mean this goat for Azazel?"
"Yes."
"Who is this Azazel?"
"A prince of wickedness out in the wilderness."
"You put two fillets of red wool on the goat. Did you
know that the Babylonians dress the goat in red?"
Hosah's smile was indulgent. "A lot of people," he
said, "have copied our ways but they don't understand
them."
In such moments Philemon wanted to leap up and

"My dear Philemon, we know that they are."

"So it is written."
"Oh, great God! If you don't stop repeating those
words I'll choke you."
Hosah looked alarmed. "You'd better leave me," he

"People used to kill their kings. They learned to offer

She was looking at him and weeping. "You must come

"Of course I'll come with you. But are you sure of

"Yes, I'm sure."
They stayed awake all night before the Day, and a
little before daylight they left the cave and went to the
temple. Then, in one of the courts, Philemon stopped in
amazement, with hot angry blood rushing to his face.
The more fanatical and emotional Jews were flogging
one another. They were beating one another with thick
leather ropes and tough green vines. Those rolling on
the earth were howling with pain and scratching with
their fingers like tormented cats. They were frothing,
their eyes were wild and rolling; and while they screamed
in their violent efforts toward self-surrender their zealous
brothers stood above them and smote with all their might
and the blows echoed in the temple walls.
"I'm going to stop this!" cried Philemon.
Judith seized his arm. "No!"
"They're mad!"
"Yes, darling, they're mad."363
"Why are they doing this?"
"For their sins."
"Oh, their sins! Their imaginary sins! Their fantasies
and illusions and morbid imaginings!"
"Come!" she said, trying to draw him away.
"I've an urge to seize a club and beat the infernal
daylights out of them !"
"Philemon, please come away." She was pulling at
his arm.
"I'm mad," he said.
"We're all mad."

When, half-turning, hesitating, he looked back at the

"What is in men ?" he asked. "There-don't worry,

Seven days before the Day, David had moved to a
chamber in the temple area. He was sprinkled with the
ashes of a red heifer. He spent the week memorizing his
lines and practicing his duties.
When it was announced that light was visible as far
as Hebron the services were begun. Philemon was stand-
ing with Judith high on the north wall, overlooking the
courts. It had been a warm night. It was a pleasant morn-
ing. While they watched and waited, David was bathing
and putting on the golden vestments. Ater washing his
hands and feet he performed the principal part of the
ordinary morning service. He then washed his hands and
feet again, put off his golden garments, bathed, put on
linen, again washed his hands and feet, and proceeded to
the second part of the service.
Upon the head of a small bull used as a sin offering
David laid both hands and said in a loud voice: "Ah,

transgressions before the Name you shall be cleansed!"
At the word Name the multitude again fell to the earth.
In the eastern part of the court of priests stood an urn

stone was the Rock on which the Name had revealed him-

Judith was now shaking uncontrollably-and so were

WHEN IT WAS LEARNED that the Syrians were gather-
ing another huge army many of the people asked why the
Holy One did not intervene. Amiel laid the blame on
the angels, the administrators of the divine judgments.
Ben Johanan said that Israel had been very wicked and
would be punished seventy times seven. Hosah had an-
other explanation. He was convinced that the Kingdom
of Heaven would soon appear on earth, and that even
then the Almighty was summoning his powers. Judas told
his men that they would serve God with his praises in
their mouth and a sword in their hand; but Hosah said
that God needed no politicians like Simon and Jonathan,368
no generals like Judas, no weapons, no armies. In his own
time and in ways that would shake the foundations of
the universe Israel's Lord would smite the heathen and
establish his people on the summit of the world.
"It might be tomorrow," he told those who came to
inquire. "It will be soon."
Judas had another sort of mind. Energetic, aggressive,
he thought of himself as God's instrument and avenger.
He would have agreed with Hosah that the Name could
destroy the world, even the universe, merely by uttering
a word-for by uttering a word he had created it. He
could paralyze Lysias and leave his armies rotting in the
field. But he chose, for reasons of his own, to let Judas
and his men destroy the enemy, and it was an assign-
ment that Judas liked.
After the Day it rained for weeks. The moment the
rains ceased Judas laid siege to the citadel. He had no
siege machinery-no heavy battering rams, no engines
to hurl stones and firepots and huge javelins, and no
caldrons of oil to kindle and pour on the enemy. He
would starve and choke them until like skeletons they
came crawling forth and then he would pile them up and
burn them alive.
"That dog Menelaus!" he cried, unaware that Mene-
laus had slipped away to Antioch and soon would be
murdered by the masters he had served.
The citadel was surrounded with armed men who
stood watch day and night. Philemon was assigned to the
second of the night watches. Armed with a dagger and a
short heavy sword taken from a dead Syrian, he paced
a watch of about a hundred feet. The spring months
passed, the summer months, and when another autumn
came those in the citadel tried to send a message to
Antioch.
The time was an hour past midnight and it was very
dark. Philemon was pacing back and forth, with a small
horn hanging from his neck, like that worn by all the
watchmen. He paced until he met the watchman on his
right and then the watchman on his left. He was midway369
in his watch when he heard a sound and at once he drew
both sword and dagger, meanwhile peering in the direc-
tion of the citadel. He waited and peered and listened.
Imagining that he had been deceived he was about to
sheathe his weapons when he heard the sound again—
and this time there could be no doubt of its nature. Before
him in the darkness was a person who was trying des-
perately to keep from coughing. All over Philemon's
back and neck he felt the vivid sensation of alarm and
fear. Should he challenge the man or wait for him to
reveal himself? Reflecting that there might be a dozen or
a score of men crawling toward him he lifted his small
trumpet and blew a shrill note. In the next moment he
heard a man leap to his feet.
"Halt!" cried Philemon. "Who is there?" Armed
men had come running and Philemon was now supported

RUMORS ABOUT KING ANTIOCHUS spread through the
city, saying that he had been strangled while plundering
a temple, that he had burst open with boils, that he had
died of a broken heart. In any case he was dead. He left
a young son, whom Lysias proclaimed king, with him-
self as regent; and the coronation was barely over when
a messenger from the citadel managed to reach Antioch.
Lysias then raised an immense army, with strong forces
of cavalry and many elephants, and again went down the
coast and turned and came up against Bethzur, to which
he laid siege. Judas had to raise his siege of the citadel
and march against Lysias with all the strength he could
muster. The two armies met at Beth Zacharias, just north
of Bethzur, and in the bloodiest and most desperate
battle since Mattathias raised the banners of revolt
Judas was beaten. Many of his men died that day, in-
cluding Eleazar, first of the Maccabean brothers to fall.
Many were left wounded on the field, where the Syrian
hordes swept over them. Judas did well to retire with
his remnants to the temple courts, which he hastened to
garrison and strengthen.376
It was just at dusk when the terrible news came to the
cave where Philemon lay. Five weeks had passed since
his duel. He was lying on a mat with Judith sitting by
him when a man rushed into the cave crying that Judas
was beaten, that the Syrian hordes were rushing to the
city. In this time it was the custom to destroy captured
cities with torture, fire and the sword, though first the
invading army raped and plundered.
Hosah and Amiel and the graybeards flung themselves
down in prayer. Judith leapt up. When the man said that
the remnants of Israel's army were filling the temple
courts, hoping to stand a siege there, she looked down
at her wounded lover.
"Philemon, can you walk?"
Philemon said he could walk and he tried to sit up. The
wound in his cheek had drawn the flesh away from his
left eye, making it look hideous; it had drawn the left
corner of his mouth down, affecting his tongue and lips
and forcing upon him halting speech.
"We must go," said Judith. "Will the courts hold all
of us?"
The man did not know.
"If we stand!" she said. "Philemon, we must hurry, so
we can find a place to spread your mat."
"Do we leave them ?" he asked, indicating the men in
prayer.
Hosah and his comrades had their faces pressed to the
floor and were swaying from side to side and calling on
the Holy One to come to their rescue.
"They'll try to hide," Judith said.
"They'll pray all night."
"And then all day!"
"They won't be alive all day.”
She went to Hosah and knelt and shook him but he did
not hear. She put her lips to his ear and shouted but he

Inside the temple walls were several thousand men,
some of them wounded, all of them stunned by the defeat.
The people of the city were pouring in through the gates.
Judith helped Philemon up the steps of the Levites and

"Or drink. God knows."
"Judas can't store much water in here."

There was great shouting and confusion in the temple
courts. Until midnight the people came in, and then the
huge gates were closed. Israel had withdrawn into the
holy place of his God to make what some of them believed
would be his last stand. Philemon was thinking of the
tremendous problems that faced Judas and his brothers,
and wondering how, even with such genius as theirs,
they could pull victory out of these pathetic ruins. Judith
was thinking of the wound in Philemon's shoulder, which
throbbed under her hand, and of the hideous scar that
lay across his cheek and throat. He had drawn the flesh
together and used small forked sticks to clutch and hold
it while it healed. During these last weeks Judith had
gone over the city and into the country to beg or steal
food for him, knowing that he would have to be well-
nourished if he were to survive fever and loss of blood.
That is what he had told her and he was a physician.
She had starved herself to give to him. And again and
again during this long night she drew a little food from379
her hoard and forced him to eat. He would say, "You
must eat with me," and Judith would pretend to chew.
But she did not deceive him. For a while she had deceived
him, but one day, studying her pale, thin face, he had
guessed the truth.
Sometime after midnight the dreadful confusion was
hushed. Weary men, exhausted by fighting, had sunk to
the earth to sleep. Judas had had a barrier flung up before
the holy place and it was empty. Everywhere thousands
lay or sat, degraded because they could not privately take
care of their needs, shamed because they were housed
like beasts, dispirited because their God had deserted
them. Before morning Philemon slept but Judith did not
sleep. She lay close to him in an effort to give him her
warmth, for the night was damp and cold. She lay against
his left side with her left arm across him; and she shiver-
ed, trying, as people will who are freezing, to gather
heat from a sudden constriction of the body's muscles.
And she was thinking. . .
...
She was thinking how strange it was that she was
lying with a son of the no-gods in the holy court of the
priests, to which the uncircumcised were never admitted;
how strange that a daughter of Israel should be so deeply
in love with a heathen. She thought it was even stranger
that a Hellene so loved a Jewess that he lost his fortune
for her and almost his life, and would lose his life certain-
ly before he was done with it. She thought how sweet
and good it was that they could lie, as now, close together
for a little while in peace; how holy it was that they had
learned to trust one another, who at the beginning had
been so suspicious; how noble it was of him to cast his
lot with a people hated and despised. She remembered
now so many things that he had said-
"I suppose the chief difference between your people
and mine is this, that yours got the notion that they have

have none. A people with such a book are likely to be-
come stereotypes, a people without it, chameleons. The380
Jew needs the impulse toward change, the Greek some
kind of tradition to govern and direct the impulse. . . ."
She remembered: "God, my dear Judith, is nothing
more than that impulse toward goodness in the souls of
people. Sin is merely the denial of it.”
She patted him, remembering.
The days spent in the sanctuary were a nightmare of

Persons were dying—the old, the weak, the wounded.
After three weeks people began to die from nothing but
want of food. Judith had been out in the court of gen-

No matter where she went she saw scenes that sickened

In another day or two, Philemon thought she would
be gone. She would be mad. Most of the people here
would go mad and would fight one another and die. And
so indeed it would have been but for what nearly all of
them accepted as the clear intervention of their God.
This is what happened.
Finding himself with death, King Antiochus had ap-
pointed one Philip as guardian over his son and as the
temporary ruler of the kingdom. Philip had raised an
army to march against the usurper Lysias and destroy
him. When Lysias heard of this he quickly made peace
with Judas, though he did not give up the citadel. He
did promise to Israel complete freedom of conscience
and worship, and he did say that he would return no more.
Never again would Israel be molested as long as it was the
obedient subject of Antioch.
Judas had been negotiating for several days. When383
an agreement was reached with Lysias, heralds announced

She knew where she was but she was too weak to rise.

like a man at all.

"Like one of Amiel's angels."

TO THEIR AMAZEMENT they found Hosah and his com-
rades in the cave. They had hidden somewhere in it and
somehow had managed to find enough food to keep
alive; and they were still living in their childlike faith
that God would soon deliver Israel. The Jews might
now have lived in peace, at least for a while, but for the
ambitions of Judas and his brothers. They were deter-
mined to destroy all Jews with Hellenic sympathies, and
Lysias had not reached Antioch before civil war again
engulfed Jerusalem. Alcimus, the new high priest, was
resolved to exterminate Judas and his brothers, and be-
cause he came from an old and distinguished line of
priests, Hosah and his comrades were willing to accept
him. Disgusted with them, Philemon and Judith left
the cave and found a small underground shelter where
they could be alone.
In Antioch affairs were in a frightful turmoil. One
Demetrius, for years a hostage in Rome, had had a
claim to the throne; and he now escaped from Rome and
fled to Syria. Lysias meanwhile defeated Philip; but the
moment Demetrius landed in Syria the army deserted
Lysias and he and the child-king were delivered to
Demetrius. He beheaded the child and tossed Lysias
into a dungeon to rot. A few days later Alcimus appeared
before him, complaining that he could not govern Israel
because of religious bigots like Hosah and political trai-
tors like Judas. He begged Demetrius to send an officer
to look into affairs in Jerusalem.
And so it was there came to the city one Bacchides, a
cruel and ruthless man. After hearing their complaints
from Reuben, Alcimus and others he resolved by treach-
ery to get his hands on all the leaders of the Pious. This,385
he was told, would be easy because they accepted Alcimus
as the high priest. He announced to the Pious that their
lives would be spared if they came to his side.
When she heard of this Judith rushed to Hosah and
seldom had a sister more bitterly and wildly reproached
a brother. "You blind and pathetic fool! You praying
fanatical simpleton! This man only wants to get hold
of you-all of you! This man only wants to torture you,
pull your joints apart, dig your eyes out, burn you alive!
Because you were born a fool must you die one?"
"Leave me," said Hosah, deeply offended.
"You!-your hands are too pure and holy to grasp a

"Don't talk to me," he said.
"By the Holy One of Israel I could choke you and kick

"He'll castrate you!"
Hosah flashed with anger. "Go away!" he cried.

Philemon's wounds had healed but his right arm was

"You never could," said Judith.
"Hush," said Philemon. "She forgets that we were
once dear friends."
"In Alexandria. We should be there now."
"It doesn't matter. Have the Greeks an Aristotle to-
day, or the Jews a Job?"
"The Jews have a David," said Judith.
Reuben looked at her. "You mean Judas. We'll take
care of him and I advise you two to stay away from
him." A little angry he turned away, saying: "I'll send
food and wine." He hesitated a moment and then came
back and faced Philemon. "I want you to understand this.
You saved my life. Food and wine don't pay for that.
I'll see you safely to any city of your choosing."
99
Judith looked quickly at Philemon. He said: "We'll
stay here."
"You realize-"
"Yes, we do."
"Your answer is final?"
"Final."
“All right, Philemon. Again, then, we say farewell!"
Swiftly Reuben went away and entered the citadel.
Judith said: "Will he send food or assassins?"
"It's hard to tell."
Reuben sent food and wine. Two men came bearing it,
one with two jugs of wine, the other with a huge basket
overflowing with food. Judith took the basket and Phil-
emon the jugs and they went back down the hill.
"Will a spy follow us?"
"I doubt it. He knows that they will get us in time."
In the cave Judith removed the cloth from the basket
and looked on such a feast as she had never looked on
before. The odors came up to her and she closed her
eyes, content to bow over them and breathe. Philemon
searched in the basket and found cups and filled the cups
with wine. Judith took her brimming cup and held it up
against his.
"Let's get drunk," she said, "and maybe they'll kill us
while we sleep."394
"We'll feast. We'll get drunk. But now we must drink
a toast."
"Not to our children for we'll never have any."
"Not to our old age. We'll never have that either."
"To what?"
"To what?"
"Why do we have to ask that?" She smiled and touched
his cup and said: "To reason."
And answering her smile Philemon said: "To justice."
40
A FEW DAYS THEY FEASTED and drank wine and were
happy. They felt strengthened. And when at last Phil-
emon went out to look around him and learn what was
being done he was startled to see a bloody head spiked
above the north gate. A man told him that this was the
head of Nicanor, a Syrian general, who had led a force
against Judas. Close by the ghastly head was a hand,
Nicanor's right hand, with a spike driven through it.
He talked a while with the man and then returned to
Judith.
"Well," he said, "Alcimus is coming with a huge
army."
99
"You mean-?" Philemon did not meet her eyes.
"Philemon, look at me! What is it?"
"A huge army," he said. "A bigger army than any
that has yet come.'
99
"Under Alcimus ?"
"Alcimus and Bacchides."
"The same Bacchides?"
"The same."
"Is it coming down the coast?"
"God, no. It's coming straight over the hills."
"Well!" she said, and gave a short bitter laugh.395
Philemon shrugged. "We were happy for a little while.
Now-"
"Now-?"
"This looks like the end."
"Where is Judas?"
"Out there somewhere. This time they'll get him.
This time they'll never leave till he's dead."
"But there's Jonathan and John and Simon."
"Yes. Then they'll be dead."
"Does Judas have a big army?"
"No. A lot of his men have deserted."
"Deserted! You mean-? You don't mean my people
are deserting him!"
"Yes. The man I talked to was a deserter."
Judith came up to him and put hands on his shoulders
and looked at his eyes. "I know what you mean. You
mean our cause is hopeless. Don't you?"
"Do you feel any hope?"
"Darling, be honest with me. Do you think we should
go to Alexandria ?"
"No."
"Please don't lie."
"O God, how can I know what we should do? I told
you it would be this way-armies-armies-armies until
not a man is left alive!"
"I'll do what you think best. If you want to go to Alex-
andria, Reuben said he would see that we got there."
"Do you mean we should run away? Should we be
among the deserters ?"
"I guess not. How large an army does Judas have ?"
"I don't know. The man said half his army deserted
before the last battle."
"But he won."
"Yes, but many more have left him since then. This
man was one of them."
"Where are all these men going?"
"I don't know. Hiding, I guess, or leaving the coun-
try."
Quietly Judith said: "What should we do?"396
"We'll just have to think about it."
"All my people are dead. I have only you. Where you
go I will go."
"Why do we talk about it?" he asked angrily. "We
know what we'll do. We'll find Judas. . . .Why talk about
it?"
"Darling, don't be angry.
"I'm not angry. But we never had a chance. Never."
"Well, if we're going to find Judas let's find him.”
They left the city and went north, with their bedrolls
across Philemon's back, their daggers in their girdles.
They met men who were fleeing south. Philemon stop-
ped one of them and said:
"Where are you going?"
"Anywhere. A vast host is coming."
"So now you're deserting. Where's Judas?"
"Back there. Most of his men have left him."
"Why? Why are they leaving him?"
The man spread his palms and shrugged. "Shall a few
of us fight multitudes ?"
"We have."
"But not such a host as this. Even Judas is frightened.
He has sent to Rome for help."
"To Rome?" Philemon turned to Judith. "Did you
hear? To Rome!"
"Rome won't help," the man said. "Even if Rome
should it will take months. The enemy is almost here."
"You'd better come along with us."
"No!"
"Coward!" Judith cried.
The man sneered at her. "Were you in the last battle?
Did you see your friends piled up four deep all around
you ?"
The next day they met Judas and the remnant of his
army coming south. The ragged, starved and dispirited
men marching with him said that Judas was going to de-
fend the holy city. He did not think it wise to stay up
north, where he could easily be surrounded, but would go397
back up to the high hills against the city and make his
stand there.
"How many of the enemy are coming?"
"Too many to count. They're like the sands of the
sea."
Judas and his host encamped at a site known as Elasa,
not far from the city. There he waited, overlooking the
lower country before him; and presently out of the north
came the thousands of infantry and cavalry under Alci-
mus and Bacchides. They were flung over such an ex-
panse of earth that their numbers seemed much greater
than they were; but even so, Judas' remnant of an army
was outnumbered twenty to one. And it was a well-fed,
well-paid and well-equipped army that came out of the
north. It had been promised the plunder of the holy
city. It was eager to fight.
When this huge army appeared Judas had with him
between two and three thousand men. During the next
night a thousand of his men deserted. All night they
slipped out of the camp and fled into the south. When
Philemon and Judith arose at daylight and looked around
them they were appalled; and after Philemon had gone
through the camp he returned and said to her:
"It looks as though half of them have gone.
"The miserable cowards."
99
Not half had gone yet. But during this day another
hundred crawled away and vanished; and during this
night, while the enemy was drawing up in battle array, a
few more hundred fled. This was a night in which nobody
slept. Judas, gaunt, haggard, and almost out of his mind
with grief, anger and despair, went among his men, beg-
ging them to stay and fight. He appealed in the name of
everything dear to them-their holy land, their holy city,
their sacred books, their God. All night he and his
brothers exhorted and threatened, or prostrated them-
selves in prayer. All night. . . .
And Philemon and Judith, too heartsick to speak, sat
side by side on their bedding and waited. With the com-
ing of daylight there would be battle.398
Just before dawn Judas stood heartbroken among his
men and looked around him. How many had remained
with him? he asked. How many? A thousand? No, some-
one said, not a thousand. Almost a thousand, someone
said, who had been trying to count them.
"Fifty times that many oppose us!"
"We'll win!" someone said.
"If Israel's God is with us we'll win!"
Philemon and Judith were listening. For hours they
had not looked at one another, for in both was the
belief that this time they would die. Then, glancing at
Judith, Philemon saw that her face was wet with tears.
He clasped one of her hands and felt her response. She
moved over, and her body trembled against him. He was
trembling also, as any brave man will who is young, yet
stands face to face with death. He looked up at the dim
sky and round him at the earth and said: "How beauti-
ful everything is!" He clutched a handful of earth and
smelled it and rubbed it against his cheek; and then Ju-
dith took a handful of earth and pressed her face against
it.
"Darling," he said, "see. The sun will soon come up.'
"The last sun for us.'
99
"The last sun for us."
99
Judas persuaded his three brothers to leave the camp,
saying that he himself would die, but that they must live
to carry on the fight until Israel was free. "We'll destroy
so many of them," he said, "that they won't be able to
strike again until Rome sends help. That will give you
time. . . ." Just before dawn he summoned all his faithful
to prayer. They all knelt with their foreheads to the
earth, and Philemon, clasping Judith's hand, whispered:
"I never knew that the earth could smell so sweet!"
When the prayers were finished Judas summoned the
captains of fifties and told them to doubly arm their men,
for most of those who had fled had left their weapons
behind. The moment they could see to move, said Judas,
they would strike at the center of the line and try to roll
the flanks back. The captains of fifties gathered their399
men in groups. The captain of the fifty which included
Philemon and Judith stood in his group and said: "If we
can penetrate the center we're to push through and
swing, half to the right and half to the left. If their
flanks give, then is when we must kill them in great
numbers." His voice was quiet. Philemon thought: Here
is a very brave man.
To Judith a few moments later he said: "We must
stay close together."
"Side by side."
"If one of us falls, the other is to stand over him till
he falls."
"Together."
99
He looked up at the sky: "It's daylight."
Judith faced him and clasped his arms. "Hold me a
few moments.'
He draw her to him and kissed her and was holding
her when Judas came by. He was trying in this last mo-
ment to cheer his men. He was saying that any one of
them was equal to a hundred of the enemy. His voice
was that of a man who would never know, not even in
death, that he had been defeated.
Then he saw Philemon and Judith and for a moment
he laid a hand on Philemon's back and looked at the scar
on his cheek. He glanced at Judith. "Fight," he said, “as
you fought at Emmaus. And may God bless you and
keep you."
Judas went away, and Philemon said, looking at the
east: "In a moment now."
Judith squeezed him and drew his head down and
kissed his mouth, his eyes, and his wounded cheek. Then
she stepped back from him and moved a finger along
the ten-inch blade of her dagger. Besides his dagger Phil-
emon had a short sword. Judith had a stout cudgel with
which to parry and strike.
Judas and his pitiful army were facing the enemy. Now
and then a man turned to look at his comrade but most
of them merely grasped their weapons and stared straight
ahead, waiting for the trumpet. The light had been grow-400
ing. It was gray and wan and in it faces looked tired and
ill. Philemon turned once again and met Judith's eyes and
smiled and with a smile she answered him.
"Soon now," he said.
Then there was a sudden and loud blast from a horn
and Judas and his remnant rushed forward in one of the
most gallant and suicidal charges in history. Philemon's
right hand was clasping Judith's left; and as they ran he
was thinking: In intense excitement there is no pain in
death! He was thinking, We must be cool and cunning,
and kill as many as we can! Then a whole world of
thoughts and memories engulfed him, and he was lost
to life and himself, a man running toward death, side by
side with the woman he loved. He knew vaguely that
some of the Jews were singing and for a moment he was
warmed by the thought that he would die in the company
of such brave men. . . .Then he knew that the man on
his left had fallen when a hurled javelin flashed for a
moment and went through him. They were still running.
Once it occurred to Philemon that he should look up at
the sky again, once again at the beauty of the world; but
he had no time, for they had engaged the foe. . . .“Judith,
stay close to me!". . . .This did not seem like a battle:
everything was numbed and dulled and obscured. The
cries should have been shrill in his ears but they came
from far away and from another world. Like a light in
his consciousness was Judith: "Stay close to me!" And
when, dimly, he saw her strike a man down and leap over
him, he thought vaguely, without emotion, that she was
a greater warrior than most men. No longer did he real-
ize that he was shouting. “Judith, are you all right? Stay
close to me!". . . He thought she kept answering him,
"I'm all right. . . .I'm all right...." He had killed a man,
using his sword. He had laid the man's skull open like a
melon. Then he ran a man through and staggered back,
trying to wrench the sword out, and calling: “Judith,
stay close to me!" He heard her call-or did he? Perhaps
he was no longer crying her name. He did not know: in
the awful heat of battle a man could not know: he could401
not know that Judas and his force had rent the Syrian
center apart and plunged through it. He could not know
that the two flanks had closed behind and encircled them.
For they were fighting, as Philemon fought, with muted
fury, with insane unconquerable fury, with dust in their
nostrils, with blood over them, with screams in their
ears like the sound of far-away bells. A man came after
a while to fight mechanically, without purpose or thought,
yet with infinite cunning. ."Judith!" A man hardly
knew what he was doing as he parried and dodged and
plunged, as he drove his sword in and withdrew it, or as
he used it like a club to split skulls; but he did keep be-
fore him the one holiness: "Judith, are you all right?" He
was not sure that he heard her answer and with an effort
he pulled himself together, panting and bloody, and look-
ed round him for sign of her. "Judith!" And then he saw
her. She was fifteen or twenty feet on the right of him and
in that moment an avalanche of men was bearing down
upon her; and he saw the flash of her dagger; and then
she was gone. He plunged toward her, mowing to right
and left with his sword as he went, and he stood over her
but men were all around him; and there was only another
moment to feel the plunge of the weapons as they came.
in him, only an instant to know shock and death. . . .

AMIEL HAD FLED to that cave beyond Modein where he
had spent so many happy months and there he had been
living, his presence known only to a faithful friend who
slipped in once in a while to bring water and food. The
friend came one day to tell him that Judas and his gallant
force had been wiped out but Amiel was not interested;
he was contemplating a mightier one than Judas. With
the divine hand guiding him he was writing:402
Arise, O God, plead your own cause!
Remember how the foolish man reproaches
you daily!
Forget not the voice of your enemies :
the tumult of those who rise against you
increases....
Because of the wickedness of his people the Holy One
had withdrawn to the upper regions; but he sent mes-
sengers down to make his will known to Amiel. Cause the
son of David to sprout and let his horn be exalted! The
son of David would come soon, with omens and portents
announcing his coming. Swords would flash in the heav-
ens, the sun and moon would turn black, great numbers
of horses would race across the cloud-hills, and every-
thing on earth would be confused and appalled. Blood
would run from wood, stones would cry out with anguish-
ed voices, barns would be empty and sowed fields barren,
and the springs and the fountains would dry up. . . .
...
There would be a forerunner, Elijah or another, to
prepare the way. Jesus ben Sirach had sensed that. Amiel
knew it in all his bones. This one would proclaim the
anointed, the root of David, the prophet like Moses-
and yet, Amiel was not sure just what the deliverer would
be like. Would he come on clouds of glory, or be a lowly
one, riding an ass? Would he be another David, smiting
hip and thigh, or a symbol of Israel the suffering servant?
Amiel looked into his depths and perceived that the king
would be clothed with divine powers and endowed with
special gifts of prophecy and wonder-making. Never for a
moment did he see him as a pre-existent christ-god: this
idea in the Oriental cults he was familiar with but had
never dwelt on, for it was alien to Judaism. As a matter
of fact, what Amiel foresaw, above all else, was a gold-
en age, a time of blessedness, the kingdom of heaven,
when the Holy One would rule and all men would ac-
knowledge him, and none would ask, "Do you know the
Torah?" for none would be ignorant. But he also believ-
ed-and most of his people would believe before another403
century had passed-that a glorious king would come,
commissioned by God to conquer the nations and elevate
Israel to a position of supreme eminence. For was it
not written in the holy books that nations that know you
not shall run unto you because of the Lord your God,
the Holy One of Israel? All nations would come to the
Torah and Jerusalem would be the capital city of the
world....
During the era of the kingdom of heaven the whole
earth would be blessed with amazing fruitfulness and none
would hunger or thirst; there would be peace and right-
eousness, and joy everlasting. The people who had walk-
ed in darkness would stand in the great light. The Father
of the world, the great Goodness, would come down from
his remote regions, to which wickedness had driven him,
and live with his people in the intimacy of old. But even
more than all that, more than the righteous kingdom and
the supremacy of Israel-more than all that, deeper and
more holy than all that, more precious than rubies and
ointment, was the certainty that Amiel and all the right-
eous would rise from the dust and live again. The warmth
of that belief enveloped him like a cloak, touched his
gentle face with the sun's glory, shone like heaven in his
eyes and vibrated like heavenly music in his bones-and
what wonders still undivined awaited Israel? Eternal
life, eternal peace and righteousness-and what more?
And what more? What greater marvels, unsuspected,
but certain and imminent, would descend from on high,
when the son of man, the horn of David, came to his
people?
In the dim light he peered at the holy words:
And all the trees of the field shall know that
I the Lord
have brought down the high tree, have exalted
the low tree
have dried up the green tree, and have made
the dry tree
to flourish: I the Lord have spoken and
have done it!404
NOTES AND COMMENTARY
NO EXPERIENCE has been more rewarding for me than the
reading of a great many books on the subjects of ancient Judaism
and Christianity by both Jewish and non-Jewish scholars. For
one thing, it has been amazing to observe how widely scholars
can vary in their conclusions who examine the same materials.
For another thing, it has been a lesson in tolerance. Though it
is true, as Prof. Lake has said, that it is the "general tendency
of ecclesiastical scholarship to consult the records of the past
only to find the reflections of its own features"; though it is true,
as Cumont has said, that "preconceived notions are always the
most serious obstacles to an exact knowledge of the past"; it is
also true, as Prof. Rylaarsdam reminds us, that the "co-operation
of Jewish and Christian scholars is one of the happy phenomena
of our time." That is a great deal to have won.
In an effort to see, not a reflection of my features but the truth,
I have read all the ablest Jewish scholars available to me, in-
cluding Abrahams, Baron, Bentwich, Dembitz, Ginsberg, Graetz,
Greenstone, Finkelstein, Kohler, Lauterback, Loewe, Marcus,
Margolis, Montefiore, Schechter, Waxman, Zeitlin, and others.
Of course I do not quote in the following Notes 'scholars', either
Jew or gentile, who are merely special pleaders. They make such
preposterous statements as this by Felix A. Levy, that Israel "was
free of all those mythological features that characterized other
folk religions and from the beginning had a pure monotheism.”
Even Prof. Orlinsky, an able scholar, can say that what "Mar-
golis has done for Joshua needs to be done also for the other books
of the Old Testament. Yet it is very doubtful that there will
arise in the future other scholars of the quality of Margolis to per-
form the task." Christian scholars have been just as fatuous.
Lietzmann, for example, successor to the great Harnack, says
that after the final destruction of the Temple, "Judaism began
gradually to disappear from history."405
Readers of this novel who would understand its implications
may wish to dwell on Frazer's statement that the "publication of
the Deuteronomic Code in written form (3rd cen. B.C.) marked
an era in the history not only of the Jewish people but of human-
ity. It was the first step toward the canonization of Scripture
and thereby to the substitution of the written for the spoken
word as the supreme and infallible rule of conduct. The accom-
plishment of the process by the completion of the Canon in
the succeeding centuries laid thought under shackles from which
in the Western world it has never since wholly succeeded in
emancipating itself.”
As for the Maccabean period, Prof. A. Smith in his great
work Jerusalem reminds us that of "all the movements of history,
none are more fitted to attract our curiosity than those by which
Jew and Greek first came into contact: when the minds were
confronted and the spritual heritages began to be exchanged,
whose concurrence and interaction were destined to exercise so
enormous an influence upon civilization." The period was, Prof.
Riggs says, “in reality the period of the intense struggle for su-
premacy of two spiritual forces-Hellenism and Judaism." Prof.
Moulton thinks that our "whole modern civilization and culture
rests upon the coalescence, in the old Roman world, of Greek
and Hebrew thought." Bentwich holds the opinion that "had
the Jews met Hellenism on its own soil and in its prime, a true
harmony between the two might possibly have resulted and pro-
duced a civilization more splendid and more complete than the
world has yet known." Lightley and others have observed that "it
seems not at all improbable that the 'peaceful penetration' of the
new culture would have exterminated Judaism altogether." Prof.
Guignebert pursues the same thought when he says that if An-
tiochus "had not shown such ill-timed haste and had not tried
to force the Hellenizing movement among his Jewish subjects, Pa-
lestine would have followed the same course as Egypt and Syria."
It seems never to have occurred to anyone to ask what the
Western world would be like today if the Maccabeans had lost.
There would then have been no more Jews-Klausner admits that
they would all have been "swallowed up"; and there would have406
been no Christianity, or in any case the moral reformation under
that name would have been very different from what it was.
It takes a singularly obstinate prepossession in favor of what
came to be over what would have been, granted other circum-
stances, to suppose that the former, because of blind fate or
divine intercession, is invariably better. We must admire Bent-
wich's generous admission, though on its own soil was in the na-
ture of things impossible, and in its prime begs the question.
The heart of the matter, it seems to me, is the great truth
which Moulton missed and Inge grasped. There was no coales-
cence in the Roman world of Greek and Jewish thought. Early
Christianity almost destroyed itself trying to reconcile the irrec-
oncilable elements in Hellenism and Judaism. There has been
no coalescence since. This fact, so obvious to contemplation, is
surely second to none if we would understand the forces which
have shaped Western civilization. The most crucial of all struggles,
ancient or modern, has been that between Judaism and Hellenism.
Neither won, and the struggle continues unabated; for today,
as Inge says, "these two streams, the Semitic and the European,
the Jewish and the Greek, still mingle their waters in the turbid
flood which constitutes the institutional religion of civilized human-
ity; but to this day the waters flow side by side in the same
bed, perfectly recognizable-so alien are the two types to each
other."
It is with great reluctance that I append notes at such length
as to make them appear to be grotesquely out of proportion to
the novel itself; but this subject is so sensitive and controversial,
and its facts are so contrary to opinions widely held, that I have
felt it necessary, even at the risk of wearying the reader, to give
on disputed matters a sufficient number of authorities to estab-
lish the facts beyond cavil. I have thought it best to place the
materials in alphabetical order, so that the reader who may not
be interested in all the items can readily turn to those which do
interest him. To save space, titles of publications, etc., are omitted.
All authorities are among the ablest in these fields, save a very407
few, chiefly Jews, whose remarks are specially pertinent where
they speak.

ANTIOCHUS Typical of some Jewish opinion is Glazer: "this
monster, who had a heart of stone..." Of Chris-
tian, Milman: "Antiochus united the quick and versatile charac-
ter of a Greek, with the splendid voluptuousness of an Asiatic.”
Box: "that ardent apostle of Hellenic culture." Mahaffy: "with
many good qualities...a violent Hellenist." Case: “Brave, gen-
erous...at the same time eccentric, passionate, and possessed of
immeasurable self-conceit." Enslin: "by no means the weak dema-
gogue that has often been pictured." Kittel says he saw in Judaism
"nothing but the offspring of haughtiness and obstinate conceit,"
and Legge that "only an empire united and homogeneous in faith
and culture could resist" Rome; Oesterley and Robinson that his
effort "was probably due as much to the encouragement from
Hellenistic Jews as to a personal desire to champion Hellenism....
Jews, in defense of their Law, had as their primary antagonists
men of their own race." After Simon won, heathen, says Case,
"were exterminated with a ruthlessness worthy of Antiochus."
ANGELS Jastrow: "Demonology remains an exhibit of the mind's
diabolical power to create a devastating fiction and
live in and by it." Oesterley: "Our word 'demon' meant originally
an entirely good spirit, or soul. . ." In this time, says Box, it was
Satan's function "to watch over human affairs and beings with
the object of searching out men's sins and accusing them in the
celestial court." The conception of Satan in Psalms, Job, etc.,
says Carter, was due to foreign influence; and Finkelstein says
Satan in Zechariah "clearly replaced for him the Zoroastrian
Ahriman." As for angels, Hatch: "A transcendent God was in him-
self incommunicable: the more the conception of his transcendence
was developed, the stronger was the necessity for conceiving of
the existence of intermediate links." Because it was a sacrilege,
says Abelson, to cherish belief in God's close contact, Jews were
"forced to fall back upon a substitute. This substitute was the409
angel." Finkelstein: "Just as the medieval Christians transformed
local gods into saints and evil spirits, so the ancient Israelites...
saved their beloved ancestral myths by attributing them to
'angels'." G. F. Moore: "The angels . . . are spirits, not immaterial
but of an ethereal, fiery substance, blazing light. Or those which
are employed on God's errands are winds; while those which
form the heavenly choir are fire....They do not eat and drink."
In pre-Exile literature the Hebrews had no Satan or Devil.
APOCALYPSE Typical apocalypses are Daniel, Revelation.
ture
Enslin: "This curious and bizarre type of litera-
purports to be written by great worthies of the past-
Ezrah, Moses, Abraham, Noah, even Adam...authors are always
convinced that the things which they are announcing 'must short-
ly come to pass'." A. H. Forster: "may be defined as that litera-
ture which deals with matters which by their nature must be
revealed." Harnack: "Apocalyptic was keener upon the downfall
of the heathen than upon their conversion." E. F. Scott thinks
its origin in "the last resort" Babylonian; many scholars trace
it to Persia.
ATONEMENT Barton and others think it a "development from
the primitive Semitic custom of wailing for
Tammuz,” and Hooke points out its "closest resemblance to the
common Babylonian puhu, or substitution ritual." It was the
most important of all the holy days, says Friedlander; and
Schauss, "the holiest day of the year." For Jews, says Cornill,
every misfortune "is punishment for sin," and atonement, says
M. Joseph, is the purification of desire. Idelsohn: "a scapegoat
(Azazel) was sent into the desert, to carry away the sins of the
people (Lev. xvi, 22)." Montefiore: "By the letter of the law
it was seemingly implied that the guilt of all sins, of what kind
so ever, be they ritual or moral, voluntary or involuntary, would
be wiped out and atoned for by the ceremony." A. R. S. Ken-
nedy: "These sins were conceived as not only symbolically but
actually transferred to the head of the goat." The goat had
become the surrogate, says Frazer, of the more ancient custom
of slaying the man-god to take away sins.410
BAPTISM seems not to have been sacramental for the Jews.
Brandt: "While he stands in the water, the rabbis,
who stand by as witnesses, declare to him once more some of
the greater and lesser commandments. Then the candidate im-
merses himself completely in the water and rises out of it a com-
plete Israelite."

CHASIDIM meant the Pious; some scholars use the word Puri-
tans. Ginsberg: "Pharisaism at its very appear-
ance in history, about 170 b.c.e., represented two distinct cur-
rents, the conservative and the progressive." Chief aim, says
Kohler, was "attainment of the Holy Spirit thru fasting and prayer
severe in dealing with woman in her menstrous state" and
would not let her adorn herself "lest her attractiveness induce
her husband to have intercourse with her. They refrained
from "harmless pleasures," says Glazer, "and denounced others
for enjoying them. There were some over-pious Judeans who con-
demned all medical skill and aid; they insisted all maladies were
sent from God, he alone could cure them." True successors of
Nehemiah and Ezrah, they regarded all contact with other
peoples, says Peritz, “as contaminating." Oesterley and Box: "They
did consider themselves as 'separated' from the bulk of the people
by their superior holiness regarded with contempt the mere
common herd." Only those, says Pfleiderer, who “shared the pious
attitude belonged to God's congregation." Case: "The fanatical
Chasidim...

CHILD G. Stanley Hall: "Nowhere was the passion for
EXPOSURE children, which Ploss has shown to be so strong
and universal among lower races, more intense
than among the ancient Hebrews." But the Greeks, says Sumner,
"regarded infanticide as the necessary and simply proper way
to deal with a problem which could not be avoided.” Infanticide,
says Lippert, was a "widespread form of primitive social fore-
sight."
CHOSEN Deuteronomy: "Thy God hath chosen thee to be a
PEOPLE special people unto himself, above all people that411
are upon the face of the earth." Fifth Jewish Sybil: "The
godlike heavenly race of the blessed Jews, who dwell round-
about the city of God at the navel of the world...shall endure
forever and be exalted by Providence." Wisdom, xix, 22: "For
in all things, O Lord, thou didst magnify thy people and glorify
them." Philo: "The most God-beloved of all nations, and one
which appears to me to have received the offices of priesthood
and prophecy on behalf of the whole human race." J. ben Levi:
"Even as the world cannot exist without winds, so mankind can-
not exist without Israel. . . . Even thus did the Holy One say:
'Of all the nations that I have created it is Israel that I love best"."
Jewish Prayer Book: "Blessed art thou, O Lord our God, who
hast chosen us from among all nations." Jewish aloofness impelled
Celsus to write: "If the Jews were content to observe their
laws, nobody would reproach them; but since they pride them-
selves upon perfect knowledge and refuse to mix with other
men, they are in the wrong. For their religious ideas are not
peculiar to them, nor is there any indication that they enjoy the
esteem and love of God to a higher degree than others." Gude-
mann says: "All history teaches that every people regards itself
as the chosen, and exhibits the higher self-consciousness if it
does not put forward its defects as merits."
Because the heart of Judaism is the doctrine of the chosen
people, a generous expression of opinions will be given. A typical
fallacy is that in Alex Comfort's novel On This Side Nothing:
"Jews are not born, but made." If made, their prophets made
them, not gentiles. The modern orthodox Jewish position is David
Ben-Gurion's (Time, Aug. 16, 1948): "There have been only
two great peoples: the Greeks and the Jews. Perhaps the Greeks
were even greater than the Jews. . . . We had a message to give
the world, but we were overwhelmed, and the message was cut
off in the middle. In time there will be millions of us-becoming
stronger and stronger-and we will complete the message.”
Jewish Statements. Baron: "The Jewish religion without the
'chosen people' is unthinkable." Loewe: "The foundation of Ju-
daism rests on two principles: the unity of God and the choice
of Israel." Radin: "The people of the Lord." Montefiore: "It was
a comfort to insist that, in spite of all Israel's woes, God loved412
Israel with a peculiar and passionate love, and that he hated
the 'nations'." M. Joseph: "Israel has been chosen by God to
be a consecrated race....Israel's distinction is that he possesses

be the chosen people. . . . It is not clear that the Jew must re-
main distinct if he is to continue efficiently to serve the cause
of humanity?" Kohler: "They believed that in them all the na-
tions of the earth should be blessed." Dubnow: "The Israelitish
people as a whole, without distinction of rank and regardless
of the social prominence of individuals, has been called to guide
the other nations...” Friedlander: "The conviction that the seed
of Abraham have the distinction and the mission to become a
source of a blessing to all mankind was transmitted from genera-
tion to generation." Greeenstone: "He will be a light to all na-
tions..." Edersheim says the ancient rabbis taught: "The very
air of Israel makes one wise. God had created the world on ac-
count of Israel, and for their merit....Israel had been in God's
thoughts before anything had actually been created. . . . The ab-
horrence, not unmingled with contempt, of all Gentile ways..."
M. Lazarus: "A kingdom of priests!...Israel has to segregate it-
self." Bentwich: "In the first of the blessings which Balaam pro-
nounces upon the children of Israel, he exclaims: 'Lo, it is a
people that shall dwell alone, and shall not be reckoned among
the nations.' Philo, expounding the passage, adds: ‘Israel shall
be apart not so much by reason of the separation of their homes
or the cutting-off of their land, but by reason of the peculiarity
of their customs; for they shall not mix with other peoples'....
Jews were allotted a special quarter in the Hellenistic cities, not
by way of restriction, but as a privilege." Glazer: "Ezra and the
reigning senate in Jerusalem insisted sternly upon the exclusion
from the community of all people who could not claim Judaic
descent." "It was this aloofness," Waxman points out, "which
irritated the other nations, the fact that the Jews denied the right
to other religions to exist, that they considered themselves a chosen
people all these taken together made them an object of hatred
and even of contempt." "The Covenant made the Jew self-con-
fident and arrogant," says Abrahams, "but these very faults were
needed to save him. It was his only defense against the world's413
scorn." There was no scorn before the faults developed. The Jew
went so far, says Sekles, as to feel a “sublime, noble pride in
being singled out to suffer"-see Martyrdom.

Non-Jewish Statements. It was, says Hunter, the centuries just
after the Exile (6th cen. b.c.e.) that "fixed the attitude of the
Jews toward the gentiles." Peet: “A sense of nationality to which
there is perhaps no parallel in history." Sumner: emphasized
everything which could differentiate them from heathen." Toy:
"Yahweh, as national God, is always ready to favor his people
if he alone be recognized and obeyed.” Kittel: “. . . pride that
Israel possessed in the Law a more perfect system of duties and
regulations than any other nation. Breasted: "Their exclu-
siveness brought them disfavor and trouble." Gibbon: ... flatter-
ed by the opinion that they alone were the heirs of the covenant;
and they were apprehensive of diminishing the value of their in-
heritance by sharing it too easily with the strangers of the earth.”
Oesterley and Box: ". . . a fanatical and ever-increasing devotion
to the Torah, and long before the advent of Christ it had become
crystallized into a settled national trait, which, later on, the an-
tagonism between the Christian and the Jewish church only served
to emphasize." Olmstead: "No loyal Jew could doubt that his God
was all powerful . . . if he wished he could destroy the gentiles
from off the earth." McGiffert: ". . . recipients of his choicest
blessings." Norton: ". . . the object of his greatest solicitude and
favor." H. T. Fowler: ". . . his exclusive people, who are to be
guarded from all foreign contamination." J. Weiss: ". . . imposes
on them special duties, and gives them a peculiar mission." Loehr:

to be found in every gifted people on earth, can assume a grievous

acle race." Wernle: ". . . shall be liberated and exalted to lord-

the Jews, a small branch of the whole human family, were in a more
special sense than other members of that family the objects of the

they raised him to the position of sole creator of heaven and earth,

they could not but regard the dominion of other gods on the

sufferings of the Jews, and especially of the elite among them,
were so great toward the close of the Persian empire that, by a
pathetic fallacy, it seemed as if all mankind except the Jews were
morally worthless." H. P. Smith: "The exclusiveness of the Hebrew
faith was to the Greek mind of the day incomprehensible."
Schultz: "Almost all the nations that ever came into historical
contact with Israel are at some time or other [represented as
enemies]." Mahaffy: "... the often barefaced attempts to magnify
the Jewish race at the expense of truth and soberness." Whittaker:
“... an intense bigotry hitherto unknown, and unparalleled except
among their successors." Stade: "According to Ezekiel's idea of
god there must be the most rigorous separation of Israel from
the whole world." Renan said (and Bentwich, a Jew, approves):
"The Jews had a patriotism aimed not at the formation of great
compact states, but of little autonomous communities in the bosom
of other states." Enslin: "This feeling of separateness increased
until he believed that it had always been in force...gentiles in
every age have not unnaturally looked askance at this quintes-
sence of clannishness." Goodenough: "The Jews became a race
with a single and exclusive cult." Halliday: "There was no bar
to racial intermixture like the color prejudice...in the Roman
empire the process was one of denationalization,” and all races
but Jews tended toward absorption. Carter: "The horror of being
absorbed... led them to become exclusive. When they thought
of their neighbors it was to pray for their destruction." H. P.
Smith: "To the stricter Jews the gentiles had become objects of
hatred only." G. A. Smith: "From the isolated position of Judea415
""
sprang the defects of her virtues-her selfishness, provincialism,
and bigotry." Box: "All this contributed to make them unpopu-

race so unpopular, their intolerance, their denial of the gods...
Derwacter: "The Jews were intolerant in an age when men gen-
erally were widely tolerant. . . . They claimed . . . the future of
the world in their hands." Lofthouse: "Dislike and condemnation
of the gentiles went together-an attitude that was quickly recip-
rocated; and the vicious circle was drawn more and more firm-
ly, until loyalty to Yahweh was felt to involve hatred of all his
creatures who did not share descent from Abraham." Ehlhorn:
"This widespread hatred of the Jews...was doubtless to some
extent a consequence of their hatred of the brethren. They were
treated with contempt because they themselves despised the un-
clean gentiles." No Christian scholar, except Herford, has been a
more vigorous champion of Judaism than G. F. Moore. He says:
"The conviction that Judaism as the one true religion was destined
to become the universal religion was a singularity of the Jews.
No other religion in their world and time made any such pre-
tensions or cherished any such aspirations. It was an exclusive-
ness the rest of mankind did not understand and therefore doubly
resented. And it must be admitted that the manner in which
the Jews asserted their claim and descanted on the sin and folly
of polytheism and idolatry and the vices of heathen society was
not adapted to make them liked in an age that knew nothing
of jealous gods, and when all manner of national and personal
religions, native and foreign, lived amicably and respectfully side
by side. If the Jews alone were excepted from this universal
toleration, as Philo complains, it was chiefly because they alone
were intolerant." Carus: "The Jew alone resists absorption.” Still,
as T. H. Robinson says, "There can be little doubt that many
races contributed to the blood of the later Israel." As for Jewish
exclusiveness, Ihring reminds us that "the goal of history is
not particularism and division, but communion and unity, and
the individuality of men and nations is not destroyed but rather
intensified and ennobled by the element of universality."
CIRCUMCISION Moore, "All the more bitter was the feeling416

CONDITIONS, May says the "first nine chapters of Proverbs
MORAL
is a fair representation of conditions in the
third and second centuries B.C. The temple was
filled with riot and revelings by the heathen, who dallied with
harlots, and had to do with women within the sacred precincts."
Blunt: "The Book of Proverbs and Ecclesiasticus give an un-
pleasant impression of the moral condition of Jewish society.
Licentiousness, conjugal infidelity, open robbery, and the chican-
eries of commercial cunning seem to be common."
DANIEL, Since Anthony Collins proved the Maccabean origin
BOOK OF of Daniel, no reputable scholar has questioned it,
though many still do who pretend to scholarship.
Cheyne: "Few will deny that the Book of Daniel is the work of
the early Maccabean period." Schurer: “. . . the direct product of
the Maccabean struggles, in the very heart of which it came into
existence." Although, says Reinach, the 'prophecies' in Daniel
"have been accepted by the whole body of Christian theology,
they can no longer deceive any thinking person." Driver dates it
167-8; Barton, 168-5; Burkitt "soon after 168"; Taylor, "about
166"; Whittaker about 164; Cornill, in the first month of 164.
Its author is unknown. Some authorities think he used one or
more earlier writings; Torrey and others put these in the middle
of the third cen.; Baumartner, in the Persian period; Welch argues
for a Babylonian origin. Montgomery suggests this type of story
was derived from Egyptian models. Jack points out that there
was a primordial hero with the name Dn-el, i.e., Daniel. All
the reputable scholars think the book was written to encourage
the Jews in their struggle under the Maccabees. Peters says the
book is "a history of the inner life and thought of the Chasidim."
Margolis and Marx say: "The visionary who wrote the book...
reasoned that the present was the final onslaught on God's people
and the Holy City." The author pretended the book was written
long before his time, says Bewer, because "he would never have417
gained a hearing...if he had appeared in person before the
people or had sent out his book under his own name." With few
exceptions, says Encyc. Biblica, people used materials that were
"not in any proper sense historical at all...our distinctions be-
tween authentic history, legendary history, pure legend, and myth,
he made as little as he recognized our distinction of natural and
supernatural. It was all history to him." The apocalyptics, says
Sachs, seized upon everything concerning the "hallowed past."
Barton: "All apocalypses are attributed to persons who did not
write them." Lietzmann: "Daniel is the first to have compre-
hended all earthly history as a great unity which moves towards
a final goal according to a divine plan, and his understanding
determined the thought and action of mankind for two millenia
[italics supplied]."
DEATH AND Trachtenberg: "Death itself is the work of spirit
MOURNING forces." Dembitz: "It is the desire of every
good Israelite to die with the words declaring
God's unity upon his lips."
ECCLESIASTES Use of this is anachronistic if Cheyne is right
in dating it in the time of Herod, or Pfeiffer,
150; Hitzig dates it 204; Kuenen, end of third cen.; Rowley,
third cen.; Bewer, 200; Sarton, 250-168; Barton, about 200;
M'Neile, "the outpouring of the mind of a rich Jew, at the be-
ginning of the second century." If it is as old as Solomon, says
Delitzsch, "there can be no history of the Hebrew language.” The
meaning of the title is obscure. Heine called it the Song of Songs
of Skepticism; Delitzsch, the Song of Songs of Religion. Klausner
thinks it shows the influence of the "thought and culture and
language of the Greeks,” but Renan, overriding Graetz and others,
could find "not a Greek word nor any well-marked Hellenism”
in it. Some have thought the author a disciple of Heraclitus; some,
of Epicurus; some, of the Stoa. Sellin finds in it a mixture of
Greek philosophies; Langdon sees a Babylonian, Galling an Egyp-
tian influence. Cheyne rejects Greek influence, but Mahaffy says:
"Hellenistic thinking has been a cause of the curious contrast
which the work shows to other Hebrew theology." Many Jews418
opposed the admission of this and the Song of Songs into the
Canon: Hillel accepted, Shammai rejected; the controversy raged
100 years.

EPICURUS Few great men have been so traduced. The typical
Christian attitude is that of the Catholic historian
Duchesne. But Dill: "...a profoundly religious mind, to whom per-
sonal immortality is not a necessity of his religion." Thilly: "...not
a doctrine of sensuality." W. Walker: "in his own life he was
an ascetic." James: "The dictum 'eat, drink and be merry, for
tomorrow we die' is a travesty of the Epicurean philosophy."
W. M. Macdonald: “...must be acquitted of the charge of sensu-
ality." Case: a brilliant exception to the general run of men
in that he ventured to withstand religion to her face. Other people
were terrified by the story of the gods, by thunderbolts, and
the roar of the electric storm. But these things only gave him
fresh courage, filling him with a desire to burst the fast bars of
nature's portals and bring to men a knowledge of the perfectly
natural character of all physical phenomena." C. H. Moore: "The
noble resignation, the high moral and humane zeal, which charac-
terized the Epicurean school at its best..." Hunkin: “The main
purpose of Epicurus was to deliver men from fear, especially
fear of the gods and of life after death." Oates: "sought...to rid
men from certain besetting fears...' Norton: "aimed to free
men from superstitious fear.” Bevan: "We have never been thor-
oughly frightened; the ancient world was frightened. . . . There
is no point upon which the ancient followers of Epicurus pour
out their gratitude to their Master more ecstatically than that he
had delivered them from religion, from all the terrors of the
Beyond." Enslin: "to every Jew an abomination"--but not to
Hellenic Jews. Bentwich: "The hostility of Judaism toward...
Epicurus"-a hostility which Christianity inherited.

EZEKIEL "Ezekiel did more," says Loisy, "than any other writ-
er to prevent Judaism from understanding its own
history"-and its own religion, adds Guignebert. H. P. Smith says
he was a man "nervously abnormal"; Bowman, that he was "the
storm center of criticism in the period between the wars"; and419
Prussner that fresh examination of his book has produced the
"troublesome result that our earlier faith in its essential unity and
trustworthiness as a record of Ezekiel's life and thought is no
longer shared by all."
FEAST OF Plutarch compared it to the "holy rites of Bac-
TABERNACLES chus." Its origin and meaning, says Benzinger,
are uncertain. It is generally agreed that sexual excesses were com-
mon; Adam Clarke, "there is no room for doubt that these Suc-
coth were tabernacles wherein young women exposed themselves
Kalisch: "presenting . . . the virginity of maidens as a
first fruit offering." The command to drink was taken, says G. F.
Moore, "with somewhat exaggerated literalness." Schauss, a very
conservative Jew, admits that it sometimes "went beyond limits"
and "often became a tumultuous wild bacchanalia."
