GREEK Legge: "Greek art and learning which still form our
best and proudest intellectual possession." Pointing out
that Hellenic thought was influenced by India, Persia, Babylon,
Egypt, N. Schmidt says: "The Greeks had the happy faculty of
putting the impress of their genius so thoroughly upon anything
they touched that even what they borrowed has all the appear-
ance of being their peculiar property." Box: "took an intellectual
view of the world." Bevan: "essential character of Hellenism
was that it broke free from the old rule of stereotyped custom in
thought and practice, that it brought into operation . . . reason
applied to the real facts of the world." Hatch: "The power of
generalizing and of forming abstract ideas . . ." Wenley: "The
problem of Greek civilization was the problem of man's freedom."
Dill: "Greek philosophy was the great solvent of faith.” Cumont:
"resolutely rationalistic: her greatest originality lies here." Bous-
set: "In the whole scope of genuine Greek philosophy the idea
is unheard of that the best and highest as the purpose of all human
living is not innate in the human soul." The Greek passage from
the supernatural to the natural is, says C. Cohen, "the most im-
portant step ever made in the history of mankind; it marked the
emergence of a new kind of thinking, and the opening of a conflict
that is still in being [italics supplied]." As Pfleiderer puts it,420
against many gods "they set up one god the governing rea-
son."

GREEK Wenley: "an imaginative rather than an ethical
RELIGION faith." Lawson: "religion and morality were to the
Greek mind divorced, or rather had never been wedded.” Brif-
fault: "Greek gods remained to the last amoral; they never be-
came differentiated into 'good' and 'evil." Tucker: "Greek gods
are simply glorified humanity." Foucart: "They were better men
than their gods: it was not the gods who improved them, but
they who improved and elevated their gods [hasn't it always
been so among all people?].” Jane Harrison points out that for
the later Greeks the gods were little more than art objects.
GOD Though Prof. Moore emphatically insists that scholars err
who think God for the Jews of this time was “abstract or
transcendent" Loewe, one of the ablest of Jewish scholars, says:
"The cardinal point in the proper appreciation of the Divine
Being must be an adequate understanding of His Immanence and
Transcendence" and that the Pharisees were "concerned lest
the doctrine of the divine Immanence should conflict with that of
Transcendence." Schmidt says before contact with the Greeks
Judaism "rigidly adhered to this doctrine of the divine transcend-
ence" but M. Joseph says, “A remote God is no God." On the
nature of deity as conceived by Jews of this time the works of
Schechter are excellent. On the concept of deity itself G. P. Mur-
dock says: "we have an evolutionary sequence of soul-ghost-
spirit-god, each of which shades gradually and imperceptibly into
the next." See Sumner and Keller, Science of Society, II, 931.
Though Jehovah is an improper name for the Hebrew deity many
gentile scholars cling to it, Cheyne finding it "more euphonious” and
Case going so far as to translate "Mi kamakha baalim Yahweh" as
“Who is like thee among the gods, Jehovah.” (!) As Trattner says:
“The name Jehovah rests on an erroneous use of the Hebrew
vowels. As a matter of scholarly accuracy the name Jehovah should
be avoided and Yahweh used in its place.” As a matter of court-
esy to Jews also, because Yahweh is their deity.421
IMAGES So fanatical were Jews in resistance to images that
after the destruction of Jerusalem they refused even
to look at coins with images on them. Rome issued a coin for
Jews with the horn of plenty on it. Josephus records that when
Tiberias put images in Jerusalem Jews prostrated themselves
"and continued immovable in that posture for five days and as
many nights." When Pilate commanded Jews to admit Caesar's
image, Josephus says that they "fell down in vast numbers," saying
they'd rather be slain than have their laws transgressed.
IMMORTALITY Rohde: "It is beyond calculation what power
has been wielded since their first appearance
by the Platonic Dialogues in the confirmation, dissemination, and
precise definition of the belief in immortality-a power that with
all its alteration in the passage of the centuries has maintained
itself unbroken into our times." Sayce: "The Mosaic law main-
tained a resolute silence on the doctrine of a future life. Of the
doctrine of a resurrection there is not a whisper." C. H. Moore:
"it is a striking fact that the Jews were later than most of the
peoples about them in conceiving of individual immortality.”
Carter: "When the Jews came into contact with the Persians hold-
ing with fervor the hope of immortality, they could not but ask
themselves whether that hope was to be discovered in their own
religion." Jewish belief in immortality developed, says Blunt, with
the Messianic hope. Kohler: "We may state with certainty that
only when the Jews came in touch with Persia [was] the belief
in a hereafter and in the resurrection-alike unknown to Ecclesi-
astes and Ben Sira-adopted by them." Desperation as a nation,
says Pfleiderer, made them more zealous in the belief, and their
contact with the Greeks, says Bentwich, nourished it. Finkelstein
plausibly argues that among the Jews the belief arose with the
poor: "those at the bottom of the social ladder have to find their
escape in dreams and visions." The belief must have developed
rapidly during Maccabean period: Jesus ben Sirach wrote about
200 that the expectation of man was worms; his grandson 50
years later translated him to mean resurrection of the body.
INTERMARRIAGE Carus: "In pre-Exilic times, no objection422
was ever raised to intermarriage with foreigners. Moses mar-
ried first the daughter of a Kenite and then even an Ethiopian
. Solomon was the son of a Hittite woman.

xvii, 42) as red-haired and of fair complexion." But after the

classing heathen women with slaves." Axenfeld: "the constant
strain between the demand that the heathen should be included
and the dread which this excited." May: “All sorts of disorders
were said to be born of the marriages contracted with foreigners."
Friedlander: "the issue of such alliances must be treated as il-
legitimate. Those who love their religion . . . will do their utmost
to prevent the increase of mixed marriages." Feldman: "Just
as oil does not mix with other liquids, so does Israel not mingle
with other nations.

JERUSALEM We know little about the city at this time. Al-

JESUS BEN Ecclesiasticus is title given his work by early
SIRACH Christian Church; scholars date it around 200 or
a little later. Reinach thinks he had some knowledge of Greek
philosophy; Toy, that he imitated Proverbs; and Finkelstein that
he wrote it to "win over some of his assimilationist comrades”
and had to "exaggerate the virtues of his people and the vices
of its rivals." Radin: "a solemn and somewhat opinionated old
man who, as he himself tells us, would neither laugh nor play
with his children for fear of imperilling his much prized dignity."428
But Schechter: "there is always with Ben Sira the question
whether he really meant what he said."
JEW What was the Jew? First are opinions by Jews, followed
by opinions by gentiles. Akiba said: "Laughter and levity
accustom a man to immortality." Baron: "The Jewish mind, fol-
lowing the logic of its extraordinary destinies, appears as an unin-
terrupted chain of antinomies and paradoxes. The greater the dis-
crepancy between theory and reality, the more tragic became the
inner conflicts in the Jewish personality." Abrahams: "The Jew is
really a very sentimental being. He loves symbols. A good deal of
his fondness for ritual is due to this fact. The outward marks of an
inner state have always appealed to him." Bentwich: "The Jewish
mind was essentially imaginative and intuitive and averse to exact
metaphysics or close reasoning." Schechter: "Life with the Jew
meant religion.” Enelow quotes an ancient rabbi: “when they
ascend they go up to the stars, and when they descend they go
down to the dust." Jeremiah compared Jews to two baskets of
figs, the good being very good, the bad very bad. Edersheim: “Ex-
citable, impulsive, quick, sharp-witted, imaginative; fond of pa-
rable, pithy sayings, acute distinctions, or pungent wit . . . zeal-
ous, with intensely warm Eastern natures, ready to have each pre-
judice aroused, hasty and violent in passion, but quickly assuag-
ed "Robertson Smith: "deficient in plastic power and the
faculty of sustained and orderly effort." Ewald: "suddenness of
emotion and act, intensity and variety of simple and irrepressible
feelings, the highest tension and rapid collapse of imagination."
Inge: "the Jewish mind and character in spite of its deeply re-
ligious bent was alien to mysticism." Wenley: "Neither distinct-
ively artistic, nor intellectual, nor civic, he was preeminently re-
ligious." S. A. Hook: "a simplicity and narrowness of thought, in-
tensity, fanaticism, and even ferocity." H. P. Smith: "The Semitic
mind has little use for merely speculative thinking." Box: “defective
on the analytical side where the Greek is so strong. The Hebrew
genius does not find itself at home in discussion, examination, com-
parison, criticism." Oesterley and Robinson: "feels very strongly,
but is not given to abstract speculation. . . . he does not reason
consecutively or follow a complicated train of thought." G. F.
•424
Moore: "Dogmatic atheism and theoretical skepticism are the out-
come of philosophical thinking, to which the Jews had no inclina-
tion." Cumont: "The dangerous idea that knowledge is the reward
of piety..." Commenting on Loeb's statement that Psalms rep-
resent abnormal states of mind, Cheyne: "there is certainly a
sweetness in their sufferings which an outsider cannot understand;
for, the more miserable they are, the more they realize that one
stronger than the strong cares for them.... the pious were to some
extent the authors of their own misery. They had no political in-
sight, and were incapable of practical compromise." For other
people, says Derwacter, "their religious attitude was summed up in
atheism, their social attitude in misanthropy, and their political
attitude in disloyalty." They became, says Joseph Lewis, “Children
of Darkness for the love of their God."

JEW of the Greeks." The orthodox Jewish position is Zeit-

Streeter: "In all ages there has been a wide gulf between ortho-

JEW vs. Bentwich thinks the struggle between Judaism and Hel-
GREEK lenism “one of the fundamental struggles in the march
of civilization”—if it was not the fundamental struggle, what was?
But it was not, as he says, essentially a conflict between mono-
theism and paganism. Matthew Arnold: “the uppermost idea with
Hellenism is to see things as they really are, the uppermost idea
with Hebraism is conduct and obedience"-based of course on
'revelation'. Bentwich adds: "Greeks were filled with the idea of
intellectual superiority, the Jews with the conviction of a moral and
religious mission." Sachar: "The two points of view could not
very well be reconciled in an individual [italics supplied]. One
could not accept a revealed law as ultimate, and yet honestly
question the very foundations of life." The clash between the two,
says Baron, "could be long postponed, but not avoided." Greeks,
says Finkelstein, were asking, What is the real? Jews, What is the
right? Dubnow: "It was a clash between two theories of life. It
cannot be denied that in matters of the intellect, especially in the
field of philosophy and science, not to mention art, it might have
been greatly to the advantage of the Jews to have become disciples
of the Greeks." Farrer: "the debt of the Hellenizing Jews to the
Greeks is beyond all comparison in excess of any we know of as
due from the Greek race to the Hebrew." Butcher: "Both
peoples felt themselves a peculiar people . . ." Wenley: "The
Greek developed his genius in joy, the Jew in misfortune." B. P.
Church: "No greater contrast could have existed than between the
optimistic and joyful thought of Greece and the gloomy expression
of later Judaism. The results of the combination are with us still
[italics supplied]." Pfleiderer says the "combination" of the two
prepared the way for Christianity but let us observe again that
they were never harmoniously combined. As Sorley says, the ef-
fort to combine them "well nigh rent the infant church in twain.”
(My 9th volume will deal with this.)426

JUDAISM Heine called it not a religion but a misfortune; what-
ever it is, scholars find it difficult to write objectively
on this subject. At one extreme is Finkelstein (usually so sound):
"in the preservation and advancement of learning, it has become
the cornerstone of modern civilization"; on the other, Glover: "The
Pharisee was essentially an actor-playing to himself the most
contemptible little comedies of holiness." Enelow: "Religion be-
longs to the Jewish substance; all the rest is accident.” M. Lazarus:
"Every house a temple, every heart an altar, every human being
a priest. Radin: "No people of antiquity.
ever worked
their religious customs and convictions into their daily lives so
thoroughly as did the Jews." Schechter: “a great Infinite, composed
of as many endless Units, the Jews." Loewe: "The sight even of a
misshapen creature evokes the blessings of thanks to God for vary-
ing the forms of His handiwork." Abrahams calls it the religion of
reason, but in all my reading nobody else had called it that.
Sack: "the purely religious cult." Breasted: "In the ancient East
monotheism was but imperialism in religion.” Vignoli: “from the
primitive ideas of mythology to the conception of the absolute
and infinite Being"--but other peoples preceded them in this con-
cept. Wernle: "the conception of religion as a legal, a national
system." Carus: "a unique phenomenon." Streeter: "a religion with
a single dogma-that God is One." But Judaism admitted, says
Lake, "the widest possible range of thought" because it “rested not
on uniformity of thought, but on obedience to the Law." Dujar-
din: "an intolerant national religion." Budde: "a religion of
choice and not of nature." Oesterley and Box: "It may be doubted
if any religious system has ever been so thoroughly and consist-
ently applied and carried out." H. W. Robinson: "Some would add
a further criticism . . . disregard of artistic beauty . . . lack of in-
terest in speculative truth." Sorley: “unspeculative, exegetical, and
founded on a worship of the letter [see Word]." Robertson: "the
bare theory of a Single God is not more but less ethically ele-
vated than the theory of Dualism, which is an effort to find an
...427
•
• • •
ethical solution where the former does not even face the problem."
G. F. Moore: "The prejudice of many writers on Judaism against
the very idea of good works is a Protestant inheritance
from Luther's controversy with Catholic doctrine, and further
back from Paul's contention that there is no salvation in Judaism.”
Grant: “Judaism has made charity and alms-giving a sine qua
non of the pious life.” As to its origin, Goldziher: “Genesis a
mere literary creation." Driver: "their beliefs about the origin and
early history of the world, their social usages, their code of civil
and criminal law, their religious institutions, can no longer be
viewed as differing in kind from those of other nations . . . all, as
is now known, have substantial analogies among other peoples."
McCown: "Their laws, including some of their most highly ethical
elements, were to be found, even with improvements, in the Code
of Hammurabi. Their ideals of the universal justice of God, of his
disregard, even hatred, of wealth and power, and of his care for
the poor, the orphan, the widow had already been proclaimed in
Egypt or Babylonia or both, long before the birth of Abraham."

JUDITH This character is not based on the Book of Judith,
which, says Mahaffy, "jumbles names and events in a
ludicrous manner." Waxman: "full of errors." Oesterley: "the book
is not history." On Judith's precociousness, Finkelstein: “the
twelve-year-old girl of the East was far more sophisticated and
understanding than the child of a modern occidental metropolis."

LOVE, Opinions vary widely on the subject of love (man-
ROMANTIC woman) in the ancient world. G. Stanley Hall:
"Romantic love in any modern sense had little literary devel-
opment among the ancient Hebrews save so far as in their minds
it was always religious." That among some peoples there were
deep attachments we know; that there were any Hollywood ver-
sions of love is very improbable.
MACCABEES
Bevan thinks the word a shortened form of
Makabiahu, “meaning the name of the Lord";
Curtiss, that it meant the extinguisher; Edersheim, that it was
from Maqqabha, "a hammer, like Charles Martel"; and some have428
thought it referred to a physical defect in Judas. Baron: "the
Maccabean upheaval, in many senses the first war of religion in
history." Case: "for the first time . . . a war of extermination of
a religion." As for the sources of it, Mahaffy says, "that excellent
historical document the first Book of Maccabees"; but Case: "It
is noticeable . . . that the writer of I Maccabees glosses over
the defeat of his heroes so thoroughly as to make a circumstantial
account almost impossible.” This novel rests on the account put to-
gether by many scholars. It closes in the year which marked,
says Wellhausen, “the proper end of the religious war of the
Jews. Thereafter the occasion of the conflict was not religion but
government."
MARRIAGE G. F. Moore: “Marriage was regarded not only
as the normal state, but as a divine ordinance. It
was a generally accepted opinion that the world was created for
the multiplication of the species.” An unmarried man diminished
the likeness of himself to God, even though the Jews had no di-
vine Mother! The married woman was "consecrated to her hus-
band, set apart for him exclusively." The death penalty for
adultery had not yet been relaxed-and was not until the Christian
era.
MARTYRDOM Martyr in Greek meant witness and was the
name, says Reinach, “given to those who pro-
claimed their faith in the face of suffering." G. Stanley Hall:
"The ancient Jews had pitied themselves uniquely and cumu-
latively . . .” Baron: "this was the period of increased exaltation
of martyrdom."
MATTATHIAS Mathews says he was of priestly descent, and
Kuenen, a priest; but that he was is doubted,
says H. P. Smith, "by some."

nothing dishonorable in having commerce with a
prostitute." Briffault: "The relations of hetairai to their lovers
could be as sincere and detached from mercenary considerations429
as sexual relations can be . ." Blumner: "Their fame spread
... and their rooms were crowded by men of the first rank."
MENELAUS Some have thought he was a priest; I have fol-
lowed Finkelstein. Mathews: "With Greek influ-
ences thus ubiquitous and persistent, it is not strange that men
like Menelaus should have been eager to lead Judea out from
its isolation into the circle of a more brilliant civilization."
MESSIAH W. D. Wallis developed the thesis that in times of
hardship all ancient peoples turned to notions of a
·
ap-
deliverer [don't they still?]. Dickey: "Jewish ‘utopianism'
pealed especially to the ignorant and exploited masses.” Israel's
most striking feature, says Peters, was the messianic hope, which
became, says H. W. Robinson, "both her glory and her ruin.”
The belief became strong that "God would intervene in favor of
his chosen people, and would overthrow the enemies of Israel.”
The hope now began, says H. P. Smith, "to take the fantastic
shape of later apocalyptic visions" as there was revived, says
Hooke, "the old conception of the divine king as the center of
the hopes of the community." McGiffert: "Jews. . . to be raised to
a position of supremacy over all the earth." Though for the Jews,
says Dodd, the term had "no clear and constant significance," he
was to be, says Schurer, "a human king and ruler, but as one
endowed by God with special gifts and powers." Some like Gress-
mann have argued that the Jews took the idea from other people,
and Wernle says it was revived "from a bygone age.” The earli-
est Jewish expression of it Cheyne thinks is in Ezekiel, xvii, 22-24,
though he "virtually abandoned it." Volz thought the idea ap-
peared first in Jeremiah; Torrey, the Second Isaiah; but Schmidt:
"the expectation seems to have appeared for the first time soon
after the conquest of Palestine by Pompey in 63 B. C." In any
case, says Loehr, the "classical example in the OT is the Book
of Daniel." Greenstone thinks the idea as old as Moses, and Baron
goes back for it to Genesis, xl,10 ff.
MODEIN Glazier puts it only 3 m. north of Jerusalem; but430
G. A. Smith: "on the edge of Ajalon." As with so many biblical
places we don't know where it was.
MONTHS Jews took their months from the Babylonians: Nisan
(Nisannu); Iyar (Airu); Sivan (Sivannu); Tammuz
(Duzo); Ab (Abu); Elul (Ululu); Tishri (Tashritu); Marheshvan
(Arah-semna); Kislev (Kislivu); Tebeth (Tebitu); Shebat (Sha-
batu); Adar (Addaru). Adar Sheni was their extra month. Nisan
was roughly our March 15-April 15.

MOTHER, Hannay: "the Hebrews, who were brought under
DIVINE a most severe almost malevolent masculine cult
which despised women . . . so strong was the Hebrew prejudice
against women in religion that they never evolved any word for
goddess the Queen of Heaven reigned supreme until the
Hebrews dethroned her and made her the author of Evil. a
companionless god is a lifeless conception." Briffault: "it was no
easy task for the Yahweh reformers to press on the people the
claims of Yahweh to their fidelity in his solitary male form."
Meyer points out that at Elephantine Jews associated two female
deities with their worship of Yahweh. That a people without a
divine mother have had so incalculable an influence on the West-
ern world remains one of the riddles of history.

DRAMA mysteries "consisting chiefly of dramatic presenta-

NAME Encyc. Biblica "The name of a person or thing was for
often al-
most an equivalent for the thing itself. This is especially true of
the names of God." Rome had a secret name, so well preserved,
says Reinach, that "we do not know it to this day.” Lenormant:
"Everyone knows to what a pitch the belief in the all-powerful
and hidden name of God has grown amongst the talmudical and
cabalistic Jews .. "Enslin: "blasphemy was not committed by
uttering the sacred name or even by using it as an oath. It con-
sisted in cursing God by the name of God-God damn God'
-though of course God was not the name. Jews in particular,
says Guignebert, attached to the names of men and things "a
peculiar value, at once metaphysical, mystical and magical.” Cu-
mont: "he who could pronounce the exact name of an individual
or of a divinity was obeyed as a master by his slave." Moore: "uni-
versal belief. . . that the names of gods in incantations and ad-
jurations put the power of these gods at the command of the ma-
gician. The Jews similarly believed that all manner of miracles
could be wrought by one who knew the secret of 'the Name'." It
was out of this fear that the third commandment developed. Toy
says the name was "omitted cause reverentiae by late scribes."
Common substitutes were Holy One, Lord of the Worlds, King of
Kings, Name, Place, All Present, our Father who is in Heaven,
Heaven. Cheyne: 'heaven' or 'the name' or 'the Holy One' would
generally meet every need." The Word as surrogate for God has
descended, says Olmstead, "from the most ancient Shumerian
Inim Enlila, the divine command of the first Lord of the Gods,
Enlil of Nippur." An ancient Jewish writing says: "One may not
say that the invocation of God's Name obliges him to do the will of
the invoker, that God himself is coerced by the recital of his
Name; but the Name itself is invested with the power to fulfill the
desire of the man who utters it." It was through the name, Rashi
(11th cen.) tells us, that the universe was created. It was deemed
impious, says Dembitz, even to think of the sound of the Name.
Kohler: "It is always the invocation of the sacred Name that
makes the prayer, the blessing or curse effective.” The magic in
words, says Trachtenberg, "elevated the process of naming a child
into one of major importance." The Jewish Ency. says an amulet
<<432
with the Name on it had the power to frighten robbers, calm the
sea, cure disease, or if thrown on a man to kill him. Rabbis
Hanina and Oshaya were believed to have created a calf by ut-
tering the name. Some scholars argue that Jove-Jupiter-Jehovah-
Jahweh are the same word, since the interchanging of letters was
a common magical art.
NAMES, See Trachtenberg. J. Taylor: "Names which have a
OF JEWS religious import are more characteristic of the Semitic
races... especially true of the Israelites.” In many instances their
personal names incorporated a part of their name for deity.
NEW YEAR Schurer thinks the beginning of the year with
Tishri more ancient than with Nisan; others do not
agree. Wellhausen: "The ecclesiastical festival of new year in the
priestly code is autumnal . . . the civil new year has been separ-
ated from the ecclesiastical and been transferred to spring." Of
course the Christian new year has no logic in it.
PARENTS, Simeon ben Yohai: "Great is the honoring of father
HONORING and mother, for God makes more of it than hon-
oring himself." Moore: "Among all the commandments the
'weightiest of the weighty' is filial piety.”
PERSIAN De la Saussaye: "No people ever came nearer to
INFLUENCE monotheism than the Persians." Toy: “a remark-
able and noble attempt . . ." J. Murphy: "Zoroaster, the Aryan
apostle of an early monotheism . . .” Judaism was “inevitably modi-
fied" by the dualism of Persia, the idealism of Plato, the asceti-
cism of Pythagoras. "The Persian eschatology as a whole was
taken over by Jewish thought." Cheyne: "It will hardly be denied
that Jewish religion owes a debt of gratitude to Babylon and Persia
the Jewish belief in immortality comes from Zoroastrianism."
Mills: "The entire mass of the Medo-Persian Mazda-worship is
brought into close association with Judaism in an unparalleled
manner in the familiar passages which meet us in Chronicles,
Ezra, Nehemiah, the later Isaiah, Daniel, etc." Goldziher: "no
early influence of Mosaic doctrines on the theories of the Zend
...433
books is even conceivable.” McCown says Jews "probably borrowed
their conception of the resurrection, the coming reign of God,
and other features of their eschatology from the Persians." Bel-
lange: "Judaism essaying in the Achaenemid epoch to speak of a
law, a prophet, an Exodus, and one only God, in the very coun-
tries in which Mazdeism developed, must have found in Mazdeism
a powerful helper." Cumont: "It is generally admitted that Judaism
took the notion of an adversary of God (Satan) from the Maz-
deans." Under the name of Zoroaster in the Alexandrian library
were two million lines. As for the similarities, Carter: "Each was
proclaimed by a prophet. Each worshiped one God. Each believed
in an evil power. Each forbade images . . . each was intolerant
toward other systems. Each developed priestly cults, and em-
phasized ceremonial cleanness. Each had something like a syna-
gogue worship." There is hardly any doubt, says Baron, that Ju-
daism "received strong stimuli" from Parsiism. Millman: "Satan, an-
gels, immortality, resurrection-all Persian and Zoroastrian doctrines
imbibed by the Jews." Cardinal Newman: "the doctrine of the
Logos is Platonic; that of the Incarnation Indian; that of a divine
kingdom Judaic; that of angels and demons (and a Mediator)
Persian; . . connection of sin with the body is Gnostic; the idea of
a new birth Chinese and Eleusinian; of sacramental virtue Pytha-
gorean; of Trinity common to East and West... Sepp: "No nation
has been so clever as the Hebrews in appropriating to themselves
the property of others. ." Ginsberg: "the Jew always had a
genius for assimilating foreign matter by impressing upon it his
own individuality.” In short, it is generally agreed that contact with
the Persians literally transformed Judaism. Of course most of the
OT was written after that contact.
•
"
PRAYER The oldest known Jewish book of prayers is the 'Deser
Rab Amram' of the 9th cen., c. e.; the earliest extant
collection is the 'Benedictions' of Natronai in the same century.
Prayer, says Maspero, "was a formula of which the terms had an
imperative value, and the exact enunciation of which obliged the
god to concede what was asked of him." Berachoth 15b: “The value
of the words uttered with the lips is determined by the devotion
of the heart." Codes: "One should say at least 100 benedictions434
daily." But as Abrahams says, much Jewish prayer is not petition
but praise. Their habit of swaying, Oesterley and Box think, is
"a faint remnant of the religious dance," though Dembitz thinks
it derives from “the nervous temperament of the Jew, who likes to
speak with his whole body." In prayer the Jews "turn toward the
earthly abode of God. Abroad, they turn their faces toward the
land of Israel; in the land, toward Jerusalem, in Jerusalem, toward
the temple; in the temple they direct their thoughts to the holy of
holies." Compston says tephillah, their word for prayer, is “pos-
sibly derived from the root meaning 'to cut'. If so, this might hark
back to days when devotees lacerated their flesh in worship (cf. I
K. 18-28)."
PRIESTS Josephus: "Different races have their claims to nobil-
ity on various grounds; with us a connection with the
priesthood is the hallmark of an illustrious line." The priestly law,
says Benzinger, "is the keystone of the whole system. . ." Fink-
elstein: "the priesthood was generally as unlettered as the rest of
the nobility. In the last Temple days when learning had achieved
some standing there were High Priests who could not read the
Bible. To make a sign instead of writing one's name . . . became a
symbol of nobility." The high priesthood, says Snaith, became
"the aim of worldly and unscrupulous men." Schechter: "Heaven
knows that we have very few prejudices in favor of ecclesiastics or
priests of any description or age. Some high priests were very ob-
noxious indeed." Edersheim condemns the laws for their "pettiness
and narrowness" and Montefiore says their "drawback or misfor-
tune" was the equal "accentuation of the ceremonial and the
moral." Barton: "These traditions descended to such details that
they define the kind of knots that a woman could tie and untie in
making her toilet." See the Codes.
PROSELYTES Th. Reinach: "We cannot account for the enor-
mous growth of the Jewish population in Egypt,
Cyprus, and Cyrene without assuming a large admixture of gen-
tile proselytes." Cheyne: "Whatever pride a foreigner had taken in
his birth or riches or wisdom had to be laid aside when he became
a proselyte.” Moore: "the hereditary privilegia of born Jews were435
not conceded to . . . proselytes." Schurer: "the proselyte cannot be
on the same level with the Jew before God." Harnack: "That a
man had to become a Jew in order to become a monotheist was
utterly absurd: it degraded the creator . . . to a national god."
PSALMS Barton: "No one now thinks of attributing the Psalms
in the form in which we have them to David." Cheyne:
"I hold the post-exilic date of every part of the Psalms to have been
abundantly proved." They are, says Cornill, a product of the per-
iod of the second temple. But some scholars believe some of the
Psalms to have been pre-exilic.
PUNISHMENT Finkelstein: "It did not occur to them that
man himself was the author of his thoughts and
inclinations. . . . Never having conceived of intention as separate
from act, they did not reckon with it." He reminds us that Xerxes
smote the Hellespont for sinking his ships. R. Nehemiah: "Pre-
cious are sufferings, for as sacrifices atone so do sufferings atone."
RESURRECTION See Persian influence. Leuba: "It would be
difficult to evaluate the harm done to hu-
manity in the past by the conviction that the real destination
of man is the world to come." The doctrine of resurrection, says
Gilbert, first appeared in Jewish writings in Daniel—that is, in
the Maccabean period. The idea came, scholars agree, from the
Persians. Cultured people, both Jew and gentile, were horrified by
this new belief in the resurrection of the body. The Sadducees
contemptuously rejected it.
RITUAL We do not know what liturgical materials were used
at this time. Those used in this novel are based on
conjectures by Dalman, Abrahams, Hirsch, Zunz, Elbogen, Dem-
bitz, Kohler and others.
RUTH Encyc. Biblica: "all competent critics of the OT agree
that the story of Ruth has been invented." It is nothing
but fiction, says Pfeiffer.436
SABBATH Schechter: "Israel fell in love with the Sabbath, and
in the hyperbolic language of the Agadah the Sab-
bath is personified as the 'Bride of Israel', whilst others call it
'Queen Sabbath' . . ." It is generally thought its origin is Baby-
lonian. For the innumerable Sabbath restrictions see the Codes
(G. Friedlander, Laws and Customs of Israel).

SABBATH H. T. Fowler: "There were whole companies of
SLAUGHTER pious Jews who would rather die than resist

themselves to be slaughtered by their enemies rather than use
arms... Finkelstein: "More than once Jerusalem was taken
without resistance because its soldiers refused to fight on the Sab-
bath day. . . . The 'Book of Jubilees' gives 15 violations punish-
able by death, and the Mishnah classifies the prohibited acts
under 39 heads." The Sabbath slaughter in this story is thought
to be authentic.

LUSTING
SCRIBE AND Abot, i. 5: "One who prolongs conversation with
a woman does himself harm.” Akiba: "Sport and
frivolity familiarize a man with lewdness." The
Patriarch Judah said: "Heedfulness leads to cleanness; cleanness
to purity; purity to holiness; holiness to humility; humility to the
fear of sin; the fear of sin to saintliness." Moore: "It may be
questioned whether the exponents of Jewish piety, like Christian
saints, did not think more about the snares of women and the
lusts of flesh than was good for them." Cheyne: "Still it was a
Jewish idea that connubial intercourse was inconsistent with
ceremonial purity."
SCRIPTURE, Though Albright says: "Conservative scholars
REVISION OF are, we believe, entirely justified in their vigor-
ous denunciation of all efforts to prove the
existence of fraudulent invention and deliberate forgery in the
Bible," the evidence is overwhelmingly against him. Klausner:
"Jews who, in order to glorify their nation and their religion and
to prove the antiquity of both, found it necessary or proper to
create a whole forged literature, according to which venerable
figures among the Greek poets and authors recognized the im-
portance of Judaism . . ." Mahaffy: "The whole age was now438
full of literary dishonesty." A. H. Forster: "Allegory, forgery,
falsification, plagiarism, were rampant." Bentwich: "Today, when
the distinction between fiction and history has been considerably
developed, literary forgeries may appear not only contemptible
but immoral. That distinction was unrecognized in the Alexandrian
age." When scribes "found a passage in the Bible that in its
literal meaning conveyed anthropomorphic ideas . . . introduced
allegorical interpretations." Rowley: "There was little sense of
literary proprietorship among the Hebrews . . . an author felt him-
self at liberty to use or alter what he found in earlier works."
Jews of this time, says Duff, "had no sense at all of servility
to an unchangeable biblical text"; and Moxom that they "re-
sorted to the most elaborate allegorical interpretations of the
OT, by which they were enabled to blend the doctrines of Plato
and the Stoics with the teachings of Moses." Inasmuch as a
"real conflict between divine commandments was inconceivable,
it was the task of the learned," says Moore, "not only to harmon-
ize them by interpretation but to combine them for practical
observance." Just as the Greeks felt the need to save Homer from
impiety by making him allegorical, so the Jews, says Hatch, “felt
in no less degree" the need to save their sacred books. M. Joseph
says that though Christianity, still accepting the anthropomor-
phisms, "declares that God once walked the earth in the guise
of a man, lived a man's life," the Almighty has never “put on
the garb of flesh. .. 'No man can see me and live'." In "the
most primitive biblical records," Finkelstein points out, “God him-
self is described in bold anthropomorphisms" and the Jewish
rationalization of the matter is stated by Friedlander: "In the
Bible anthropomorphic expressions are employed in order to
illustrate the different acts of Divine Providence . In this time,
"
as Hooke says, there was much editing "to remove what the later
religious point of view felt to be discreditable elements." Of such
countless instances, one given by Margolis is: "Thus where the
sacred writers spoke of cursing God the text was made to say
'bless' for 'curse'. It is a euphemism pure and simple." But the
chief efforts, says Edersheim, centered on the anthropomorphisms.
Whittaker: "... the effort to explain away those anthropomor-
phisms in the Bible which were a scandal to Jewish and Christian439
·
theologians." Sekles: the efforts of the Talmudists to accom-
plish a correct appreciation of the anthropomorphisms," Mai-
monides said in the Middle Ages that "every false conception of
the biblical anthropomorphisms" had been rejected. A story
was told of Ben Garon (1st cen. c. e.) that he sat in his study with
300 jars of oil until he was able to 'harmonize' Ezekiel with the
Pentateuch. (Some Jews had wished to reject Ezekiel from the
Canon.) As late as the Middle Ages Jews, like Christians, were
still forging and inventing. They went so far, says Bentwich,
as to try to attach Aristotle to Judaism, making him a pupil of
Simon the Just or of Gamaliel (both of whom lived centuries
later) or making him a Jew of the tribe of Benjamin who took all
his wisdom from Solomon. On the innumerable changes made in
the OT see Pfeiffer, Introduction, 71-126.
SHEMA meant hear!' says Klausner and was "the most es-
sential portion of a Jewish act of prayer." Oesterley
and Box: “... undoubtedly one of the most ancient features in
Jewish liturgical worship." Dembitz: “it is a leading Jewish
principle that whatever duty can be performed at break of day
should be performed before the morning meal and before any
business or pleasure is entered on; and this principle applies most
strongly to the reading of the Shema 'when thou riseth'. . .'
""
SHOFAR When I published in an article the statement that the
shofar was blown anciently to drive evil spirits away
I received from Jewish officers at a nearby airbase letters accusing
me of malicious ignorance. Trachtenberg: "... the blowing of the
shofar 'drives off evil spirits' and confuses and confounds the
devil." Idelsohn: "In ancient times the blasts of the horn were
believed to have the power of driving away evil spirits."
SIN Jews had no word for repentance: sin was turning away,
repentance back to, God. It was, says Cheyne, ‘a breaking
loose', 'a missing of the right aim'. Uhlhorn: "Strictly speaking
the ancient world knew nothing of sin. It deplored the need, the
misery, the transitoriness of human life; but it had no concep-
tion of the corruption of human nature." Moore: "In no ancient440
...
religion is normal piety so pervaded by the consciousness of sin.
Protestants are so habituated to associate the word sin ex-
clusively with the so-called moral law, and to regard knowledge
and intentions as of the essence of sin, that it requires some effort
to put themselves at the point of view of the OT, consistently main-
tained by Judaism, of which none of these things is true." R.
Smith: "I am not aware that anything quite parallel to the ordi-
nary Hebrew sin-offering occurs among the other Semites; and
indeed no other Semitic religion appears to have developed
the need for priestly intervention between the laity and the most
holy things." The conception of sin as ingrained in man, says
Tennant, “attained to full development in the later OT books."
Lightfoot: "The Oriental mind . . . was prone to regard matter
as the source of evil." With Jews, says Bernard, it was the "pre-
valent feeling that the nation rather than the individual was the
subject of sin." They thought, says Fairweather, that "this world
is essentially bad, and . . . for the solution of all enigmas we
must look to the world to come.” Schultz: "The one essential
point in the whole ceremony of sacrifice is the confession of sin."
Snaith: "The most noteworthy development of the post-exilic per-
iod was the ever-increasing importance attached to expiatory of-
ferings." Abrahams: "Rabbinic Judaism took over from the OT a
belief that disease was a consequence of sin. .” H. W. Robin-
son: "Why do men die? The natural answer of a Hebrew thinker
... is that this suffering necessarily points to sin." All of which
casts little light on why Jews developed the most oppressive sense
of sin known to the ancient world, and taken over by Christianity.
Edward Carpenter put his finger on it: "It was in the very con-
ception of a separate self that 'sin' and disunity first began; and
it was by 'sacrifice' that unity and harmony were restored, ap-
peasement and atonement effected." Being the most separate of
all people, it was inevitable that the Jew should feel himself to
be the most sinful: separateness and sin nourished one another.

SONG OF Friedlander gives the modern orthodox Jewish posi-
SONGS tion: "The faithfulness of the beloved to her lover,
her resistance to all temptation, and the concentration of all her
thoughts on the well-being of her lover, form the theme of the
book." Some Jews, says Jeremias, saw the song as a worldly poem,
some as a sacred book. Toy: "All that can be certainly said of it
is that it is a poem in praise of love." Rowley: "The latest view
. . finds in it a liturgy, but this time a liturgy of the Adonis-
Tammuz cult." Attempts to explain it have led to "the most ab-
surdly fanciful interpretations." For Egyptian parallels to, see

SOUL Reinach: "The idea of soul divorced from matter is a
result of animism." Moore: "soul, concretely imagined as
the vital breath (Gen. 2,7), or as the blood or in the blood (Lev.
17, 14)." Farbridge: "Arabs and Jews also represented the soul
as a bird hovering around the body and screeching." H. W. Rob-
inson: "To the Hebrew, the soul is not an esoteric and mystical
abstraction; it is the breath . . ."
SYNAGOGUE We don't know what synagogue service was like
at this time; materials in this novel are based
on Baron, Finkelstein, Schurer, Singer, Oesterley and Box, Dem-
bitz, Kohler. The materials of Hosah's lesson are based chiefly
on Singer. The word is Greek, meaning assembly but we know
"practically nothing" of its early rise and development. Finkel-
stein thinks it pre-exilic; Abrahams puts it in the Persian period;
Kohler, in the early period of the Exile. Synagogues, says Calder,
"existed in all the principal cities of Asia Minor." Codes: "It is
forbidden to eat, drink, sleep or even to doze in a synagogue. We
should not kiss our little ones therein, because it is not meet to
distract one's attention from the love of God." Derwacter: "I do
not know of any specific passage in ancient Jewish literature in-
viting or welcoming the Gentile to the synagogue service. How-
ever, there can be no doubt that the Gentiles did attend." Farb-442
ridge: “...the perpetual lamp of the synagogue was interpreted
by the rabbis as a symbol of God's presence in Israel.” Rabinowitz
reminds us that the synagogue had a “profound influence” on the
development of early Christianity.
TEFILLIN Apparently, says Kohler, it was under the Persians
that the Pious introduced the wearing of the tefillin,
“which had been used before as amulets." Oesterley and Box: "Of
the origin of the Tefillin there can be little doubt; . . amulets worn
as safeguards against demons. . ." Edersheim: "We have it ex-
pressly stated in an ancient Jewish Targuim that the 'tephillin' pre-
vented all hostile demons from doing injury to an Israelite." They
also, says Oesterley, “attributed a protective power to the Mezuzah,
especially in warding off demons." Lenormant says of talismans,
"First of all there were those which consisted of bands of cloth,
covered with certain written formulas. . ." Though Kohler says
impatiently that tephillin has nothing to do with the word for
prayer, A. R. S. Kennedy (Prof. of Hebrew, Univ. of Edinburgh)
says, "By the Jews then as now the phylacteries were termed
tephillin, the plural of the ordinary word for prayer.”
TORAH To understand Judaism one must understand the Jew's
attitude toward the Torah (the 1st five books of OT),
usually, as M. Joseph says, translated as law. Ginsberg: "Torah
is not law. It is an expression for the aggregate of Jewish teach-
ings." Klausner says it means literally instruction; Pfeiffer, “in-
struction of the priest"; Duff, "It does not mean law, but doc-
trine"; Herford, "Torah never meant law. It means teaching."
But most gentile and some Jewish scholars call it Law. In this
novel the Torah always means the first five books. Enslin: "Fail-
ure to grasp this fundamental thread, namely, that for Judaism
there was not-could not be-such a conception as the modern
'progressive revelation', not only renders the rabbinic teachings
totally unintelligible, but throws much of the OT into hopeless
confusion." That is, those who hold by the Torah reject von Hart-
mann's view: "Certainly the modern man has reached the point
at which he consciously holds that acts which are merely the docile443
execution of an extraneous will can never lay claim to moral value
in the true sense of the word."
Psalm 119: “Oh, how I love Thy Torah!" R. Judah ben Ilai; “God
looked at the Torah when he created the world." R. Eliezer ben
Hyrcanus said without the Torah the universe could not endure.
As Bokser puts it, "it was for the sake of the Torah that the whole
creation came into being." Ben Bag Bag: "Turn it and turn it
over again, for everything is in it, and contemplate it, and wax
gray and old over it, and stir not from it." R. Meir: "It clothes him
in meekness and reverence; it fits him to become just, pious, up-
right, and faithful; it keeps him from sin." R. Joshua ben Levi:
"Every day a Bath-kol goes forth from Sinai, proclaiming these
words, Woe to mankind for contempt of the Torah.” Johanan ben
Zakkai: "If you have learned a great deal of Torah, do not claim
credit for yourself, for that is what you were made for." R.
Benaiah: "The world and all that fills it was created only for the
sake of the Torah." Hillel said what Bag Bag repeated: “Turn it
over and turn it over again, for everything is in it." Philo said
Torah taught men to worship a Being "more primal than the
Monad." A Jewish maxim: "Whatever a clever scholar is des-
tined in the future to reveal in the Torah was already stated to
Moses on Sinai." Talmud: "Nothing exists of which there is not
some indication in the Torah." Asked by his nephew whether hav-
ing learned Torah he might study Greek R. Ismael replied: “And
you shall meditate therein day and night.' If you can find an
hour which is neither day nor night, then you may study Greek."
The foregoing are expressions by ancient rabbis. Rabinowitz:
"To the Jew the Torah was the be-all and end-all of his existence."
Lazarus: "Moral laws are not laws because they are written;
they are written because they are laws." The will of God, says
E. I. J. Rosenthal, was revealed through the Torah. Edersheim:
"Terrible as it may sound, it is certainly the teaching of Rabbinism,
that God occupied so many hours every day in the study of the
Law." Trachtenberg: "one must not lean on it, place anything upon
it, touch it with unclean hands, kiss it immediately after kissing
wife or child, have intercourse in its presence. Finkelstein:
"It covered every aspect of human behavior: religious ceremonial,
·
"444
...
the protection of health, the interpretation of literature, the regu-
lation of the calendar, the relation of Israel to its neighboring
peoples, as well as ethics, manners and beliefs, and civil and
criminal jurisprudence . . . determined such questions as whether
or not one might greet a bereaved person, praise a bride extrava-
gantly... regulated one's diet, one's dressing habits, and one's
relations with one's wife." Abrahams: "Conduct
was regu-
lated in the minutest details." (On the incredible number of the
details see Laws and Customs of Israel.) Moore: "The whole world
is not equal in worth to one word of the Law." Dale: "In God the
Law is alive..." Toy: "an attempt to define all the beliefs and acts
of life..." Gorres: "guards the divine against the arrogance of
probing reason." H. F. Stewart: "it contained all dogma.. [as]
Dr. Claude Montefiore has said, and I imagine all faithful Jews
would subscribe to the saying, 'In orthodox Judaism the Law sup-
plied the place which the Person of Christ occupies in Christian-
ity'." Goodenough says much nonsense has been written about the
burden of the Torah; but Lofthouse: "Six centuries after the ap-
pearance of Deuteronomy, the Jewish doctors had lost themselves
in decisions as to the tithing of mint and cummin, the ridiculous
discussions as to how many ounces a man might carry on the
Sabbath, giving a freer reign to casuistry than it has ever en-
joyed, before or since, even with the Jesuits."

UNCLEANNESS (The Hosah chapters were based entirely on
Jewish sources.) Jevons: "Everything which
comes in contact with a tabooed person or thing becomes itself
as dangerous as the original object, becomes a fresh center of in-
fection..." Glover: "The Jews would not 'eat with blood', lest
the soul of the beast should pass into the man." Weiss: “Inter-
course with heathenism is considered especially polluting..."
Moore: "The clothing, seat, or couch of the common man might
be unclean, and communicate their contagious defilement to any
one who even accidentally or unwittingly touched them, with
secondary consequences that might be most serious.” Blunt: “All
primitive religions display great terror of any functions connected,
however remotely, with the organs of reproduction. Child-
birth.

terribly feared.. Finkelstein:445
"A citizen of Jerusalem who found that he was impure with a
major impurity-which meant, it must be remembered, not only
when he had happened to touch an unclean vessel or attend a
funeral but also when he had been with his wife-was literally
barred from his own home." If when impure he "touched an
earthenware vessel or stove, it became incurably impure. The neigh-
bors with whom he shared the stove would thus not let him ap-
proach it. In fact, he himself would not dare to draw near it. . .'
Codes: "On the following occasions the hands must be washed:
on awakening from sleep in the morning, on coming from the
lavatory or bath, after cutting the nails or hair, after taking off
one's boots, after attending a funeral or going into the house where
the corpse lies, or after touching anything unclean or any part
of the body usually covered. . ." Edersheim: "No pious Jew
would of course have sat down at the table of a Gentile. If
...
a heathen were invited to a Jewish house he might not be left
alone in the room, else every article of food or drink on the table
was henceforth to be regarded as unclean. . . . In truth, the bitter
hatred which the Jews bore to the Gentiles can only be explained
from the estimate entertained of his character. The most vile, and
even unnatural crimes were imputed to them. It was not safe to
leave cattle in their charge, to allow their women to nurse in-
fants, or their physicians to attend the sick, nor to walk in their
company without taking precautions against sudden and unpro-
voked attacks. They should, so far as possible, be altogether avoid-
ed." The Greek, says Schurer, "looked at the Jews, crushed under
the weight of their ancient and primitive laws, and pitied them.”
Montefiore: "equal accentuation of the ceremonial and the moral.
More precisely, the evil lay in that mournful relic of outworn
paganism-the conception of external holiness and pollution, of
clean and unclean." Reinach: "Hebrews abstain from killing or
eating animals such as the pig, whose ancestors had been the
totems of their forefathers. . ." The zeal of the scribes, says Schurer,
"was continually increasing the number of snares, by which an
Israelite... might incur uncleanness. Israelites traveling in
foreign countries had to restrict themselves to vegetable raw
materials. . .”

WISDOM
LITERATURE
Consisted of Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, various non-
canonical books. Their wisdom literature is not,
says Loewe, "the 'know thyself' of Socrates or the 'I searched my-
self' of Heraclitus, but "The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the
Lord'..." Waxman: “Wisdom, or Hokma to the Jews never meant
philosophy in the Greek sense." Goodenough: "Jews had begun to
transform their Jewish doctrine of Wisdom into the mystic doctrine
of Sophia as the Light-Stream"-see his brilliant Mystic Gospel of
Hellenistic Judaism. Oesterley and Robinson: "The earlier Hebrew
Wisdom literature was largely influenced by Egyptian and Baby-
lónian thought."
WOMEN, Lecky: "It is one of the most remarkable and,
POSITION OF to some writers, one of the most perplexing facts
in the moral history of Greece that in the former and ruder period
women had undoubtedly the highest place." Lippert: "Plato had
no choice but to make woman morally lower [than man]. He re-
quired a degeneration of the man in order to form women from the
cowardly among them." Tucker: "It is a great blot on Athenian
civilization that the position of woman had retrograded since the
days of Homer." Warde Fowler: "the Bacchanalian conspiracy of
186 B. C., in which women played a very prominent part, is ex-
plicit, and shows that there was a 'new woman' even then, who
had ceased to be satisfied with the austere life of the family and
with the mental comfort supplied by the old religion, and was
ready to break out into recklessness. . ." Among the Romans her
position was the highest in the ancient world; among the Jews
she occupied a high position as wife and mother but her mind was
held in contempt. Typical of many rabbinic statements is that of
Ben Hyrcanus: "A woman has no learning except about the spindle.
Let the words of the Torah burn up but let them not be trans-
mitted to a woman." Radin: "It would be quite far-fetched to as-
sert that there was anything like sex-equality, or even marital
equality, in ancient Israel. The wife was distinctly the inferior.
Her adultery was punishable with death. Her husband was her
master, baal; she addressed him as 'My Lord'." Finkelstein: "both
sons and daughters remained the property of the father; . . the
daughter, no longer offered for sale as a slave, still carried, as a447
purchasable wife, some of the stigma of her earlier status." Schech-
ter says since the rabbis concluded that “woman had extinguished
‘the light of the world' she ought to atone for it by lighting the
candles for the Sabbath. . . . One privilege was left to women—
that of weeping." Delitzsch: "To be seen with her hair uncovered
was regarded as a disgraceful exposure, and to sing verged on
unchastity." As May says, the rules had nothing to do with pro-
tecting the purity of women but were a "protection only of the
property" as is "made obvious by three passages in the Mosaic
law. First, the rule of Exodus as to seduction stands not among
the laws of personal injury but at the close of a list of cases of
pecuniary compensations for injury to property. Second, the se-
ducer's knowledge of the woman's married status was immaterial
to the crime of adultery. third, where no property-right in
a woman existed which the Hebrew law wished to protect, infringe-
ment upon a woman's chastity was not punishable.” Selden points
out that seduction of a girl past 12 years and 6 months was not a
crime. Lippert: "All punishment of sexual transgressions within
marriage is based originally on the conception of the property right
of the husband." Testament of Twelve Patriarchs: "evil are wom-
guard your senses from every woman." Philo said woman
is a “selfish creature. . . terribly calculated to agitate and overturn
the natural inclinations of a man."

WORD Kipling: "Words are of course the most potent drugs
used by mankind." Jastrow: "Both folk-minded and doc-
trinal mind are impressed with the power of words." Moore: "A
blessing or a curse. is not the expression of a benevolent or
malevolent, but impotent, wish; it is a blessing or a curse. Once
uttered, it is beyond the speaker's power to revoke or reverse it.
•
Similarly the prophetic word is not a mere prediction that
something will come to pass; it brings to pass what it foretells."

fying personality is common among primitive tribes. The Scriptures

It was a common Jewish thought that God created the world with
a combination of letters. Says the Sefer Yezirah: "By thirty-two
wonderful paths the Eternal has created the world in three forms:
number, letter, and speech; ten numbers self-contained and twenty-
two letters (the number in the Jewish alphabet)." Case: "Belief
in the sanctity of the letter was. a Jewish inheritance." Cohen:
"No illusion of primitive mankind is more marked than the belief
that words are things. . . . Messrs. Ogden and Richards, in their
excellent work, The Meaning of Meaning, properly remark that
"The persistence of the primitive linguistic outlook not only
throughout the religious world, but in the work of the profoundest
thinkers, is indeed one of the most curious features of modern
thought [italics supplied]."
In conclusion: Izhar is based on an actual case. In use of OT
materials I have sometimes followed the Protestant, sometimes
the Hebrew, Bible, but occasionally have taken the translation
of a scholar, which agrees with neither.UNIVERSITY OF MICHIGAN
